\begin{exercise}
What is the order of growth of the run time of a 
deterministic Finite State machine?
\begin{answer}\verified
Linear.
Finite State machines consume one character at each step.
So they run in time equal to the length of the input.
In the notation of \cref{def:TMTime},
$t_{\mathcal{M}}(\sigma)=\lh{\sigma}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
% \begin{exercise} \label{exer:LogSmallerThanPower}
% Do the converse half of \cref{ex:CompareLogToPower}:
% fix a logarithmic base $b>0$ and
% show that for any exponent $d>0$ the power function
% $x^d$ is not~$\bigOh(log_b(x))$
% \end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{exparts}
\item Verify that the function $f(x)=7$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
\item Verify that $f(x)=7+\sin(x)$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
  So if a function is in $\bigOh(1)$, that does not mean that it is 
  a constant function. 
\item Verify that $f(x)=7+(1/x)$ is also $\bigOh(1)$.
\item
  Show that a complexity function~$f$ is $\bigOh(1)$ if and
  only if it is bounded above by a constant, that is, 
  if an only if there exists
  $L\in\R$ so that $f(x)\leq L$ for all inputs $x\in\R$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
Let $g$ be the function $g(x)=1$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Directly apply the definition of Big~$\bigOh$, \cref{def:BigO},
by taking $N=1$ and $C=8$.
If $x>N=1$ then $C\cdot g(x)=8\cdot 1\geq f(x)=7$.
\item 
Recall that $\sin(x)$ is bounded between $-1$ and~$1$.
Take $N=1$ and $C=9$.
If $x>N$ then $C\cdot g(x)=9\geq 7+\sin(x)=f(x)$.
\item Fix $N=1$ and~$C=8$.
If $x\geq N=1$ then $C\cdot g(x)=2$ is greater than $f(x)=7+(1/x)$.
\item We first show that if it is bounded then it is~$\bigOh(1)$.
Suppose that~$f$ is bounded by~$K\in\R^+$.
Take $N=1$ and~$C=K$.
If $x\geq N=1$ then $C\cdot g(x)=K\geq f(x)$,
so $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$.

For the converse, suppose that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$.
Then there exists constants $N,C\in\R^+$ so that $x\geq N$ implies that
$C\cdot g(x)=C\geq f(x)$.
Let $K=\max(C,f(0), \ldots f(N-1))$.
If $x\in\R$ then $f(x)\leq K$, so $f$ is bounded by~$K$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Where does $g(x)\leq x^{\bigOh(1)}$ place the function
$g$ in the Hardy hierarchy?
\hint~see the prior question.
\begin{answer}\verified
A function $\map{f}{\R}{\R}$ is $\bigOh(1)$ if it is bounded by
a constant.
So $g(x)\leq x^{\bigOh(1)}$ says that the function~$g$ 
has at most a polynomial growth rate.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Let $f(x)=2x$ and $g(x)=x^2$.
  Prove directly from \cref{def:BigO} that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$, but that
  $g$ is not~$\bigOh(f)$.
  \begin{answer}\verified
    The first half, that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$, is straightforward.
    Fix $N=1$ and $C=2$.
    If $x\geq N=1$ then $C\cdot g(x)=2x^2\geq 2x=f(x)$
    (because $2x^2-2x=0$ gives $2\cdot(x(x-1))=0$ and so the 
    largest $x$ for which they are equal is $x=1$).

    For the second half, that $g$ is not~$\bigOh(f)$,
    we will show that for every $C$ there is
    no $N$ that will work.
    So fix a~$C$, aiming to prove 
    that for any~$N$ it is not the case that
    $x\geq N$ implies that $C\cdot f(x)\geq g(x)$.
    For any~$N$, consider $x=\max(N, C+1)$,
    which is greater than or equal to~$N$.
    Because $x>C$, we have that $g(x)=x^2>C\cdot x=C\cdot f(x)$.
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
  Prove that $2^n$ is~$\bigOh(n!)$.
  \hint~because of the factorial, consider these natural number functions
  and find suitable $N,C\in\N$.
  \begin{answer}\verified
    Let $N=4$ and~$C=1$.
    If $n\geq N=4$ then 
    $C\cdot n! =n! 
         =4!\cdot 5\cdot 6\cdots (n-1)\cdot n 
         \geq 2^4\cdot 2^{n-4}=2^n$
    (note that $4!=24 > 2^4=16$).
  \end{answer}
\end{exercise}
    
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:PowersOfLogSmallerThanAnyPoly}
Use L'H\^{o}pital's Rule as in
\cref{ex:CompareLogToPower} to verify these for any~$d\in\R^+\!$:
\begin{exparts*}
% \item $(\log_b(x))^2$ is $\bigOh(x^d)$ 
\item $(\log_b(x))^3$ is $\bigOh(x^d)$ 
\item for any~$k\in\N^+$, $(\log_b(x))^k$ is $\bigOh(x^d)$. 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This limit equals~$0$
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^2}{x^d}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2\log_b(x)\cdot\frac{1}{x\ln(b)}}{dx^{d-1}}
  =\frac{2}{d\ln(b)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\log_b(x)}{x^{d}}
\end{equation*}
by the calculation in \cref{ex:CompareLogToPower}.
\item This limit equals~$0$
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^3}{x^d}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{3\big(\log_b(x)\big)^2\cdot\frac{1}{x\ln(b)}}{dx^{d-1}}
  =\frac{3}{d\ln(b)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^2}{x^{d}}
\end{equation*}
by the calculation in the prior item.
\item The prior item suggests an argument by induction.
So
assume that the statement holds for the powers $n=1$, $n=2$, \ldots $n=k$
and consider the $n=k+1$ case.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^{k+1}}{x^d}
  =\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(k+1)\big(\log_b(x)\big)^k\cdot\frac{1}{x\ln(b)}}{dx^{d-1}}
  =\frac{k+1}{d\ln(b)}\cdot\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\big(\log_b(x)\big)^k}{x^{d}}
\end{equation*}
Finish by applying the $n=k$~inductive hypothesis.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
% \begin{exercise}
%   Name three functions that are $\bigOh(Z)$ where $\map{Z}{\N}{\N}$ is the
%   zero function.
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What is the running time of the empty Turing machine?
\begin{answer}\verified
The empty Turing machine ignores its input and halts immediately.
So $\hat{t}_M(n)=0$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
  Assume that $\map{g}{\R}{\R}$ is increasing,
  so that $x_1\geq x_0$ implies that $g(x_1)\geq g(x_0)$.
  Assume also that $\map{f}{\R}{\R}$ is a constant function.
  Show that $f$ is $\bigOh(g)$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Fix $N=1$ and find $C\in\R$ large enough that $C\cdot g(1)\geq f(1)$.
Because $g$ is increasing, if $x>N=1$ then 
$C\cdot g(x)\geq C\cdot g(1)\geq f(1)=f(x)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
% What is the connection between computable functions and 
% feasibly computable functions?
\begin{exparts*}
\item Show that there is a computable function whose output values
 grow at a rate that is $\bigOh(1)$, one whose values grow at
 a rate that is $\bigOh(n)$, one for $\bigOh(n^2)$, etc.
\item The \HP{} function $K$ is uncomputable.
  Place its rate of growth in the Hardy hierarchy, \cref{table:HardyHierarchy}.
\item Produce a function that is not computable because its output 
 values are larger than those of any computable function.
 (You need not show that the rate of growth is strictly larger, only
 that the output values are larger.)
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The function $f_0(x)=0$ is clearly computable and has
  output values that grow at a rate that is $\bigOh(1)$.
  Similarly, the output values of $f_1(x)=x$ grow
  at a rate that is $\bigOh()$,
  and $f_2(x)=x^2$ is $\bigOh(x^2)$, etc.
\item The function
  \begin{equation*}
    K(x)=
    \begin{cases}
      0  &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\diverges$}  \\
      1  &\case{if $\TMfcn_x(x)\converges$}  
    \end{cases}
  \end{equation*}
  is clearly $\bigOh(1)$.
\item Let $M(n)$ be the maximum value of $\TMfcn_i(j)$ where $0\leq i,j\leq n$
  and $\TMfcn_i(j)\converges$ (if there is no such maximum because none of 
  the $\TMfcn_i(j)$ converge then define $M(n)=0$).
  This is not a computable function, but it is a function.
  Then the function $\hat{M}(n)=M(n)+1$ has the property that for any
  computable function $\TMfcn_e$, 
  for all $n>e$ if $\TMfcn_e(n)\converges$ then $\hat{M}(n)>\TMfcn_e(n)$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Stack Exchange user templatetypedef
  \url{https://stackoverflow.com/a/19647659/7168267}
\end{credit}
An algorithm that inputs natural numbers runs in 
pseudopolynomial time\index{pseudopolynomial}
if its runtime is polynomial in the numeric value of the input~$n$, 
but exponential in the number of bits required to represent~$n$.   
Show that the naive algorithm to test if the input is prime,
which just checks whether it is divisible by any number
that is smaller and greater than~$2$, 
is pseudopolynomial.
(\hint~we can check whether one number divides another in quadratic time.) 
\begin{answer}\verified
Consider the number~$n\in\N$.
The hint says that we can check whether a number divides~$n$ in
quadratic time.
Wrapping a loop around that to do all numbers~$m$ with $2<m<n$ adds
one to the exponent.
So the naive algorithm is~$\bigOh(n^3)$ in the value of~$n$.

However, in terms of bits,
the number~$n$ is represented by about $\lg(n)$-many bits.
That is, this expresses the runtime as a function of 
the size of the input.
\begin{equation*}
   \text{runtime}=n^3=(2^{\text{size}})^3=2^{3\cdot\text{size}}
\end{equation*}
So the relationship between the size of the input
and the runtime is exponential.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
  Show that $\bigOh(2^x)\in\bigOh(3^x)$
  but $\bigOh(2^x)\neq\bigOh(3^x)$.
\begin{answer}\verified
Use \cref{lem:LHBigO}.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2^x}{3^x}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\bigg(\frac{2}{3}\bigg)^x
  =0
\end{equation*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}  

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Stack Exchange user Daniel Fischer,
  \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/674039},
  and Stack Exchange user anon, 
  \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/61741}
\end{credit}
\Cref{table:HardyHierarchy} states that $n!$ grows slower
than~$n^n$.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Verify this. \hint~although $n!$ is a natural number function,
  \cref{lem:LHBigO} still applies.
\item Stirling's formula is that $n!\approx \sqrt{2\pi n}\cdot (n^n/e^n)$.
  Doesn't this imply that $n!$ is $\bigTheta(n^n)$? 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item With an eye toward \cref{lem:LHBigO}, consider the ratio.
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{n!}{n^n}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2}{n}\cdots\frac{n}{n}
                \leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot 1\cdots 1
\end{equation*}
Clearly the limit of the ratio $n!/n^n$ is~$0$.
\item 
No, don't overlook the~$e^{-n}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Two complexity functions $f,g$ are 
asymptotically equivalent,\index{asymptotically equivalent}\index{similar@$\sim$, asymptotically equivalent} 
$f\sim g$, if $\lim_{x\to\infty}(f(x)/g(x))=1$.
Show that each pair is asymptotically equivalent:
\begin{exparts*}
\item $f(x)=x^2+5x+1$ and~$g(x)=x^2$,
\item $\lg(x+1)$ and~$\lg(x)$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item Apply L'H\^{o}pital's Rule twice.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^2+5x+1}{x^2}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2x+5}{2x}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2}{2}=1
\end{equation*}
\item Apply L'H\^{o}pital's Rule, remembering to use the Chain Rule 
for the numerator.
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\lg(x+1)}{\lg(x)}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{(1/x\ln(2))\cdot 1}{1/x\ln(2)}
  =1
\end{equation*}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise}
Is there an~$f$ so that $\bigOh(f)$ is the set of all polynomials?
\begin{answer}\verified
No.
If $\bigOh(f)$ is the set of polynomials then~$f$ must be a polynomial.
However, then~$f$ has some degree, and 
no polynomial of higher degree is a member of~$\bigOh(f)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:OrdersBetweenPolyAndExp}
There are orders of growth between polynomial and exponential.
Specifically, $f(x)=x^{\lg x}$ is one.
\begin{exparts}
\item Show that $\lg(x)\in\bigOh((\lg(x))^2)$ but
  $(\lg(x))^2\notin\bigOh(\lg(x))$.
\item Argue that for any power~$k$, we have $x^k\in\bigOh(x^{\lg x})$
  but $x^{\lg(x)}\notin\bigOh(x^k)$.
  \hint~take the ratio, rewrite using $a=2^{\lg(a)}$,
  and consider the limit of the exponent.
\item Show that $x^{\lg x}=2^{(\lg x)^2}$.
 \hint~take the logarithm of both halves.
\item Show that 
  $x^{\lg x}$ is in $\bigOh(2^x)$.
  \hint~form the ratio using the prior item.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
Recall the logarithm rules that $\log_b(x^a)=a\cdot\log_b(x)$ 
and $b^{\log_b(x)}=x$.
\begin{exparts}
\item  L'H\^{o}pital's Rule gives
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\lg(x)}{(\lg(x))^2}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1/x\ln(2)}{2\lg(x)\cdot 1/x\ln(2)}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{2\lg(x)}
  =0
\end{equation*}
and so \cref{lem:LHBigO} gives the required conclusion.
\item By \cref{lem:LHBigO}, it suffices to show that this is~$0$. 
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^k}{x^{\lg(x)}}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty} x^{k-\lg(x)}
\end{equation*}
Rewrite. 
\begin{equation*}
  x^{k-\lg(x)}
  =2^{\lg(\,x^{k-\lg(x)}\,)}
  =2^{(k-\lg(x))\cdot \lg(x)}
  =2^{k\lg(x)-(\lg(x))^2}
\end{equation*}
Apply the prior item to conclude that the limit of the exponent is~$-\infty$,
and so the limit of the entire expression is~$0$.
\item Taking $\lg$ of $x^{\lg(x)}$ gives 
$\lg(x^{\lg x})=\lg(x)\cdot\lg(x)$.
Taking $\lg$ of the other expression gives $\lg(2^{(\lg x)^2})=(\lg(x))^2\cdot \lg(2)$.
Since the logarithm and exponential are inverse functions,
that is $(\lg x)^2$.
\item Rewrite $x^{\lg(x)}$ as $2^{(\lg(x))^2}$.
\cref{lem:LHBigO}
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{2^{(\lg(x))^2}}{2^x}
  =
  \lim_{x\to\infty} 2^{(\lg(x))^2-x}
  =
  0
\end{equation*}
gives the desired conclusion.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
  
% ......................
\item[{\it The remaining exercises verify results that were
  presented without proof.}]

\begin{exercise} 
  Verify the clauses of \cref{lem:BigOAlgProps}.
  \begin{exparts*}
    \item
      If $a\in\R^+$ then
      $a f$ is also~$\bigOh(g)$.
    \item
      The function $f_0+f_1$ is~$\bigOh(g)$, where $g$ is defined by
      $g(n)=\max(g_0(n),g_1(n))$.
    \item
      The product $f_0f_1$ is~$\bigOh(g_0g_1)$.
      % (The product of two functions $h_0,h_1$ is defined by
      % $h_0h_1(n)=h_0(n)\cdot h_1(n)$.)
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
We will use the notation from the lemma statement.
\begin{exparts}
\item The assumption is that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$.
So there are constants $N,C\in\R^+$ such that 
$x\geq N$ implies that $C\cdot g(x)\geq f(x)$.
For the function~$a\cdot f$, use the constants
$\hat{N}=N$ and $\hat{C}=a\cdot C$.
Then $x\geq\hat{C}$ implies that $\hat{C}\cdot g(x)\geq f(x)$.
\item The function $f_0$ is $\bigOh(g_0)$ 
and the function $f_1$ is~$\bigOh(g_1)$.
So there are constants $N_0,C_0,N_1,C_1\in\R^+$ such that
$x\geq N_0$ implies that $C_0g_0(x)\geq f_0(x)$, and 
$x\geq N_1$ implies that $C_1g_1(x)\geq f_1(x)$.
Take $\hat{N}=\max(N_0,N_1)$ and $\hat{C}=\max(C_0,C_1)$.
Then $x\geq \hat{N}$ implies that 
$\hat{C}\cdot (g_0(x)+g_1(x))=\hat{C}g_0(x)+\hat{C}g_1(x)\geq f_0(x)+f_1(x)$.
\item As in the prior item, 
there are constants $N_0,C_0,N_1,C_1\in\R^+$ such that
$x\geq N_0$ implies that $C_0(x)\geq f_0(x)$, and 
$x\geq N_1$ implies that $C_1(x)\geq f_1(x)$.
Take  $\hat{N}=\max(N_0,N_1)$ and $\hat{C}=C_0\cdot C_1$.
Then $x\geq \hat{N}$ implies that 
$\hat{C}\cdot (g_0(x)\cdot g_1(x))
  =C_0g_0(x)\cdot C_1g_1(x)\geq f_0(x)\cdot f_1(x)$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
  Verify these clauses of \cref{lem:BigThetaEquivalence}.
  \begin{exparts*}
  \item The big-$\bigOh$ relation is reflexive.
  \item It is also transitive.
  \end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item We must show that $f$ in~$\bigOh(f)$.
Take $N=1$ and $C=1$.
\item Suppose that $f_0$ is~$\bigOh(f_1)$ 
and that $f_1$ is~$\bigOh(f_2)$.
Then there exists $N_0,N_1,C_0,C_1\in\R^+$ such that 
if $x\geq N_0$ then $C_0\cdot f_1(x)\geq f_0(x)$, 
and 
if $x\geq N_1$ then $C_1\cdot f_2(x)\geq f_1(x)$.
Take $\hat{N}=\max(N_0,N_1)$ and $\hat{C}=C_0\cdot C_1$.
Then $x\geq \hat{N}$ implies that 
$\hat{C}\cdot f_2(x)\geq C_0\cdot f_1(x) \geq f_0(x)$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/925053/using-limits-to-determine-big-o-big-omega-and-big-theta
% (given an example where the limit doesn't exist?)
% http://web.mit.edu/broder/Public/asymptotics-cheatsheet.pdf
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Stack Exchange user Ilmari Karonen,
  \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/925053/using-limits-to-determine-big-o-big-omega-and-big-theta}
\end{credit}
  \Cref{lem:LHBigO} says that if the limit of the ratio of two functions
  exists then 
  we can determine the $\bigOh$ relationship between the two.
  Assume that $f$ and~$g$ are complexity functions.
  \begin{exparts} 
    \item Suppose that 
      $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$ exists and equals~$0$.
      Show that $f$ is $\bigOh(g)$.
      (\hint~this requires a rigorous definition of the limit.)
    \item We can give an example where $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$ 
       even though $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$
       does not exist.
       Verify that, where $g(x)=x$ and where
       $f(x)=x$ when $\floor{x}$ is odd and
       $f(x)=2x$ when $\floor{x}$ is even.
  \end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item If $\lim_{x\to\infty}f(x)/g(x)$ exists and equals~$0$ then 
  for any $\varepsilon>0$ there is an~$N\in\R$ such that $x\geq N$ implies that 
  $f(x)/g(x)<\varepsilon$.
  That gives $f(x)<\varepsilon\cdot g(x)$.
  Fix $\varepsilon=1/2$ and $C=2$. 
  Take inverses to get that $x\geq N$ implies 
  $C\cdot g(x)=2g(x)\geq f(x)$. 
\item To show that $f$ is~$\bigOh(g)$, take $N=1$ and~$C=4$.

  On the other hand, 
  the limit doesn't exist, as the ratio oscillates between $1$
  and~$2$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:PolySlowerThanExp}
  Prove \cref{lem:PolySlowerThanExp}.
\begin{answer}\verified
First use L'H\^{o}pital's Rule to get that 
if $g(x)=a_mx^m+\cdots+a_0$
then $g$ is~$\bigTheta(x^m)$.
Then \cref{ex:CompareLogToPower} shows that any logarithmic function
is $\bigOh(g)$.

For the other half, fix a base~$b>1$ and consider the exponential function
$h(x)=b^x$.
L'H\^{o}pital's Rule gives this
\begin{equation*}
  \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^m}{b^x}
  =
  \frac{m}{\ln(b)}\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{m-1}}{b^x}
  =
  \frac{m(m-1)}{(\ln(b))^2}\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{x^{m-2}}{b^x}
  =\cdots =
  \frac{m!}{(\ln(b))^m}\cdot \lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{1}{b^x}
  =0
\end{equation*}
and \cref{lem:LHBigO} gives the desired conclusion.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{big O@big $\bigOh$|)}






% =============================================
\section{A problem miscellany}\index{problem miscellany|(}
Much of today's work in the Theory of Computation is driven by problems
that come from a field outside the subject.
We will describe some problems to get a sense of the ones that 
appear in the subject, and also to use for examples and exercises.
These are all well known.









\subsection{Problems with stories}
We start with a few problems that come with stories.
Besides being fun, and an important part of the 
field's culture, these stories
also give a sense of where problems come from.

\margingraphic[l]{0.18\textwidth}{complexity/pix/hamilton.png}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rowan_Hamilton}{William Rowan Hamilton 1805--1865}}\index{Hamilton, W R!picture} % By William_Rowan_Hamilton_portrait_oval.png: Unknown William_Rowan_Hamilton_portrait_oval_2.png: Unknown derivative work: Quibik [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
W~R Hamilton was a polymath whose genius was recognized early and he was 
given a sinecure as Astronomer Royal of Ireland.  
He made important contributions to classical mechanics, where his 
reformulation of Newtonian mechanics is now called Hamiltonian mechanics. 
Other work of his in physics helped develop classical field theories such as 
electromagnetism and laid the groud work for
the development of quantum mechanics.  
In mathematics, he is best known as the
\citetext{inventor of the quaternion number system}{See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_quaternions}.}.

One of his ventures was a game, \citetext{\textit{Around the World}}{% 
  Another version was
  called \textit{The Icosian Game}.
  See \url{http://puzzlemuseum.com/month/picm02/200207icosian.htm}.}.
The vertices in the graph below 
were holes labeled with the names of world cities.
Players put pegs in the holes, looking for a circuit that visits each 
city once and only once. 
\begin{animatedfigure}{Hamilton's \textit{Around the World} game}\centering
  % \includegraphics[height=0.75in]{complexity/icosehedron.png}
  % \hspace{2em}
  \animategraphics[poster=first]{2}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{13}{14}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\noindent It did not make Hamilton rich.
But it did get him associated with a great problem.

%<*problem:HamiltonianProblem>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}]\index{Hamiltonian Circuit@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}
Given a graph, decide if it contains a \definend{Hamiltonian circuit},
a cyclic path that includes each vertex once and only once.
\end{problem}
%</problem:HamiltonianProblem>

This is stated as a type of problem called a decision problem, 
because it asks for a `yes' or `no' answer.
In this section we will see a
a variety of types of problems.
The next section will say more about problem types. 

%<*discussion:KnightsTouri>
A special case of the \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem
is the \definend{\probname{Knight's Tour}} problem,\index{Knight's Tour@\probname{Knight's Tour} problem}
to use a chess knight to make a circuit of the squares on the board.
(Recall that a knight moves three squares at a time,
two in one direction and then one perpendicular to that direction.)
%</discussion:KnightsTouri>
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1in]{complexity/pix/knight.jpg}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.2in]{complexity/pix/knightstour.png}}
\end{center}
%<*discussion:KnightsTourii>
\citetext{This is the solution given by L~Euler}{%
  The figure is from \autocite{EulerKnightsTour}.}.
In graph terms, there are sixty four vertices,
representing the board squares.
An edge goes between two vertices if they are connected by a single knight move.
\probname{Knight's Tour} asks for a Hamiltonian circuit 
of that graph.
%</discussion:KnightsTourii>

\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} has another famous variant.

%<*problem:TravellingSalesman>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Travelling Salesman}]\index{Travelling Salesman problem@\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem}  \label{ex:TravellingSalesman}
Given a weighted graph, where we call the vertices $S=\set{c_0,\ldots c_{k-1}}$
`cities' and we call the edge weight  $d(c_i,c_j)\in\N^+$ for all
$c_i\neq c_j$ (subject to $d(c_i,c_2)=d(c_j,c_i)$) 
the `distance' between the cities, 
\citetext{find the shortest-distance circuit that visits every city}{%
  \probname{Travelling Salesman} was first posed by K~Menger,
  in an article that appeared in the same journal and the same issue as
  G\"odel's Incompleteness Theorem.}
and returns back to the start.
\end{problem}
%</problem:TravellingSalesman>

\margingraphic[l]{0.375\textwidth}{complexity/pix/xkcdtsp.png}{\label{fig:xkcdTravellingSalesman}\href{https://xkcd.com/399/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
We can start with a map of the  
state capitals of the forty eight contiguous US~states
and the distances between them: Montpelier~VT to Albany~NY is $254$
kilometers, etc.
From among all trips that visit each city and return back to the start,
such as 
$\text{Montpelier}\rightarrow\text{Albany}\rightarrow\text{Harrisburg}\rightarrow \cdots \rightarrow\text{Montpelier}$, we want the shortest one.

As stated, this is an optimization problem.
However, we can recast it as a decision 
problem.
Introduce a bound $B\in\N$ and change the problem statement to
`decide if
there is a circuit of total distance less than~$B$'.
If we had an algorithm to quickly solve this decision problem
then we could also quickly solve the optimization problem:~ask 
whether there is a trip bounded by length $B=1$, then ask if there is a 
trip of length $B=2$, etc.
When we eventually get a `yes', we know the length of the shortest trip.

\margingraphic{0.18\textwidth}{complexity/pix/euler.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler}{Leonhard Euler 1707--1783}}\index{Euler, L!picture} % Jakob Emanuel Handmann [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3ALeonhard_Euler.jpg
The next problem sounds much like \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}, in that it
involves exhaustively
traversing a graph.
But it proves to act very differently.

Today the city of Kaliningrad is in a Russian exclave between
Poland and Lithuania.
But in 1727 it was in Prussia and was called 
K\"onigsberg.\index{Konigsberg@K\"onigsberg}
The Pregel river divides the city into four areas,
connected by seven bridges.
The citizens used to promenade,
to take leisurely walks or drives where they could see and be seen.
Among these citizens the question arose:~can 
a person cross each bridge
once and only once,
and arrive back at the start?
No one could think of a way but no one could think of a reason that there was
no way.
A local mayor wrote to Euler, who proved that
\citetext{no circuit is possible}{%
  For each land mass, for each bridge in
  there must be an associated bridge out.
  So an at least necessary condition is that the land masses have an even
  number of associated edges.}.
This paper founded Graph Theory.
\begin{center}  \setlength{\fboxsep}{0pt} \setlength{\fboxrule}{0.8pt}
  % By Merian-Erben [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons
  % https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AImage-Koenigsberg%2C_Map_by_Merian-Erben_1652.jpg
  % \autocite{EulerBridges}
  \vcenteredhbox{\fcolorbox{boldcolor}{white}{\includegraphics[height=1.35in]{complexity/pix/koenigsberg.jpg}}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=0.6in]{complexity/pix/eulerbridges.jpg}}
  \hspace{2em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems15.pdf}}
\end{center}
Euler's summary sketch is in the middle and the graph is on the right.

%<*prob:EulerCircuit>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Euler Circuit}]\index{Euler Circuit@\probname{Euler Circuit} problem} \label{prob:EulerCircuit}
Given a graph, find a circuit that traverses each edge once
and only once, or declare that no such circuit exists.
\end{problem}
%</prob:EulerCircuit>

Next is a problem that sounds hard.
But all of us see it solved every day, for instance when we 
ask a phone for the
shortest driving directions to someplace.

%<*prob:ShortestPath>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Shortest Path}]\index{Shortest Path@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} \label{prob:ShortestPath}
Given a weighted graph and two vertices, 
find the shortest path between them, or report that no path exists.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ShortestPath>

There is an algorithm that solves this problem quickly.\footnote{%
  Dijkstra's algorithm is at worst quadratic in the number of vertices.}
For instance, with this graph we could look for 
the cheapest path from $A$ to~$F$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs08.pdf}
\end{center} 

The next problem was discovered in 1852 by a young mathematician, F~Guthrie,
who was drawing a map of the counties of England.
He wanted to color the counties,
and naturally wanted to different colors for counties that share a border. 
His map required only four colors and he
conjectured that for any map at all, four colors were enough.

\margingraphic[l]{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/demorgan.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus_De_Morgan}{Augustus De Morgan 1806--1871}}\index{De Morgan, A!picture} % By Sophia Elizabeth De Morgan (Memoir of Augustus De Morgan) [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/2c/De_Morgan_Augustus.jpg
Guthrie imposed the condition that 
\citetext{the countries must be contiguous}{%
  A notable example of a non-contiguous country in the wold today
  is that Russia is separated from Kaliningrad, the city that 
  used to be known as K\'{o}nigsberg.},
and he defined `sharing a border' to mean sharing an interval,
not just a point (see \cref{exer:FiveColors}).
Below is a map and a graph version of the same problem.
In the graph, counties are vertices and edges connect ones that are adjacent.
A crucial point is that the graph is 
\definend{planar}\Dash\index{graph!planar}\index{planar graph}
\citetext{we can draw it in the plane}{%
  This is because the graph comes from a planar map.} 
so that its edges do not cross.

The \probname{Four Color}\index{Four Color@\probname{Four Color} problem} 
problem \citetext{inputs a planar graph}{%
  The graph is undirected and without loops.} 
and outputs
the vertices partitioned into no more than four sets, called 
\definend{colors},\index{colors}\index{graph!colors}
such that adjacent vertices are in different colors.
(It is a special case of the  problem given next.)
\begin{animatedfigure}{\protect\citetext{Counties of England and the derived planar graph}{%
    This is today's map.
    At the time, some counties were not contiguous.}}\centering
  \vcenteredhbox{\animategraphics[height=1.65in,poster=last]{2}{languages/counties}{00}{01}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=1.65in]{complexity/asy/problems/problems16.pdf}}
\end{animatedfigure} 

\margingraphic[l]{0.27\textwidth}{complexity/pix/fourcolorssuffice.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_color_theorem}{Appel and Haken's post office celebrating}} % from http://mathyear2013.blogspot.com/2013/04/dr-kenneth-appel-and-four-color-map.html
% \margingraphic[l]{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/fourcolorssuffice.jpg}{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_color_theorem}{Postmark celebrating the solution}} % from http://mathyear2013.blogspot.com/2013/04/dr-kenneth-appel-and-four-color-map.html
Guthrie consulted his former professor, A~De~Morgan,
who was also unable to either prove or disprove the conjecture.
But he did make the problem famous by promoting it among his
friends.
It remained unsolved until 1976, when
K~Appel and W~Haken reduced the proof to 1\,936 cases and got a
computer to check those cases.

This was the first major proof that was done on a computer and 
\citetext{it was controversial}{
  See \url{https://www.maa.org/sites/default/files/pdf/upload_library/22/Ford/Swart697-707.pdf}.}.
Many mathematicians felt that the purpose of the subject was to 
understand the things studied, and not just be satisfied when a 
computer program that perhaps seems to be bug-free
assures us that theorems are verified.\footnote{%
  This is in contrast to the goal of the \textit{Entscheidungsproblem}.}
In the interim, though, a new generation has appeared that is more 
comfortable with the process and  
now computer proofs are routine, or at least not as controversial.

%<*prob:GraphColorability>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Graph Colorability}]\index{Graph Colorability@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem} \label{prob:GraphColorability}
Given a graph and a number $k\in\N$, decide whether the 
graph is \definend{$k$-colorable}, 
whether we can partition its vertices into $k$-many sets, 
$\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{C}_0\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{C}_{k-1}$, such
that no two same-set vertices are connected.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[\probname{Chromatic Number}]\index{Chromatic Number@\probname{Chromatic Number} problem} \label{prob:ChromaticNumber}
Given a graph, find the smallest number $k\in\N$ such that the 
graph is \definend{$k$-colorable}.
\end{problem}
%</prob:GraphColorability>

\margingraphic{0.14\textwidth}{complexity/pix/boole.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Boole}{George Boole 1815--1864}}\index{Boole, G!picture} % See page for author [Public domain], via Wikimedia Commons; https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File%3AGeorge_Boole_color.jpg
Our final story introduces a problem that  
will serve as a benchmark to which we compare others.
In 1847, G~Boole outlined what we today call Boolean algebra in
\citetext{\textit{An Investigation of the Laws of Thought on Which are Founded the Mathematical Theories of Logic and Probabilities}}{%
  See \protect\url{https://www.3quarksdaily.com/3quarksdaily/2018/02/george-boole-and-the-calculus-of-thought-5.html}}.
It provided a systematic mathematical treatment
of simple logical relations.

A variable is \definend{Boolean}\index{boolean}\index{boolean!variable} 
if the values that it takes on are
$T$ or~$F$.
A \definend{Boolean function}\index{boolean!function}\index{function!boolean} 
inputs and outputs tuples of those. 
\definend{Boolean expressions}\index{boolean!expression} 
connect variables using the
binary \definend{and operator,~$\wedge$},
the binary \definend{or operator,~$\vee$},
and the unary \definend{not operator,~$\neg$}.
Here is a Boolean function given by 
an expression with three variables.
\begin{equation*}
  f(P,Q,R)=
  (P\vee Q)\wedge (P\vee \neg Q)\wedge (\neg P\vee Q)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R) % \wedge (\neg P\vee R)
\end{equation*}
We will assume that expressions are in 
\citetext{\definend{conjunctive normal form}}{%
  Any Boolean function can be expressed in that form.},\index{conjunctive normal form}\index{CNF, Conjunctive Normal Form}
so they consist of clauses of $\vee$'s connected with $\wedge$'s.
A Boolean formula is \definend{satisfiable}\index{satisfiable} 
if there is some combination
of input $T$'s and $F$'s so that the formula evaluates to~$T$.
This \definend{truth table}\index{truth table} 
shows the input-output behavior of the
function defined by that formula.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\vee Q$ &$P\vee \neg Q$ &$\neg P\vee Q$ &$\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R$ &$f(P,Q,R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
That $T$ in the final column witnesses that this formula is satisfiable.

%<*prob:SAT>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Satisfiability}, $\SAT$]\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}\index{SAT@$\SAT$|see {Satisfiability}}  \label{prob:SAT}
Given a propositional logic formula,
decide if it is satisfiable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:SAT>

%<*prob:ThreeSAT>
\begin{problem}[\probname{$3$-Satisfiability}, $3-\SAT$]\index{3 Satisfiability@\probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem}\index{3 SAT@$3$-$\SAT$}\label{prob:3SAT}
Given a propositional logic formula in conjunctive normal form
in which each clause has at most three variables,
decide if it is satisfiable.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ThreeSAT>

Observe that if 
the number of input variables is~$v$ then the number of rows in the truth table
is $2^v$. 
So solving $\SAT$ appears to require
exponential time.
Whether that is right is a very important question, 
as we will see in later sections.







% =========================================
\subsection{More problems}
We will list more problems and examples, albeit without stories. 

%<*prob:reachability>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}]\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path@\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}\label{prob:vertextovertexpath}
Given a graph and two vertices,
find if the second is reachable\index{reachable vertex}\index{vertex!reachable} 
from the first,
that is, if there is a path to the second from the first.\footnotemark[1]
\end{problem}\footnotetext[1]{There are lots of problems about paths, so 
  calling this just the \probname{Path} problem is confusing.
  Some authors call this 
  \probname{$st$-Path},\protect\index{st Path@$st$-Path problem|see {Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem}} 
  \probname{$st$-Connectivity},\protect\index{st Connectivity@$st$-Connectivity problem|see {Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem}}
  or \probname{STCON}.\protect\index{stcon@STCON problem|see {Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem}}}
%</prob:reachability>

\begin{example}
These are two Western-tradition constellations, Ursa Minor
and Draco.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems26.pdf}
\end{center}
Here we can solve the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem by eye.
For any two vertices in Ursa Minor there is a path and
for any two vertices in Draco there is a path.
But if the two are in different constellations 
then there is no path. 
\end{example}

%<*prob:minspanningtree>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Minimum Spanning Tree}]\index{Minimum Spanning Tree@\probname{Minimum Spanning Tree} problem} \label{prob:minspanningtree}
Given a weighted undirected graph,
find a \definend{minimum spanning tree}, a subgraph containing
all the vertices of the original graph such that its edges have a mininimum
total.
\end{problem}
%</prob:minspanningtree>

This is an undirected graph with weights on the edges.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity21.pdf}
\end{center}
The highlighted subgraph
includes all of
the vertices, that is, it 
\definend{spans}\index{span a graph}\index{spanning subgraph}\index{graph!spanning subgraph} 
the graph.
In addition, its weights total to a minimum from among all
of the spanning subgraphs.
From that it follows that this subgraph is a 
\definend{tree},\index{tree}\index{graph!tree} 
meaning that it has no cycles, or else we could eliminate
an edge from the cycle and thereby lower the edge weight total without
dropping any vertices.

This % e \probname{Mimimum Spanning Tree}\index{Mimimum Spanning Tree@\probname{Mimimum Spanning Tree} problem} 
problem looks like the \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem
in requiring that the subgraph contain all the vertices.
One difference is that for the \probname{Mimimum Spanning Tree} 
problem we know algorithms that are quick, that are $\bigOh(n\lg n)$.

%<*prob:VertexCover>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Vertex Cover}]\index{Vertex Cover@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a 
size~$B$ \definend{vertex cover},\index{vertex cover}\index{graph!vertex cover}
a set of vertices,~$C$,
such that for any edge, at least one of its ends
is a member of~$C$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:VertexCover>

\begin{example}
A museum posts guards to watch the exhibits.
They have eight halls, laid out as below, and they will
post guards at corners.
What is the smallest number of guards that will suffice to watch
all of the hallways?
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs15.pdf}
\end{center}
Obviously, one guard will not do.
A two-element list that covers is $C=\set{w_0,w_4}$.
\end{example}

%<*prob:Clique>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Clique}]\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
\label{prob:Clique}
Given a graph and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if the graph has a 
size~$B$ \definend{clique},\index{clique, in a graph}\index{graph!clique}
a set of $B$-many vertices such that any two are connected.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Clique>

If the graph nodes represent people
and the edges connect friends then we want to know if there are
$B$-many mutual friends.
Any graph with a $4$-clique has the subgraph like the one below on the left
and any graph with a $5$~clique has the subgraph like the one the right.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity02.pdf}
  \hspace{3em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity12.pdf}
\end{center}

\begin{example}
Decide if this graph has a $4$-clique.
\begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Clique
  problem}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=first]{1}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{08}{09}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\end{example}

%<*prob:Broadcast>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Broadcast}]\index{Broadcast@\probname{Broadcast} problem}  \label{prob:broadcast}
Given a graph with initial vertex~$v_0$, and a bound~$B\in\N$,
decide if a message can spread from $v_0$ to every other vertex
within $B$~steps.
At each step, any node that has heard the message can transmit it to
at most one adjacent node.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Broadcast>

\begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Broadcast
  problem}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=first]{1}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{02}{06}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\begin{example} \label{ex:broadcast}
In the graph no vertex is more than three edges away from the initial one.
The animation shows it taking four steps to broadcast.
\end{example}

%<*prob:ThreeDMatching>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Three-dimensional Matching}]\index{Three-dimensional Matching@\probname{Three-dimensional Matching} problem}  
\label{Prob:ThreeDMatching}
Given as input a set $M\subseteq X\times Y\times Z$, where the sets $X,Y,Z$
all have the same number of elements, $n$,
decide if there is a \definend{matching},\index{matching, three dimensional}
a set $\hat{M}\subseteq M$ containing~$n$ elements such that no
two of the triples in $\hat{M}$ agree on any of their coordinates.
\end{problem}
%</prob:ThreeDMatching>

\begin{example}
Let $X=\set{\str{a},\str{b}}$, $Y=\set{\str{b},\str{c}}$,
and~$Z=\set{\str{a},\str{d}}$, so that $n=2$.
Then $M=X\times Y\times Z$ contains these eight elements. % $\sequence{x,y,z}$.
\begin{equation*}
  M=\set{
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}},
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}},
        \sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}},
        \sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}
        }
\end{equation*}
% \begin{center} \small
% \begin{tabular}{cc}
%   \multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{a}$}
%   &\multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{d}$}    \\[-0.5ex]
%   \begin{tabular}{r|cc}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{b}$}
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{c}$}  \\ \cline{2-3}
%     \rule{0pt}{9pt} $x=\str{a}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}}$  \\
%     $x=\str{b}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}}$  \\
%   \end{tabular}
%   &\begin{tabular}{r|cc}
%     \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{b}$}
%     &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$y=\str{c}$}  \\ \cline{2-3}
%     \rule{0pt}{9pt}  $x=\str{a}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}}$  \\
%     $x=\str{b}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}}$
%       &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$  \\
%   \end{tabular}
% \end{tabular}
% \end{center}
The set $\hat{M}=\set{\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}}$
has $2$ elements and they disagree in the first coordinate,
as well as on the second and third.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
\begin{credit}
  Sean T.\ McCulloch,  
  \url{https://npcomplete.owu.edu/2014/06/03/3-dimensional-matching/}
\end{credit}
Fix $n=4$ and consider $X = \set{1,2,3,4}$, $Y = \set{10,20,30,40}$, 
and $Z = \set{100,200,300,400}$, all four-element sets.
Also fix this subset of $X\times Y\times Z$.
\begin{align*}
  M &=\{\,
        \sequence{1,10,200}, 
        \sequence{1,20,300}, 
        \sequence{2,30,400}, 
        \sequence{3,10,400},   \\ 
        &\qquad\sequence{3,40,100}, 
        \sequence{3,40,200}, 
        \sequence{4,10,200},
        \sequence{4,20,300}
        \,\}
\end{align*}
A matching is 
$\hat{M}=\set{\sequence{1,20,300}, \sequence{2,30,400}, 
  \sequence{3,40,100}, \sequence{4,10,200}}$.
\end{example}

%<*prob:SubsetSum>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Subset Sum}]\index{Subset Sum@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}  \label{prob:SubsetSumDecision}
Given a multiset of natural
numbers $S=\set{n_0,\,\ldots\, n_{k-1}}$ and a target $T\in\N$,
decide if a subset of~$S$ sums to the target.
\end{problem}
%</prob:SubsetSum>

Recall that a multiset\index{multiset} 
is like a set in that the order of the elements
is not significant but is different than a set in that repeats do not
collapse:~the multiset $\set{1,2,2,3}$ is different than the 
multiset $\set{1,2,3}$.

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SubsetSum}
Decide if some of the numbers
$\set{911,22,821,563,405,986,165,732}$ add to $T=1173$.
One such collection is $\set{165, 986, 22}$.
% sage: [randint(1, 999) for i in range(8)]
% [911, 22, 821, 563, 405, 986, 165, 732]
% \begin{equation*}
% 911
% \quad 22
% \quad 821
% \quad 563
% \quad 405
% \quad 986
% \quad 165
% \quad 732
% \end{equation*}
\end{example}

\begin{example} 
No sum of the numbers
$\set{831,357,63,987,117,81,6785,606}$ adds to $T=2105$.
All of the numbers are multiples of three, while the target~$T$ is not.
% sage: [randint(1, 999) for i in range(8)]
% [835, 367, 63, 987, 117, 81, 678, 506]
\end{example}

%<*prob:Knapsack>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Knapsack}]\index{Knapsack@\probname{Knapsack} problem}
Given a finite set $S$ whose elements~$s$
have a weight~$w(s)\in\N^+$ and a value~$v(s)\in\N^+$, along with a
weight bound $B\in\N^+$ and a value target~$T\in\N^+$, find a subset
$\hat{S}\subseteq S$ whose elements have a total weight less than or equal
to the
bound and total value greater than or equal to the target.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Knapsack>

Imagine that we have items to pack in a knapsack and we can carry at most
ten pounds.
Can we pack a value of~$T=100$ or more?
\begin{center}\small 
\begin{tabular}{r|cccc}
  \tabulartext{Item}
    &\tabulartext{a} &\tabulartext{b} &\tabulartext{c} &\tabulartext{d}  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \rule{0pt}{9pt}  % scooch more room
  \tabulartext{Weight}  &$3$  &$4$  &$5$   &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{Value}   &$50$ &$40$ &$10$  &$30$ 
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
We pack the most value while keeping
to the weight limit by taking items (a) and~(b).
So we cannot meet the value target.

%<*prob:Partition>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Partition}]\index{Partition@\probname{Partition} problem}
Given a finite multiset~$A$ that has for each of its elements
an associated positive number size $s(a)\in\N^+$,
decide if there is a division of the set into two halves,
$\hat{A}$ and $A-\hat{A}$, so that the total of the sizes is the same in
both halves, $\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Partition>

\begin{example}
The set
$A=\set{\str{I},\str{a},\str{my},\str{go},\str{rivers},\str{cat},\str{hotel},\str{comb}}$
has eight words.
The size of a word, $s(\sigma)$, is the number of letters.
Then $\hat{A}=\set{\str{cat},\str{river},\str{I},\str{a},\str{go}}$
gives $\sum_{a\in\hat{A}}s(a)=\sum_{a\notin\hat{A}}s(a)=12$.
\end{example}

% Recall that if we have an exponential relation $b^n=a$ then we say that
% the exponent~$n$ is the logarithm, base~$b$, of~$a$, written $\log_b(a)=n$.
% For instance, $2^7=128$ so we say that $\log_{2}(128)=\lg(128)=7$.

% Discrete logarithms are the discrete analogue of those.
% Fix a prime~$p$ and consider the numbers $G=\set{0,1,\ldots\, p-1}$.
% We can multiply two of these and then take the answer module~$p$.


% \begin{problem}[Discrete Logarithm]
% Let $p\in\N$i be a prime.
% Given a base $b\in\set{0,1,\ldots\,p-1}$ and
% a number $a\in\set{0,1,\ldots\,p-1}$, find an exponent~$n$ such that
% $b^n\equiv a\pmod{p}$.
% \end{problem}

% We can obviously brute force the problem by trying all exponents up to
% $p-1$. (Why suffices?)
% But that is slow.
% As far as we know, the Discrete Logarithm problem is
% very hard to solve quickly.

%<*prob:Crossword>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Crossword}]\index{Crossword@\probname{Crossword} problem}
Given an $n\times n$ grid, and a set of $2n$s-many strings, each
of length~$n$, decide if the
\citetext{words can be packed into the grid}{%
  The earliest known example is the Sator Square,\index{Stator Square} 
  five Latin words that pack into a grid.
  \begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics[height=.75in]{complexity/pix/sator.jpg}}
  \hspace{3em}
  \begin{tabular}{ccccc}
  S &A &T &O &R  \\
  A &R &E &P &O  \\
  T &E &N &E &T  \\
  O &P &E &R &A  \\
  R &O &T &A &S
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  % https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sator_Square
  % By M Disdero (Taken at Oppede, Luberon, France) [GFDL (http://www.gnu.org/copyleft/fdl.html) or CC BY-SA 3.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/3.0)], via Wikimedia Commons
  % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/7/71/Sator_Square_at_Opp%C3%A8de.jpg
  It appears in many places in the Roman Empire, often as graffiti.
  For instance, it
  was found in the ruins of Pompeii.
  Like many word game solutions it sacrifices comprehension for form but
  it is a perfectly grammatical sentence that translates as something like,
  ``The farmer Arepo works the wheel with effort.''
  % See https://www.academia.edu/5111444/_Magic_Squares_Alphabet_Jumbles_Riddles_and_more_The_culture_of_word-games_among_the_graffiti_of_Pompeii_in_The_Muse_at_Play._Riddles_and_Wordplay_in_Greek_and_Latin_Poetry_eds._J._Kwapisz_D._Petrain_and_M._Szymanski_Munich_De_Gruyter_2012_65-80
}.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Crossword>

\begin{example} % http://cgi.csc.liv.ac.uk/~ped/teachadmin/COMP202/annotated_np.html
Can we pack the words
\str{AGE}, \str{AGO}, \str{BEG}, \str{CAB}, \str{CAD}, and \str{DOG}
into a $3\times 3$ grid?
\begin{animatedfigure}{Instance of the Crossword
  problem}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=last]{2}{complexity/asy/problems/problems}{00}{01}
\end{animatedfigure} 
\end{example}

%<*prob:FifteenGame>
\begin{problem}[\probname{$15$ Game}]\index{15 Game@\probname{$15$ Game} problem}
Given an $n\times n$ grid holding tiles numbered $1$, \ldots\,~$n-1$,
and a blank,
find the minimum number of moves that will put the tile numbers into
ascending order.
A move consists of switching a tile with an adjacent blank.
\end{problem}
%</prob:FifteenGame>

This game was
\citetext{popularized as a toy}{%
  It was invented by Noyes Palmer Chapman, a postmaster in Canastota, New York.
  As early as 1874 he showed friends a precursor puzzle.
  By December~1879
  copies of the improved puzzle were circulating in the northeast
  and students in the American School for the Deaf and other
  started manufacturing it.
  They become popular as the ``Gem Puzzle.''
  Noyes Chapman had applied for a patent in February, 1880. 
  By that time the game had became a craze in the US,
  somewhat like Rubik's Cube a century later.
  It was also popular in Canada and Europe.
  See~\autocite{wiki:FifteenPuzzle}}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics[width=0.28\textwidth]{complexity/pix/15game.png}
\end{center}

The final three problems may seem inextricably linked, but
as we understand them today, they seem to be different in the
big-$\bigOh$ behavior of the algorithms to solve them. 
% In 1801, Gauss gave two reasons to solve them,
% ``The problem of distinguishing prime numbers from composite numbers
% and of resolving the latter into their prime factors is known to be one
% of the most important and useful in arithmetic.
% It has engaged the industry and wisdom of ancient and modern geometers
% to such an extent that it
% would be superfluous to discuss the problem at length \ldots{}
% Further, the dignity of the science itself
% seems to require that every possible means be explored for the solution of a
% problem so elegant and so celebrated.''

%<*prob:Divisors>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Divisor}]\index{Divisor@\probname{Divisor} problem}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, find a nontrivial divisor.\label{prob:Divisor}
\end{problem}
%</prob:Divisors>

When the numbers are sufficiently large,
\citetext{we know of no efficient algorithm to find divisors}{% https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integer_factorization
  An effort in 2009 to factor a $768$-bit number ($232$-digits) used
  hundreds of machines and took two years.
  The researchers estimated that a $1024$-bit number would take about
  a thousand times as long.}.\footnote{%
  No efficient algorithm is known on a non-quantum computer.}
However, as is so often the case, at this time
we also have no proof that no efficient algorithm exists.\footnote{%
  There is no proof despite centuries of ingenious attacks on the problems
  by many of the brightest minds of the past, and of today.
  The presumed difficulty of this problem is at the heart of widely used
  algorithms in cryptography.}
Not all numbers of a given length are equally hard to factor.
The hardest numbers to factor, using the best currently known techniques,
are semiprimes,\index{semiprime}\index{number!semiprime} 
the product of two prime numbers.

%<*prob:PrimeFactorization>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Prime Factorization}]\index{Prime Factorization@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}  \label{prob:Factorization}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, produce its decomposition into a product of primes.
\end{problem}
%</prob:PrimeFactorization>

\citetext{Factoring seems, as far as we know today, to be hard}{%
  Finding factors has for many years been thought hard.
  For instance, a number is called a Mersenne prime
  if it is a prime number of the form $2^n-1$.
  They are named after M~Mersenne, a French friar and important figure in
  the early sharing of scientific results,
  who studied them in the early 1600's.
  He observed that
  if $n$ is prime then $2^n-1$ may be prime, for instance with $n=3$,
  $n=7$, $n=31$, and $n=127$.
  He suspected that others of that form were also prime, in particular
  $n=67$.

  On \ymd{1903}{10}{31}{} F~N Cole,
  then Secretary of the American Mathematical Society,
  made a presentation at a math meeting.
  When introduced, he went to the chalkboard and in complete silence
  computed $2^{67}-1=147\,573\,952\,589\,676\,412\,927$.
  He then moved to the other side of the board, wrote
  $193\,707\,721$ times $761\,838\,257\,287$, and worked through the
  calculation, finally finding equality.
  When he was done Cole returned to his seat, having not uttered a word
  in the hour-long presentation.
  His audience gave him a standing ovation.

  Cole later said that finding the factors had been a significant effort,
  taking ``three years of Sundays.''}.
What about if you only want to know whether a number is prime or composite,
and don't care about its factors?

%<*prob:Composite>
\begin{problem}[\probname{Composite}]\index{Composite@\probname{Composite} problem}\label{prob:Composite}
Given a number~$n\in\N$, determine if it has any nontrivial factors;
that is, decide if there is a number~$a$ that divides~$n$ and
such that $1<a<n$.
\end{problem}
%</prob:Composite>

\margingraphic{0.30\textwidth}{complexity/pix/aks.jpg}{\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AKS_primality_test}{Nitin Saxena (b~1981), Neeraj Kayal (b~1979), Manindra Agrawal (b~1966)}}\index{Saxena, N!picture}\index{Kayal, N!picture}\index{Agrawal, M!picture} % http://www.horizons-2000.org/9.%20Active%20Projects/read/PRIMES%20is%20in%20P.htm; came from MAA focus?
For many years the consensus among experts was that \probname{Composite}
was probably quite hard.\footnote{%
  There are a number of probabilistic algorithms that are often
  used in practice that can test
  primality very quickly, with an extremely small chance of error.}
One reasonable justification was that, 
for centuries, many of the smartest people in the world
had worked on composites
and primes, and none of them had produced a fast test.
But in 2002, M~Agrawal, N~Kayal, and N~Saxena
proved that primality testing can be done in time polynomial 
in the number of digits of the number.
This is the AKS primality test.\footnote{%
  At the time that they did most of the work, Kayal and Saxena were 
  undergraduates.}\index{AKS primality test}
Today, refinments of their technique run in
$\bigOh(n^6)$.

This dramatically illustrates
that, although experts are expert and
their opinions have value, nonetheless they can be wrong.
People producing a result that gainsays established orthodoxy
has happened before and will happen again.

In short,
one correct proof outweighs any number of expert opinions.

  
% ------------------

\begin{exercise}
Decide if each is prime.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $5\,477$
\item $6\,165$
\item $6\,863$
\item $4\,207$
\item $7\,689$ 
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Yes, it is prime.
\item No, it is not prime. 
Besides the trivial ones, the divisors of $6\,165$ are
$3$, $5$, $9$, $15$, $45$, $137$, $411$, $685$, $1\,233$, and~$2\,055$.
\item Yes.
\item No.
Besides itself and~$1$, this number is also divisible by 
$7$ and~$601$.
\item No.
Besides the trivial divisors of itself and~$1$, this number also has
the divisors $3$, $11$, $33$, $233$, $699$, and~$2\,563$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exparts*}

\item $52\,424$
\item $9\,600$
\item $4\,331$
\item $877$
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified

\item One is $3$.
  The entire list is 
  $3$, $9$, $3469$, $10407$, and $31221$.
\item One is $2$.
  The entire list is 
  $2$, $4$, $8$, $6553$, $13106$, $26212$, and $52424$.
\item One is $5$.
  The entire list is
  $2$, $3$, $4$, $5$, $6$, $8$,
  $10$, $12$, $15$, $16$, $20$,
  $24$, $25$, $30$, $32$, $40$,
  $48$, $50$, $60$, $64$, $75$,
  $80$, $96$, $100$, $120$, $128$,
  $150$, $160$, $192$, $200$, $240$,
  $300$, $320$, $384$, $400$, $480$,
  $600$, $640$, $800$, $960$, $1200$,
  $1600$, $1920$, $2400$, $3200$, $4800$,
  and $9600$.
\item One is $61$.
  The entire list is $61$, $71$, $4331$.

\end{answer}

\begin{exercise}
We can specify a propositional logic behavior in a 
truth table and then produce such a statement in
conjunctive normal form.
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$R$}  &  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$  \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$  \\[0.5ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$  \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$        
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts}
\item The two terms $P$ and~$\neg P$ are 
  \definend{atoms}.\index{atom}\index{propositional logic!atom}
  So are $Q$, $\neg Q$, $R$, and~$\neg R$.
  Produce a three-atom clause that evaluates to~$F$ only on the
  $F$-$T$-$F$ line.
\item Produce three-atom clauses for each of the other truth table
  lines having the value~$F$ on the right.
\item Take the conjunction of those four clauses and verify that
  it has the given behavior.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The three-atom clause is $P\vee\neg Q\vee R$.
\item The clause for the $F$-$T$-$T$ line is $P\vee\neg Q\vee \neg R$.
  For the $T$-$F$-$T$ line, use $\neg P\vee Q\vee \neg R$.
  For the $T$-$T$-$T$ line, use $\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R$.
\item Here is the entire expression in conjunctive normal form.
\begin{equation*}
  (P\vee\neg Q\vee R)
  \wedge (P\vee\neg Q\vee \neg R)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee Q\vee \neg R)
  \wedge (\neg P\vee \neg Q\vee \neg R)
\end{equation*}
Verifying the truth table is routine.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Decide if each formula is satisfiable.
\begin{exparts}
\item $(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$
\item $(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This formula is satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg Q$ &$\neg Q\wedge R$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee(\neg Q\wedge R)$ \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$T$ &$T$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$F$ &$T$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item This formula is not satisfiable. 
\begin{center} % \definecolor{rowcolor}{gray}{.93} \rowcolors{3}{rcolor}{}
\small
\begin{tabular}{ccc|ccccc|c}
  $P$  &$Q$  &$R$  &$P\to Q$ &$P\wedge Q$ &$\neg P$ &$(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P$ &$\neg(P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  &$(P\to Q)\wedge \neg((P\wedge Q)\vee \neg P)$  \\ \hline    
  $F$  &$F$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$F$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $F$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$F$ &$T$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\[1ex]     
  $T$  &$F$  &$F$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ &$F$ &$F$ &$F$  &$T$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$F$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$ \\     
  $T$  &$T$  &$T$  &$T$ &$T$ &$F$ &$T$  &$F$  &$F$      
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Each of the five
\citetext{Platonic solids}{%
  See \autocite{wiki:PlatonicSolid}.}
has a Hamiltonian circuit, 
\citetext{as shown}{%
  Some PDF readers cannot do opacity, so you may not see the entire
  Hamiltonian path.}.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems31.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems32.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems33.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems38.pdf}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems36.pdf}
\end{center}
Hamilton used the fourth, the dodecahedron, for
his game.
Find a Hamiltonian circuit for the third and the fifth,
the octahedron and the icosahedron.
To make the connections easier to see, below we have grabbed a face in the back
of each solid, and expanded it 
until we could squash the entire shape down into the plane
without any edge crossings.
\begin{center}
 \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems19.pdf}}
\hspace{1em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems20.pdf}}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}\verified
For the octahedron, the path 
$\sequence{v_0,v_1,v_5,v_4,v_3,v_2,v_0}$ will do.
For the icosahedron, one path is 
$\sequence{v_0,v_{10},v_{11},v_8,v_9,v_6,v_7,v_2,v_3,v_4,v_5,v_1,v_0}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:FiveColors}
\begin{exparts*}
\item
The \probname{Four Color} problem requires that the countries be
contiguous, that they not consist of separated regions (that is, components).
Give a planar map that consists of separated regions that requires
five colors.
\item We also define adjacent to mean sharing a border that is an interval,
not just a point.
Give a planar map that, without that restriction, 
would require five colors.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This map shows five countries. 
They are not contiguous; for instance, country~$A$ has five separate
components.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems22.pdf}
\end{center}
Because of the portion in the upper left, 
country~$A$ must be a different color than any of the others.
The other portions similarly 
give that all the other countries are different colors than each other.
In total there are five countries, each a different color than all the
others.
\item Make a circle and split it into five wedges.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  % based on http://www.herinst.org/BusinessManagedDemocracy/introduction/interlocking.html
This shows interlocking corporate directorships.
The vertices are corporations and 
they are connected if they share a member of their Board of Directors
(the data is from 2004).
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems27.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item Is there a path from AT\&T to Ford Motor?
\item Can you get from Haliburton to Ford Motor?
\item Can you get from Caterpillar to Ford Motor?
\item JP Morgan to Ford Motor?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item AT\&T connects to Citigroup, which connects to Ford Motor.
\item Haliburton connects to Citigroup, which connects to Ford Motor.
\item There is no connection between Caterpillar and Ford Motor.
\item Also no connection.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
A popular game extends the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem 
by counting the degrees of separation.
Below is a portion of the movie connection graph, 
where actors are connected if they have ever been 
together in a movie. %\footnote{%
%   Information from \protect\url{https://oracleofbacon.org/}}
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems24.pdf}
\end{center}
A person's
\definend{Bacon number} is the number of edges 
connecting them to Bacon, or infinity if they are not connected.
The game \citetext{\textit{Six Degrees of Kevin Bacon}}{%
  One night, three college friends,
  Brian Turtle, Mike Ginelli, and Craig Fass,
  were watching movies. 
  \textit{Footloose} was followed by \textit{Quicksilver}, 
  and between was a commercial for a third Kevin Bacon movie. 
  It seemed like Kevin Bacon was in everything! 
  This prompted the question of whether Bacon had ever worked with 
  De Niro? 
  The answer at that time was no, but De Niro was in 
  \textit{The Untouchables} with Kevin Costner, who was in 
  \textit{JFK} with Bacon. 
  The game was born.
  It became popular when they wrote to Jon Stewart about it and appeared
  on his show.
  (From \autocite{blog:Blanda}.)
  See  \protect\url{https://oracleofbacon.org/}.} 
asks:~is everyone connected to Kevin Bacon by at most six movies?
\begin{exparts}
\item What is Elvis's Bacon number?
\item John Kennedy's (no, it is not that John Kennedy)?
\item Bacon's?
\item How many movies separate me from Meryl Streep?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts*}
\item 2
\item 2
\item 0
\item 4 (I was only in the deleted scenes.  But it is all just fun.)
\end{exparts*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:ElectoralCollegeQuestion}
Using the data in \cref{ex:ElectoralCollege}, decide if there
could be a tie in the 2020 Electoral College.
\begin{answer}\verified
A $269$-$269$ tie is easy to concoct.
For example, the electoral votes of CA, TX, FL, NY, IL, PA, 
OH, GA, MI, NC, and~VA add to $269$.
\end{answer}
% sage: 55+38+29+29+20+20+18+16+16+15+13
% 269

\begin{exercise}
Find the shortest path in this graph
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems28.pdf}
\end{center}
\begin{exparts*}
\item from $q_2$ to~$q_7$,
\item from $q_0$ to~$q_8$,
\item from $q_8$ to~$q_0$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
We can do these by eye.
\begin{exparts}
\item The shortest is $q_2$ to~$q_1$ to~$q_7$, which has length~$14$.
\item The shortest is $q_0$ to~$q_3$ to~$q_4$ to~$q_8$, of length~$23$.
\item There is no path between these two vertices.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Subset Sum} instance with 
$S=\set{21, 33, 49, 42, 19}$
and target~$T=114$ has no solution.
Verify that by brute force, by checking every possible combination.
% (You may find that the easiest way is to check it with a program.)
\begin{answer}
\figureref{fig:SubsetSumAllPossibilities} is a check of every possibility.
  \begin{figure}
    \centering\small
  \begin{minipage}{0.35\textwidth}
  \begin{tabular}{ccccc|r}   
     $21$ 
       &$33$
       &$49$
       &$42$
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{$19$}
       &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Total}}   \\ \hline   
     N &N &N &N &N &$0$ \\
     N &N &N &N &Y &$19$ \\
     N &N &N &Y &N &$42$ \\
     N &N &N &Y &Y &$61$ \\
     N &N &Y &N &N &$49$ \\
     N &N &Y &N &Y &$68$ \\
     N &N &Y &Y &N &$91$ \\

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What shape is a $3$-clique?
A $2$-clique?
\begin{answer}\verified
A $3$-clique is a triangle.
A $2$-clique is an edge.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
How many edges does a $k$-clique have?
\begin{answer}\verified
A $k$-clique has $k(k-1)/2$ edges, one for each combination of two vertices.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:CourseScheduling}
The \definend{\probname{Course Scheduling}}\index{Course Scheduling@\probname{Course Scheduling} problem}  
problem 
starts with a list of students and the 
classes that they wish to take, and then finds how many time slots are needed
to schedule the classes.
If there is a student taking two classes then those two will not be scheduled 
to meet at the same time.
Here is an instance:~a school has classes in Astronomy, Biology, 
Computing, Drama, English, French, Geography, History, and~Italian.
After students sign up,
the graph below shows which classes have an overlap.
For instance
Astronomy and Biology share at least one student while Biology and Drama
do not.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs09.pdf}
  % \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs10.pdf}
\end{center}
What is the minimum number of class times that we must use?  
In coloring terms, we define that classes meeting at the same time
are the same color and we ask for the minimum number of colors needed so that
no two same-colored vertices share an edge.
\begin{exparts*}
\item Show that no three-coloring suffices.
\item Produce a four-coloring.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
Suppose that a three-coloring suffices.
Then the  
three vertices $A$, $C$, and~$D$ form a triangle, so they must be different
colors.
Since $CDF$ is also a triangle, we conclude that $A$ and $F$ are the same
colors. 
So far we have:
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D}$.

Next, consider triangle $DFI$. It's presence gives that $C$ and~$I$ are the same
colors.
So we have 
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D}$.
Then triangle $EFI$ leads to 
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D,E}$.

Finally, triangle $CFG$ gives that $D$ and~$G$ are the same color, giving
$\mathcal{C}_0=\set{A,F}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_1=\set{C,I}$, 
$\mathcal{C}_2=\set{D,E,G}$.
But the contradicts the presence of triangle $GEF$.
\item
This is a four-coloring.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{languages/asy/graphs/graphs10.pdf}
\end{center}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Some authors define the \probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
problem as:~given a finite set of propositional logic statements,
find if there is a single input tuple $b_0,\ldots b_{j-1}$,
where each $b_i$ is either $T$ or $F$, that satisfies them all.
Show that this is equivalent to the definition given in \cref{prob:SAT}.
\begin{answer}
A finite set of propositional logic statements $\set{S_0,\ldots S_{k-1}}$
is satisfiable if and only if the single statement
$S_0\wedge S_1\ldots \wedge S_{k-1}$ is satisfiable.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Imagine a directed graph where the edges are prone to failure.
% For instance, it may be a road network in a region prone to flooding.
% For each edge we know the probability that the edge will fail.
% Show how to use a solution to the \probname{Shortest Path} problem to 
% find the path between two vertices least likely to fail.
% \begin{answer}
% The probability of success is $1$~minus the probability of failure.
% Recall that logarithms turn multiplication into 
% addition:~$\log(p_0\cdot p_1\cdots p_k)=\log(p_0)+\cdots+\log(p_k)$.
% With that in mind,
% label each edge with the negative of the logarithm (any base) of the 
% probability of success.
% Minimizing the sum of the negative logarithms,
% which is finding the shortest path in this graph, is the same as maximizing
% the positive sum.
% That's the same as maximizing the product of the probabilities of 
% success, which is the overall probability of success.
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Recall that 
\probname{Vertex Cover}\index{Vertex Cover@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem} 
inputs a graph
$\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$ and a number~$k\in\N$,
and asks if there is a subset~$S$ of at most $k$~vertices 
such that for each edge at least one endpoint is an element of~$S$.
The \definend{\probname{Independent Set}}\index{Independent Set@\probname{Independent Set} problem}
problem inputs a graph and a 
number~$\hat{k}\in\N$
and asks if there is a subset~$\hat{S}$ with at least $\hat{k}$~vertices 
such that for each edge at most one endpoint is in~$\hat{S}$.
The two are obviously related.
\begin{exparts}
\item
In this graph
find a vertex cover~$S$ with $k=2$ elements.
Find an independent set with $\hat{k}=4$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems29.pdf}
\end{center}
\item
In this graph
find a vertex cover with $k=3$ elements,
and an independent set with $\hat{k}=3$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems30.pdf}
\end{center} 
\item
In this graph
find a vertex cover~$S$ with $k=4$ elements.
Find an independent set~$\hat{S}$ with $\hat{k}=6$ elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity22.pdf}
\end{center}
\item Prove that $S$ is a vertex cover if and only if
its complement~$\hat{S}=\mathcal{N}-S$ is an independent set.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item A vertex cover with $k=2$ elements is $S=\set{q_1,q_3}$.
An independent set with $\hat{k}=4$ elements is 
$\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_2,q_4,q_5}$.
\item A vertex cover with $k=3$ elements is $S=\set{q_1,q_2,q_4d}$.
An independent set with $\hat{k}=3$ elements is 
$\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_3,q_5}$.
\item A vertex cover with $k=4$ elements is $S=\set{q_2,q_5,q_8,q_9}$.
An independent set with $\hat{k}=6$ elements is 
  $\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7}$.
\item For any edge, if it has at least one endpoint in
$S$ then it has at most one endpoint in the complement $\hat{S}=\mathcal{N}-S$.
Conversely, If an edge has at most one endpoint in~$\hat{S}$ 
then it has at least one endpoint in the complement~$S=\mathcal{N}-\hat{S}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  Jan Verschelde,
  \url{http://homepages.math.uic.edu/~jan/mcs401/partitioning.pdf}
\end{credit}
A college department has instructors $A$, $B$, $C$, $D$, and $E$.
They need placing into courses $0$, $1$, $2$, $3$, and~$4$. 
The available time slots are $\alpha$, $\beta$, $\gamma$, $\delta$,
and~$\varepsilon$.
This shows which instructors can teach which courses,
and which courses can run in which slots.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems39.pdf}
\end{center}
For example, instructor~$A$ can only teach courses $1$, $2$, and~$3$.
And, course~$0$ can only run at time~$\alpha$ or time~$\delta$.
Verify that this is an instance of the 
\probname{Three-dimensional Matching} problem and find a match.
\begin{answer}
The \probname{Three-dimensional Matching} problem requires that the
three sets, the instructors, the courses, and the time slots, 
all have the same size.
In this instance, all three have five elements.

A suitable matching is 
$\hat{M}=\set{
              \sequence{A,2,\varepsilon},
              \sequence{B,0,\alpha},
              \sequence{C,3,\gamma},
              \sequence{D,1,\beta},
              \sequence{E,4,\delta}
              }$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Consider \probname{Three Dimensional Matching}, \cref{Prob:ThreeDMatching}.
Let
$X=\set{\str{a},\str{b},\str{c}}$, $Y=\set{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$,
and~$Z=\set{\str{a},\str{d},\str{e}}$.
\begin{exparts}
\item List all the elements of $M=X\times Y\times Z$.
\item Is there a three element subset $\hat{M}$ whose triples
have the property that no two of them agree on any coordinate?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
The set $M=X\times Y\times Z$ contains these twenty seven 
triples $\sequence{x,y,z}$.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
  \multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{a}$}
  &\multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{d}$}
  &\multicolumn{1}{l}{$z=\str{e}$}    \\
  \begin{tabular}{ccc}
    $\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{d},\str{a}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{d},\str{a}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{c},\str{b},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{c},\str{a}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{a}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  &\begin{tabular}{ccc}
    $\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{d},\str{d}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{d},\str{d}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{c},\str{b},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{c},\str{d}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{d}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}
  &\begin{tabular}{ccc}
    $\sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{c},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{a},\str{d},\str{e}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{b},\str{b},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{b},\str{d},\str{e}}$  \\
    $\sequence{\str{c},\str{b},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{c},\str{e}}$
      &$\sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{e}}$  \\
  \end{tabular}  
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item There are lots of correct answers.
  One is $\hat{M}=\set{
                       \sequence{\str{a},\str{b},\str{a}},
                       \sequence{\str{b},\str{c},\str{d}},
                       \sequence{\str{c},\str{d},\str{e}}
                       }$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% [['c', 'd', 'e'], ['b', 'c', 'd'], ['a', 'b', 'a']]

\begin{exercise}
In \cref{ex:broadcast} the broadcast takes four steps.
Can it be done in fewer?
\end{exercise}
\begin{answer}
No, it cannot be done in three steps.
The longest path is to~$v_{10}$, of three edges.
Even if we start on that path by broadcasting to~$v_4$ at the
first stage, $v_0$ still must initiate broadcasts to $v_6$ and~$v_3$, 
each of which will take two stages. 
But $v_0$ can only initiate one at a time.
\end{answer}

\end{exercises}
\index{problem miscellany|)}







% =======================================================
\section{Problems, algorithms, and programs} \label{sec:ProbsAlgosPrograms}
Now, with many examples in hand,
we will briefly reflect on problems and solutions.
We will keep this discussion on an intuitive level only\Dash indeed, 
many of these things have no widely accepted
precise definition.

%<*discussion:Problem>
A problem is a job, a task.\index{problem}
Somewhat more precisely, it is a 
\citetext{uniform family of tasks}{From \autocite{Jones}.}, 
typically with an unbounded number of instances. 
For a sense of `family', 
contrast the general \probname{Shortest Path} problem
with that of finding the shortest path between 
Los Angeles and New York. 
The first is a family while the second is an instance.
We are more likely to talk about the family,
both because the second is a special case, so any conclusions about the 
first subsume the second, and because 
the first feels more natural.\footnote{There are interesting problems
  with only one task, such as
  computing the digits of $\pi$.}
We are most focused on problems that can be solved with a mechanism,
although we continue to be interested to learn 
that a problem
cannot be solved mechanically at all.
%</discussion:Problem>

%<*discussion:Algorithm>
An algorithm describes at a high level an effective way to solve a 
problem.\footnote{% https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/31953
  \citetext{There is no widely-accepted formal definition of `algorithm'}{%
    This discussion derives from  \autocite{pseudonym}.}.
  Whatever it is,
