  it fits between `mathematical function' and `computer program'.
  For example, a `sort' function takes in a set of 
  items and returns the sorted sequence.
  This behavior could be implemented using different algorithms:~merge
  sort, heap sort, etc.
  In turn, each algorithm could be implemented by many programs,
  written in different languages and for different platforms.
  So the best handle that we have is informal\Dash an `algorithm' is
  an equivalence class of programs (i.e., Turing machines),
  where two programs are equivalent
  if they do essentially the same thing.\index{algorithm!definition}
  % Any two programs which implement the same algorithm must compute the same
  % function, but the converse is not true.
  % Similarly, there is an equivalence class between algorithms, where two
  % algorithms are equivalent if they compute the same mathematical function.  
  % The hard part in all this is trying to capture what we mean by
  % ``essentially the same thing.''
  % There are some things that we obviously should include.
  % For example, fix a program that does a merge sort on sequences of strings
  % and that uses \str{x} for a variable but not \str{y}.
  % The program that you get by simultaneously replacing all the
  % \str{x}'s with \str{y}'s is essentially the same.
  % And, there are some things that we may want not to include; an
  % operating system kernel is a single program but implements many
  % algorithms.
  % But in any event, giving a completely satisfactory
  % definition of `algorithm' is an open problem.
}\index{algorithm}
An algorithm is not an implementation,
although it should be described in a way that is 
detailed enough that implementing it is
routine for an experienced professional.

One subtle point about algorithms 
is that while they are abstractions, they are nonetheless based on
an underlying computing model.
An algorithm that is based on a Turing machine model
for adding one to an input 
would be very different than
an algorithm to do the same task on a model that is like
a desktop computer, such as the RAM model.\index{algorithm!reliance on model}
%</discussion:Algorithm>

\margingraphic*{0.25\textwidth}{complexity/pix/science_united.png}
An example of a very different computing model that an algorithm could target
is distributed computation.\index{distributed computation}\index{computation!distributed}\index{Science United}
Science United lets anyone with a computer and an internet connection 
help scientific research projects by giving them computing power. 
These projects do research in astronomy, physics, biomedicine, 
mathematics, and environmental science.
Contributers install a free program that runs scientific jobs 
in the background, when they are not using the computer.
This is massively parallel computation.\footnote{%
  There are now coming up on a million volunteers offering computing time.
  To join them, visit \protect\url{https://scienceunited.org/}.}

%<*discussion:Program>
A program\index{program} is an implementation of an algorithm,
typically expressed in a formal computer language, and often 
designed to be executed on a specific computing platform.
%</discussion:Program>

To illustrate the differences between the problems, algorithms, and programs,
consider the problem of
\probname{Prime Factorization}.\index{Prime Factorization@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}
One algorithm is to use brute force, that is, given an input~$n>1$,
try every number~$k\in\open{1}{n}$ to see if $k$ divides~$n$.
We could implement that algorithm with a program written in Scheme.




\subsection{Types of problems}
There are patterns to the problems that 
we see in the Theory of Computation.
As a first example, a problem type that we have already seen
is a 
\definend{function problem}.\index{function problem}\index{problem!function}
These ask that an algorithm that has  
a single output for each input. 
A example is the 
\probname{Prime Factorization} problem,\index{Prime Factorization@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}
which takes in a natural number and returns its prime decomposition, perhaps
as a sequence of pairs, $\sequence{\text{prime},\text{exponent}}$.
Another example is the problem of finding the greatest common divisor of two
natural numbers, where the input is a pair of natural numbers
and the output is a natural number.

A second common problem type 
is the 
\definend{optimization problem}.\index{optimization problem}\index{problem!optimization}
These call for a solution that is best, according to some metric.
The \probname{Shortest Path} problem is one of these, as 
is the \probname{Minimal Spanning Tree} problem.

A perhaps less familiar problem type is a 
\definend{search problem}.\index{search problem}\index{problem!search}
For one of these, while there may be many
solutions in the search space, 
the algorithm can stop when it has found any one.
A natural example inputs a Propositional
Logic statement and outputs a
line in the truth table that witnesses that the statement is satisfiable
(or signals that there is no such line).
There may be many such lines but it only needs to find one.
Another example is the problem, 
``Given a weighted graph, and two vertices, and a bound $B\in\R$,
find a path between the vertices that costs less than the bound.'' 
Still another example is that of finding a $B$-coloring for a graph,
from among possibly many such colorings.
Another example is the \probname{Knapsack} problem. 
This problem type also appeared in the discussion on nondeterminism
on page~\pageref{anim:NondeterministicComputationTree}, where
we defined that a string is accepted if in the computation tree 
we could find at least one accepting branch.

A  
\definend{decision problem}\index{decision problem}\index{problem!decision}
is one with a `Yes' or `No' answer.\footnote{%
  Although a decision problem calls for producing a function of a kind,
  a Boolean function,
  they are common enough to be a separate category.
  } 
The first problem that we saw, the 
\textit{Entscheidungsproblem},\index{Entscheidungsproblem@\textit{Entscheidungsproblem}}
is one of these.\footnote{%
  Recall that the word is German for ``decision problem''
  and that it asks for an algorithm to decide, given a mathematical statement,
  whether that statement is true or false.}
We have also seen decision problems 
in conjunction with the \HP{}, such as the problem of
determining, given an index~$e$, whether $\TMfcn_e$ will 
output a seven for any input.
In this chapter we saw
the problem of deciding whether a given natural number is prime,
the \probname{Composite} problem,\index{Composite@\probname{Composite} problem}
as well as the 
\probname{Clique} problem,\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem}
the \probname{Partition} problem,\index{Partition@\probname{Partition} problem} 
and the \probname{Subset Sum} problem.\index{Subset Sum@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}

Often a decision problem is expressed as a
\definend{language decision problem},\index{language decision problem}\index{problem!language decision}\index{language!decision problem}
or \definend{language recognition problem},\index{language recognition problem}\index{problem!language recognition}
where we are given some language and asked for an algorithm to
decide if its input is a member of that language.
We did lots of these in the Automata chapter, such as producing a 
Finite State machine that 
decides if an input string is a member of  
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{a},\str{b}}}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ contains at least two \str{b}'s}}$,
or proving that no such machine can determine membership
in $\set{\str{a}^n\str{b}^n\suchthat n\in\N}$.

This relates to the discussion from the Languages section, on
page~\pageref{discuss:LanguageDecidesRecognizes}, about the distinction 
between deciding a language and recognizing it.

\begin{definition}
A language~$\lang$ is 
\definend{decided} by a Turing machine,\index{Turing machine!language decided}\index{language!accepted by Turing machine}\index{decided language}\index{language!decided}
or \definend{Turing machine decidable},\index{Turing machine!decidable}\index{decidable}
if the function computed by that machine 
is the characteristic function of the language. 
The language is 
\definend{recognized}\index{Turing machine!language recognized}\index{language!recognized by Turing machine}\index{recognized language}\index{language!recognized}
(or \definend{accepted}) by a machine\index{Turing machine!language accepted}\index{language!accepted by Turing machine}\index{accepted language}
when for each input~$\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\!$, if $\sigma\in\lang$ 
then the machine returns~$\str{1}$,
while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then it
does not halt and return~$\str{1}$.
\end{definition}
\noindent
Restated, $\TM$ decides the language $\lang$ if the machine 
has this input-output behavior.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=\charfcn{\lang}(\sigma)=
  \begin{cases}
    1  &\case{if $\sigma\in\lang$}  \\
    0  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Note in particular that in this case the machine halts for all inputs.
Note also that if a machine recognizes a language then when $\sigma\notin\lang$,
possibly the machine just does not halt.

\begin{remark}
One reason for our interest in problems of language membership
arises from practice. 
For instance, a language complier must recognize whether
a given source file is a member of the language.
Another is that Finite State machines can only do one thing, 
decide languages, and so to compare these with other machines 
we must do so by comparing which languages they can decide.
Still another reason is that in many contexts stating a problem in this
way is natural, as we saw with the \HP.
\end{remark}

The distinctions between problem types are fuzzy, and
we can often describe a task with more than one type.
The task of determining the evenness of a number, for instance, 
could be stated as the function problem `given~$n$, return its
remainder on division by~$2$', or as the decision problem
`given~$n$, decide if it is even'. 

There, the different types are essentially the same, but sometimes
finding the problem type that best fits a task requires judgement.
Consider the task of finding roots of a 
polynomial.
We may express it as a function problem with
`given a polynomial~$p$, return the set of its rational number roots',
or as a language decision problem with
`decide if a given $\sequence{p,r}$,
belongs to the set of all sequences consisting of a polynomial 
and one of its rational roots'.
The second option, for which the algorithm just 
plugs $r$ into~$p$, seems not to
capture the essential difficulty involved in find a root.

When we have a choice of equivalent problem types,
we prefer language decision problems.
% \footnote{%
%   Because of this,
%   some authors modify the definition of a Turing machine
%   to have it come with a subset of accepting states.
%   Such a machine solves a problem
%   if it halts on all input strings,
%   and when it halts it is in an accepting state exactly when that
%   string is in the language.
% }
It is our default interpretation of
`problem'. % \footnote{%
%   % from https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/79729
%   Not every computational problem is naturally expressable as a 
%   language decision problem 
%   Consider the task of sorting the characters of strings
%   into ascending order.
%   We could try to express it as the language of sorted strings
%   $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is sorted}}$. 
%   But this 
%   does not require that we find a good way 
%   to sort an unsorted input.
%   Another thought is to consider the language of pairs
%   $\sequence{\sigma,p}$ where $p$ is a permutation of the numbers
%   $0,\ldots \lh{\sigma}-1$ that brings the string into ascending order.
%   But here also the formulation 
%   seems to not capture the sorting problem, in that recognizing a
%   correct permutation feels different than generating one from scratch.
% }
In addition, we will be sloppy about the distinction between the
decision problem for a language and the language itself; for instance, we
will write $\prob$ for a problem.

\begin{example}
The \probname{Satisfiability} 
problem,\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem!as a language recognition problem} 
as stated, is a decision problem.
We can recast it as the problem of determining membership in the language
$
  \SAT=
  \set{F\suchthat
    \text{$F$ is a satisfiable propositional logic statement}}
$.\index{SAT@$\SAT$}
This recasting is trivial, suggesting that the language recognition
problem form is a natural way to describe the underlying task.
\end{example}

Recasting optimization problems as language decision problems
often involves
using a parametrized sequence of languages.

\begin{example}
The \probname{Chromatic Number} problem inputs a graph 
and returns a minimal 
number~$B\in\N$ such that the graph is $B$-colorable.
Recast it by considering the
family of languages,
$
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat \text{$\mathcal{G}$ that has a $B$-coloring}}
$.
% To see that having the capability to solve instances of $\lang_B$ 
% is in principle the same as being able to solve the optimization problem, 
% imagine that we are given a graph. 
If we could solve the decision problem for those languages then
we could compute the minimal chromatic number by 
testing $B=1$, $B=2$, etc., until we find the smallest $B$ for which
$\mathcal{G}\in\lang_B$.
% Conversely, if we could compute the minimal chromatic number then
% we could answer questions about particular~$\lang_B$'s, simply by seeing
% whether or not~$B$ is less than the minimum.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
The \probname{Travelling Salesman} problem
is an optimization problem.
Recast it as a sequence of language decision problems as 
above:~consider a parameter~$B\in\N$ and define
$
  \TS_B=\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat
    \text{the graph~$\mathcal{G}$ has a circuit  
          of length no more than $B$}}
$.
\end{example}

For a task, we want to state it as a problem in a way that 
captures the essential difficulty.
In particular, these recastings of optimization problems
preserves polytime solvability.
For instance, if there were a power $k\in\N$ 
such that for each~$B$ we could solve $\mathcal{TS}_B$ in 
time~$\bigOh(n^k)$ then  
looping through $B=1$, $B=2$, etc.,\ will solve
the \probname{Travelling Salseman} problem in polytime, 
namely time~$\bigOh(n^{k+1})$.





\subsection{Statements and representations}
To be complete, the description of a problem must include the form of the
inputs and outputs.
For instance, if we state a problem 
as:~`\citetext{input two numbers and output their midpoint}{% 
  See \protect\url{https://hal.archives-ouvertes.fr/file/index/docid/576641/filename/computing-midpoint.pdf}.}'
then we have not fully specified what needs to be done.
The input or output might use 
strings representing decimal numbers, or might be in binary,
or even might be in unary,
where the number $n$ is represented with $n$-many \str{1}'s.\footnote{%
  An experienced programmer may have the reaction that unary is
  wasteful\Dash
  if the input
  is one thousand then this representation would require the 
  machine to take a thousand steps just to read the input, 
  while in binary notation the input is $\str{1111101000}$, which 
  takes only ten steps to read.
  But unary is not completely useless;~we have found that it
  suited our purpose when we simply wanted to illustrate
  Turing machines.
  In any event, it certainly is possible.}
% Or we could specify something more exotic
% such as putting the sequence numbers as 
% powers of primes, so that~$\sequence{2,1,3}$ becomes 
% the single number $2^23^15^3=1500$ and then we represent that in a string of
% decimal digits.

The representation of the input and output data both matters and doesn't matter.
One sense in which it
matters is that the input's form can change the algorithm
that we choose, or its runtime behavior.
Suppose for instance that we must decide whether a number is
divisible by four.
If the input is a bitstring representing the number in binary then 
the algorithm is immediate:~a number is divisible by four 
if and only if in its 
\citetext{final two bits are \str{00}}{%
  Decimal representation is not much harder since
  a decimal number is divisible by four if and only if the final two digits
  are in the set $\set{\str{00},\str{04}, \,\ldots\,\str{96}}$.}.\footnote{%
    Thus, on a Turing machine, if when the machine starts the head is under
    the final character, then the machine does not even need to read the
    entire input to decide the question.
    The algorithm runs in time independent of the input length.
  }
In contrast, if the number is represented in unary then we may 
scan the \str{1}'s, keeping track of the current remainder modulo~$4$.

However, the representation doesn't matter in the sense that 
if we have an algorithm for one representation in hand
then we can solve the problem for other representations by translating 
to what the algorithm expects, and then applying that algorithm.
For example, 
for the divisible-by-four problem we could handle unary inputs by converting
them to binary and then applying the binary algorithm.\footnote{%
  That is, the unary case reduces to the binary one.}
The same applies to output representations.
% This might be some translation inefficiency, but it gives 
% an answer.

% \margingraphic{0.2\textwidth}{complexity/pix/norm.png}{\label{fig:xkcdnorm}\href{https://xkcd.com/2116/}{Courtesy \url{xkcd.com}}}
Another way in which the representation doesn't matter is that
typically we find that the costs of different representations
don't change the Big~$\bigOh$ runtime behavior.
For example we might have a graph algorithm whose run time is not large at 
all,~$\bigOh(n\lg n)$.
Even for this minimal time, we  can find a 
representation for the input graphs,
such as where inputting takes $\bigOh(n)$ time,
that leaves the algorithm analysis conclusion unchanged
at~$\bigOh(n\lg n)$. 

With this in mind, we will adopt the point of view,
which we shall call \definend{Lipton's Thesis},\index{Lipton's Thesis} that 
\citetext{everything of interest can be represented with reasonable efficiency by bitstrings}{%
  See
  \url{https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2010/11/07/what-is-a-complexity-class/}.
  Of course, a wag may say that
  if it cannot be represented by 
  bitstrings then it isn't of interest.
  But we mean something less tautological:~we mean that if we could 
  want to compute with it then it can be put in bitstrings.
  For example, we find that we can process speech, adjust colors on an image, 
  or regulate pressure in a rocket fuel tank, all in bitstrings,
  despite what may at first encounter seem to be the inheriently 
  analog nature of these things.}.\footnote{%
  `Reasonable' means that it is not so inefficient as to greatly
  change the big-$\bigOh$ behavior.}\index{representation, of a problem}
This applies to 
all of the mathematical problems stated earlier.
But it also applies to cases that may seem less natural, such as that we 
can use bitstrings to faithfully represent 
\citetext{Beethoven's 9th Symphony}{%
  The official story is that
  CD's are $72$ minutes long so that they can hold this piece.},
or an exquisite Old Master.\footnote{
  This is in some ways like Church's Thesis.
  We cannot prove it, but our 
  experience with digital reproduction of music, movies, 
  etc.,\ finds that it is so.}
\begin{fig}{\textit{Basket of Fruit} by Caravaggio (1571--1610)} % https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/e/e5/Basket_of_Fruit-Caravaggio_%28c.1595%29.jpg
  \includegraphics[width=0.8\textwidth]{complexity/pix/caravaggio.jpg}
\end{fig}

Consequently, in practice  
\citetext{researchers often do not mention representations}{%
  This is like a programmer saying, ``My program
  inputs a number'' rather than, ``My program inputs the
  binary representation of a number.''
  It is also like a person saying, ``That's me on the card'' rather
  than ``On that card is a picture of me.''}.
We may describe
the \probname{Shortest Path} problem as, 
``Given a weighted graph and two vertices \ldots{}''
in place of the more 
complete, ``Given
the following reasonably efficient bitstring representation of a 
weighted graph~$\mathcal{G}$ 
and vertices $v_0$ and~$v_1$, \ldots{}''
Outside of this discussion we also do this,\footnote{
  Naturally some exercises in this section 
  cover representations.} 
\citetext{leaving implementation details to a programmer}{%
  \autocite{article:MarsOrbiter}}.
(When we do discuss representations, we 
write $\strng(x)$ for a convenient, reasonably efficient,
bitstring representation of~$x$.\footnote{%
  Many authors use diamond 
  brackets to stand for a representation,
  as in `$\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}$'.
  Here, we reserve diamond brackets for sequences.})
Basically, the representation details do not affect the outcome of our analysis,
much.

\begin{remark}
There is a caveat.
We have seen that coflating 
$\set{n\in\N\suchthat \text{n is prime}}$
with  
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a prime number}}$
can cause confusion.
The distinction between thinking of an algorithm
as inputting a number and thinking of it as inputting the 
string representation of a number is the basis for describing 
the Big~$\bigOh$ behavior of that algorithm as
pseudopolynomial.
This is because the binary representation of
a number~$n$ takes $\bigOh(\lg n)$ bits and so inputting it takes 
$\bigOh(\lg n)$ ticks.
\end{remark}





 % ......................................
\begin{exercises}
% \item[\parbox{\textwidth}{\it These exercises here discuss 
%   represenations  
%   more than is usual elsewhere in the book.}]

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What is the difference\Dash speaking informally, since some of these 
do not have formal definitions\Dash between an algorithm and:
\begin{exparts*}
\item a heuristic,
\item pseudocode,
\item a Turing machine
\item a flowchart, and
\item a process?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\textit{Note:}~the meanings give here are common usage.
But because these terms are not formally defined, different authors
may use them in different ways.
\begin{exparts}
\item A heuristic is a method that, while it may be used in practice, 
is not guaranteed to be optimal, perfect, logical, or even rational.
An algorithm for a problem is guaranteed to solve the problem.

An example of a heuristic is based on the data 
in \figureref{fig:USPresidentsHeights}.
In the contests for US President since 1900, the taller candidate
has won $20$ out of $29$ times, for a sample proportion of~$0.69$.
The chance of such a result by happenstance is less than $0.02$.
You could decide to bet in the next election on the taller candidate.
This isn't logical, but it is a method and it is used in practice.
\begin{figure}
    \centering\small
    \begin{tabular}[t]{lll|c}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Year}}
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Winner}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Other}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Tallest?}} \\
      \hline
      1900 &W McKinley     &W J Bryan      &N  \\
      1904 &T Roosevelt    &A B Parker     &Y   \\
      1908 &W H Taft       &W J Bryan      &Y    \\
      1912 &W Wilson       &--2 others--   &N    \\
      1916 &W Wilson       &C E Hughes     &Y    \\
      1920 &W G Harding    &J M Cox        &Y    \\
      1924 &C Coolidge     &J W Davis      &N    \\
      1928 &H Hoover       &A Smith        &Y    \\
      1932 &F D Roosevelt  &H Hoover       &Y    \\
      1936 &F D Roosevelt  &A Landon       &Y    \\
      1940 &F D Roosevelt  &W Willkie      &N    \\
      1944 &F D Roosevelt  &T Dewey        &Y    \\
      1948 &H S Truman     &T Dewey        &Y    \\
      1952 &D D Eisenhower &A Stevenson II &Y    \\
      1956 &D D Eisenhower &A Stevenson II &Y    \\
    \end{tabular}
    \quad
    \begin{tabular}[t]{lll|c}
      \multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Year}}
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Winner}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Other}} 
         &\multicolumn{1}{c}{\tabulartext{Tallest?}} \\
      \hline
      1960 &J F Kennedy &R Nixon     &Y    \\
      1964 &L B Johnson &B Goldwater &Y    \\
      1968 &R Nixon     &H Humphrey  &Y    \\
      1972 &R Nixon     &G McGovern  &N    \\
      1976 &J Carter    &G Ford      &N    \\
      1980 &R Reagan    &J Carter    &Y    \\
      1984 &R Reagan    &W Mondale   &Y    \\
      1990 &G H W Bush  &M Dukakis   &Y    \\
      1992 &W Clinton   &G H W Bush  &--same--    \\
      1996 &W Clinton   &R Dole      &Y    \\
      2000 &G W Bush    &A Gore      &N    \\
      2004 &G W Bush    &J Kerry     &N    \\
      2008 &B Obama     &J McCain    &Y    \\
      2012 &B Obama     &M Romney    &N    \\
      2016 &D Trump     &H Clinton   &Y    \\
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{Height comparison of US Presidental winner and main opponent, 
             since 1900.} 
    \label{fig:USPresidentsHeights}
\end{figure}
\item Pseudocode describes solving a problem at a level of detail
that that lies between a program than an algorithm.
It typically includes at least a sketch of 
looping and other control structures, but not syntactical detail such 
as declaration of variables.
This is pseudocode for the recursive depth-first traversal
of a binary tree.
\begin{display}
  \begin{minipage}{0.4\textwidth}
\begin{lstlisting}
process_subtree(v):
  process_subtree(v's left child)
  process_vertex(v)
  process_subtree(v's right child)
\end{lstlisting}
  \end{minipage}
\end{display}
\item A Turing machine is more like a program than an algorithm, in that
it is an implementation of an algorithm on a specific hardware platform.
\item A flowchart is graphical.
It may include a range of detail, so that
in representing an algorithm,
it may contain a sketch of control structures
but it may also omit those details.
\item A process is an instance of a program that has been 
has been loaded into a machine for execution.
For instance, if we compile a program to solve the
\probname{Vertex to Vertex Path} problem and 
run it on hardware then we have an executing process.
Some processes, such as an operating system shell or a web server, are 
be designed to run forever, while
typically the term `algorithm' is used for something that is guaranteed to halt.
Thus, a web server may bundle together many routines that implement 
algorithms, each of which halts, but the larger bundle loops through
the subroutines forever. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  A.A. at \url{https://rjlipton.wordpress.com/2010/11/07/what-is-a-complexity-class/#comment-8872}
\end{credit}
So, if a problem is essentially a set of strings, 
what constitutes a solution?
\begin{answer}
A solution is typically an algorithm or computation that acts as the
characteristic function of the set.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
What is the difference between a decision problem and a language
decision problem?
\begin{answer}
A decision problem is any one that ends in a `yes' or `no' answer.
A language decision problem is a specialization of that, to
`yes' or `no' answers about membership in the given language.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As an illustration of the thesis that even surprising things 
can be represented reasonably efficiently and with reasonable fidelity 
in binary, we can do a simple calculation.
\begin{exparts*}
\item 
At~$30$~cm, the resolution of the human eye is about $0.01$~cm.
How many such pixels are there in a photograph that is 
$21$~cm by $30$~cm?
\item
We can see about a million colors.
How many bits per pixel is that?
\item How many bits for the photo, in total?
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
The multiplication is easy:
$(1/0.01)^2\cdot 21\cdot 30= \scinot{6.30}{6}$.
\item
The answer is $\ceiling{\lg(1\,000\,000)}$.
If all you have is a pocket calculator with a natural log function button,
then you can get $6\ln(10)/\ln(2)\approx 19.93$, so the answer is $20$.
\item Multiply $(\scinot{6.30}{6})\cdot 20=\scinot{1.26}{8}$.
That's less than $16$ megabytes, about eight books from 
Project Gutenberg.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
% sage: log(1000000,2)
% 6*log(10)/log(2)
% sage: log(1000000,2).n()
% 19.9315685693242
% sage: (1/0.01)*21*(1/0.01)*30
% 6.30000000000000e6
% sage: (1/0.01)*21*(1/0.01)*30*20
% 1.26000000000000e8
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Name something important that cannot be represented in binary.
\begin{answer}\verified
One is the smell of an baby's head.
They have a great smell.
But we don't compute it.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or false:~any two programs that implement the same algorithm 
must compute the same function.
What about the converse?
\begin{answer}\verified
  Yes, two programs that implement the same algorithm 
  must compute the same function.
  However, the converse does not hold;
  two programs computing the same function need not implement
  the same algorithm. 
  For instance, two programs may both sort input
  strings but one uses Bubble Sort while the other uses
  Quick Sort.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Some tasks are hard to express as a 
language decision problem.
Consider sorting the characters of a string
into ascending order.
Briefly describe why each of these language decision problems 
fails to capture the task's essential difficulty.
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is sorted}}$
\item $\set{\sequence{\sigma,p}\suchthat \text{$p$ is a permutation 
      that orders $\sigma$}}$
\end{exparts*}
\end{exercise}
%   % from https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/79729
%   Not every computational problem is naturally expressable as a 
%   language decision problem 
%   Consider the task of sorting the characters of strings
%   into ascending order.
%   We could try to express it as the language of sorted strings
%   $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ is sorted}}$. 
%   But this 
%   does not require that we find a good way 
%   to sort an unsorted input.
%   Another thought is to consider the language of pairs
%   $\sequence{\sigma,p}$ where $p$ is a permutation of the numbers
%   $0,\ldots \lh{\sigma}-1$ that brings the string into ascending order.
%   But here also the formulation 
%   seems to not capture the sorting problem, in that recognizing a
%   correct permutation feels different than generating one from scratch.
% }

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Sketch an algorithm for each language decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\lang_0=\set{\sequence{n,m}\in\N^2\suchthat
                  \text{$n+m$ is a square and one greater than a prime}}$
\item
$\lang_1=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots \str{9}}}
    \suchthat\text{$\sigma$ represents in decimal a multiple of $100$}}$
\item
$\lang_2=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat\text{$\sigma$ has more \str{1}'s than \str{0}'s}}$
\item $\lang_3=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \reversal{\sigma}=\sigma}$
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
These algorithms mark that input is accepted or not by printing~\str{1}
or \str{0}.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Input the sequence and unpack it to get $n,m\in\N$.
Then adds the two numbers.
If the sum is both square and one greater than a prime then print~\str{1},
and otherwise print~\str{0}.
\item
If the input is a single~\str{0}, or ends in two \str{0}'s, then print~\str{1}.
Otherwise print~\str{0}.
\item Scan the input string, keeping a running count of the number of
\str{0}'s and \str{1}'s.
At the end, if there are more \str{1}'s then print~\str{1},
otherwise print~\str{0}.
\item
Read and save the input string.
Then, compare the characters starting from the back with those starting
from the front.
If each matches (or the string is empty) then print~\str{1}.
Otherwise print~\str{0}. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Solve the language decision problem for
\begin{exparts*}
\item the empty language,\index{empty language!decision problem} 
\item the language~$\B$, 
and
\item the language~$\kleenestar{\B}$\!.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item The empty language is $\lang=\emptyset=\set{}$.
Its characteristic function is $\charfcn{\lang}(n)=0$.
The algorithm to solve this language decision problem is to, for all
input, return~$0$.
\item This language contains two strings, 
$\B=\set{\str{0},\str{1}}$ (technically, these are characters, but
we don't distinguish between characters and length-$1$ strings).
The characteristic function is this.
\begin{equation*}
  \charfcn{\B}(\sigma)=
  \begin{cases}
    1  &\case{if $\sigma=\str{0}$ or $\sigma=\str{1}$}  \\
    0  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
The algorithm that implements that function is easy.
\item The language $\kleenestar{\B}$ contains all bitstrings.
To solve the decision problem for this language, 
the algorithm returns~$1$ on all inputs. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language, sketch an algorithm that solves the language decision
problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
                  \text{$\sigma$ matches the regular expression \re{a*ba*}}}$
\item 
The language defined by this grammar
\begin{grammar}
S \produces{} AB 

A \produces{} \trm{a}A | \emptystring

B \produces{} \trm{b}B | \emptystring
\end{grammar} 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
For the algorithm, fix a Finite State machine~$\mathcal{M}$ that
recognizes the language described by \re{a*ba*}.
Now, read the input~$\sigma$
and simulate running~$\mathcal{M}$ on that input.
This will terminate in a finite number of steps.
If $\mathcal{M}$ ends in an accepting state then print~\str{1}
(or \str{yes}, or some other value signalling acceptance),
otherwise print~\str{0}.
\item
The grammar defines the language
$\lang=\set{\str{a}^nb^m\suchthat n,m\in\N}$.
The algorithm for that language is simple.
Input the string and if all of the \str{b}'s come after all of the \str{a}'s
then output \str{1} (or `yes', or whatever indicates acceptance).
Otherwise, output \str{0}.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Solve each decision problem about Finite State 
machines,~$\mathcal{M}$, by producing an algorithm.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given $\mathcal{M}$, decide if the language accepted by~$\mathcal{M}$
is empty.
\item Decide if the language accepted by~$\mathcal{M}$
is infinite. 
\item Decide if $\lang(\mathcal{M})$
is the set of all strings,~$\kleenestar{\Sigma}$. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
First observe that there is an easy algorithm that can,
given a machine~$\mathcal{M}$ and a state~$q$ in that machine,
find the set of states reachable from~$q$.
The algorithm proceeds in steps. 
At step~$1$, initialize by taking~$R_1$ to be the set of states that
are reachable from~$q$ in one transition.
Step~$i+1$ begins with the set of states that are 
reachable from~$q$ with $i$-many transitions or fewer.
In that step, for each state in~$R_i$, find if any states
reachable from it in one transition are not in~$R_i$.
Make the set~$R_{i+1}$ by adding them all to~$R_i$.
If there are no such new states then the algorithm stops.
Obviously it must stop at some point because the number of states in the
machine is finite.
\begin{exparts}
\item The language is empty if and only if from the start state,
no accepting state is reachable.
\item  % from SE user gnasher729, https://cs.stackexchange.com/a/106127/67754
The language is infinite if and only if 
there is a state~$q$ that can be reached from the initial state
such that $q$ can be reached from itself by a nonempty string, 
and some accepting state can be reached from~$q$.
\item Given $\mathcal{M}$,
obtain a new machine~$\hat{\mathcal{M}}$ that
recognizes the complement of $\lang(\mathcal{M})$ by 
copying~$\mathcal{M}$, except setting each accepting 
state of~$\mathcal{M}$ to be non-accepting in~$\hat{\mathcal{M}}$, 
and setting each non-accepting state of~$\mathcal{M}$ to be accepting
in~$\hat{\mathcal{M}}$.
Now, $\lang(\mathcal{M})$ is empty if and only if $\lang(\hat{\mathcal{M}})$
is empty.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
For each language decision problem, give an algorithm that runs in~$\bigOh(1)$.
\begin{exparts}
\item The language of minimal-length binary representations of numbers 
that are nonzero.
\item The binary representations of numbers that exceed~$1000$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item See if the input string starts with a~\str{1} bit.
\item See if the input string has length less than or equal to ten.
If the length equals ten then there are some cases to do but that 
doesn't change that the algorithm is $\bigOh(1)$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
In a graph, a 
\definend{bridge edge}\index{bridge}\index{graph!bridge edge}
is one whose removal disconnects the graph.
That is, there are two 
vertices that before the bridge is removed are connected by a path, but 
are not connected after it is removed.
(More precisely, a 
\definend{connected component}\index{connected component}\index{graph!connected component} 
of a graph is a set of vertices
that can be reached from each other by a path. 
A bridge edge is one whose removal increases the number of 
connected components.)
The problem is:~given a graph, find a bridge.
Is this a function problem, 
a decision problem, a language decision problem, a search
problem, or an optimization problem?
\begin{answer}
This is a search problem, since we need only produce a single bridge.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, give the categorization that best applies:~a function problem, 
a decision problem, a language decision problem, 
a search problem, or an optimization problem.
\begin{exparts*}
\item The \definend{\probname{Graph Connectedness}} problem,\index{Graph Connectedness@\probname{Graph Connectedness} problem}
which inputs a graph
and decides whether for any two vertices there is a path between them.
\item
The problem that inputs two natural numbers and returns their least
common multiple.
\item The 
\definend{\probname{Graph Isomorphism}} problem\index{Graph Isomorphism@\probname{Graph Isomorphism} problem} 
that inputs two graphs and determines
whether they are isomorphic.
\item The problem that takes in a propositional logic statement and 
returns an assignment of truth values to its inputs that makes
the statement true, if there is such an assignment.
\item
The \definend{\probname{Nearest Neighbor}} problem\index{Nearest Neighbor@\probname{Nearest Neighbor} problem} 
that inputs a weighted graph and a vertex, and 
returns a vertex nearest the given one that does not equal the
given one.
\item The 
\definend{\probname{Discrete Logarithm}}\index{Discrete Logarithm@\probname{Discrete Logarithm}} 
problem:~given 
a prime number~$p$ and two
numbers $a,b\in\N$, determine if there is a power~$k\in\N$ so that
$a^k\equiv b \pmod p$.
\item The problem that inputs a bitstring and decides if the number that it
represents in binary will, when converted to decimal, contain only odd digits.
% \item The problem that takes a string and decides if there is a person in
% the address book with that name.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
As remarked in the section, a decision problem involves finding a function\Dash
a Boolean function.
So in that sense, multiple answers are possible.
\begin{exparts}
\item This is a yes-or-no question,  
  so it is a decision problem.
\item Decision problem
\item Function problem.
\item Decision problem.
\item Search problem.
\item Decision problem.
\item Language decision problem,
for
$\lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
               \text{$\sigma$ represents a number that in decimal has only odd digits}}$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
For each, give the characterization that best applies:~a function problem, 
a decision problem, a language decision problem, a search
problem, or an optimization problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The \probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem,\index{3 Satisfiability@\probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem}\index{3 SAT@$3$-$\SAT$}
\cref{prob:3SAT}
\item The \probname{Divisor} problem, \cref{prob:Divisor}\index{Divisor@\probname{Divisor} problem}
\item The \probname{Prime Factorization} problem, \cref{prob:Factorization}\index{Factorization@\probname{Prime Factorization} problem}
\item The \prob[F\!-\!S\kern-1.25ptAT] problem,\index{F-SAT@\prob[F\negthinspace-\negthinspace S\kern-1.25pt AT] problem} 
  where the input is a propositional logic
  expression and the output is either an assignment of \true{} and~\false{}
  to the expression's variables that makes it evaluate to~\true{},
  or the string \str{None}.
\item The \probname{Composite} problem, \cref{prob:Composite}\index{Composite@\probname{Composite} problem}
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Decision problem
\item Search problem
\item Function problem
\item Function problem
\item Decision problem
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Express each task as a language decision problem.
Include in the description explicit mention of the string
representation.
\begin{exparts}
\item Decide whether a number is a perfect square.
\item Decide whether a triple $\sequence{x,y,z}\in\N^3$ is a 
   Pythagorean triple, that is, whether $x^2+y^2=z^2$.
\item Decide whether a graph has an even number of edges.
\item Decide whether a path in a graph has any repeated vertices.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item A language suited to Turing machines is  
$\lang_0=\set{\str{1}^n\suchthat \text{$n\in\N$ is a square}}$.
A language suited to more everyday machines is
$\lang_1=\set{d\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots{}\str{9}}}\suchthat
              \text{$d$ represents a perfect square in base~$10$}}$. 
\item 
One language is the set of triples
$\sequence{x,y,z}\in\kleenestar{\set{\str{0},\ldots~\str{9}}}$
such that $x,y,z$ represent integers in base~$10$ where 
the squares of the first two add to the square of the third.
\item 
A language is 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a graph $\mathcal{G}$ with an even number of edges}}$.
\item 
The language 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a pair $\sequence{\mathcal{G},p}$ where $p$ is a path with a repeated vertex}}$
will do.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Describe how to answer each as a language decision problem.
Include explicit mention of the string
representation.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Given a natural number, do its factors add to more than twice the number?
\item
Given a Turing machine and input, does the machine halt on the input
in less than ten steps?
\item 
Given a propositional logic statement, are there three different awssignments 
that evaluate to \true{}?
That is, are there more than three lines 
in the truth table that end in~\true{}?
\item 
Given a weighted graph and a bound~$B\in\R$, for any two vertices is there
a path from one to the other with total cost less than the bound?
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item $\set{n\in\N\suchthat\text{the factors of~$n$ sum to more than $2n$}}$.
\item $\set{\sequence{\TM,i}\suchthat\text{machine $\TM$ on input~$i$ halts in less than ten steps}}$
\item $\set{E\suchthat\text{the logic expression~$E$ can be satisfied by three distinct assignments}}$
\item $\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}}\suchthat\text{for all $v_0,v_1\in\mathcal{G}$ there is a path of cost less than~$B$}$
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:RecastAsDecision}
Recast each in language decision terms. 
Include explicit mention of the string
representation.
\begin{exparts*}
\item
\probname{Graph Colorability},\index{Graph Colorability@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem} 
  \cref{prob:GraphColorability},
\item
\probname{Euler Circuit},\index{Euler Circuit@\probname{Euler Circuit} problem} 
  \cref{prob:EulerCircuit},
\item
\probname{Shortest Path},\index{Shortest Path@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} 
  \cref{prob:ShortestPath}.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item 
The \probname{Graph Colorability} problem is stated as a decision problem.
Imagine that we had an algorithm 
to recognize membership in this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents 
      $\sequence{\mathcal{G},k}$, where $\mathcal{G}$ is $k$-colorable}}
\end{equation*}
Then, given an graph and an integer~$k$, we 
could use that algorithm to decide whether the graph is $k$-colorable.
Convert the graph and integer to a string representing 
the pair, and use the algorithm to decide if that pair is in the language.
\item Consider this.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a graph with a path that traverses each edge exactly once}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Euler Circuit} problem is given as a function problem.
If we had an algorithm 
to recognize membership in the language then, given an graph, we 
could use that algorithm to decide whether the graph has a circuit:
we just convert the graph to its representation and apply the algorithm.
If we know there is no Euler circuit then we are done.
If we know there is an Euler circuit then a search will find it.
\item 
The \probname{Shortest Path} problem is given as a function problem.
If we had an algorithm 
to recognize membership in the language
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents 
      $\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,p}$, where $p$ is a minimal-length path between the vertices}}
\end{equation*}
then, given an graph and two vertices, we 
could find the minimal path by using that algorithm to test 
all possible paths from one vertex 
to the other for minimality by seeing if the representation of the 
quad is in the language.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Restate the \HP{}\index{Halting problem@\HP!decision problem} 
as a language recognition problem.
\begin{answer}
It is a matter of recognizing members of this language.
$\set{\sequence{\TM_e,e}\suchthat e\in\N}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
As stated, the \probname{Shortest Path}\index{Shortest Path@\probname{Shortest Path} problem}
problem, \cref{prob:ShortestPath}, 
is an optimization problem.
Convert it into a parametrized family of decision problems.
\hint~use the technique outlined following the
\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem, \cref{ex:TravellingSalesman}.
\begin{answer}
Take $B\in\N$ as a bound and consider this family.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_{B}=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}\suchthat\text{There is a path from $v_0$ to $v_1$ of distance less than or equal to~$B$}}
\end{equation*}
Given a graph and two vertices we can solve the \probname{Shortest Path} 
problem for it
by searching through $\lang_1$, $\lang_2$, \ldots until we find the 
smallest bound~$B$ for which our triple is 
a member of the language~$\lang_{B}$. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Express each optimization problem as a 
parametrized family of language decision problems.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given a \probname{$15$ Game}\index{15 Game@\probname{$15$ Game} problem} 
board, find the least number of slides that will
solve it.
\item Given a Rubik's cube configuration, find the least number of moves
to solve it.
\item Given a list of jobs that must be accomplished to assemble a car,
along with how long each job takes and which jobs must be done before
other jobs, find the shortest time to finish the entire car.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Take~$B\in\N$ to be a bound and suppose that we can solve
this language decision problem, where $\mathcal{B}$ is a game board.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{B}\suchthat
               \text{$\mathcal{B}$ can be solved in fewer than $B$ slide moves}}
\end{equation*}
Given a board, 
to find the minimum number of moves, iterate through $B=0$, $B=1$, etc.,
looking to see if the given board is a member of~$\lang_B$.
Stop at the first~$B$ where it is in the language. 
That is the minimum.
\item Take~$B\in\N$ as a bound. 
Suppose that we have an algorithm to solve 
this language decision problem, where $\mathcal{R}$ is a Rubic's cube 
arrangement.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{R}\suchthat
               \text{$\mathcal{R}$ can be solved in fewer than $B$ cube moves}}
\end{equation*}
Given a cube, to find its minimum number of moves, 
iterate through $B=0$, $B=1$, etc., until a $B$ appears so that
the given cube is in~$\lang_B$.
That is the minumum number of moves.
\item Take~$B\in\N$. 
Suppose that we have an algorithm to solve 
this language decision problem, where $\mathcal{S}$ is a specification
a car's assembly jobs. 
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_B=\set{\mathcal{S}\suchthat
               \text{$\mathcal{S}$ can be accomplished in less time than $B$}}
\end{equation*}
Given a specification, find the minimum time by  
iterating through $B=0$, $B=1$, etc., until a $B$ appears so that
the give specification is in~$\lang_B$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
As stated, the 
\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}\index{Hamiltonian Circuit@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}}
problem is a decision problem.
Give a function version of this problem.
Also give an optimization version.
\begin{answer}
A function version would input a graph and
output a circuit (if there is no circuit then it would output something
like the string \str{None}).

An optimization version would return a circuit that is best by some
criterion.
For instance, in a weighted graph it would return the circuit
of least weight; this is the \probname{Travelling Salesman} problem.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The different problem types are related.
Each of these inputs a square matrix~$M$
with more than $3$~rows, and relates to a $3\times 3$ submatrix 
(form the submatrix by picking three rows and three columns, which need
not be adjacent).
Characterize each as a function problem, 
a decision problem, a search
problem, or an optimization problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item Find a submatrix that is invertible.
\item Decide if there is an invertible submatrix.
\item Return a submatrix 
that is invertible, or the string `\str{None}'.
\item Return a submatrix 
whose determinant has the largest absolute value.
\end{exparts}
Also give a language for an associated language decision problem.
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Search problem
\item Decision problem
\item Function problem
\item Optimization problem
\end{exparts}
A language suitable for an associated language problem is
$\set{M\suchthat \text{$M$ has a $3\times 3$ invertable submatrix}}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Convert each function problem to a matching decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item The problem that inputs two natural numbers and returns their
product.
\item The \probname{Nearest Neighbor}\index{Nearest Neighbor@\probname{Nearest Neighbor} problem} 
problem,
that inputs a weighted graph and a vertex and
returns the vertex nearest the given one, but not equal to it. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Given a triple $\sequence{n_0,n_1,p}\in\N^3$, decide if $p=n_0\cdot n_1$.
\item Given a triple
$\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1}$, where $\mathcal{G}$ is a weighted graph 
and $v_0\neq v_1$ are vertices in that graph,
decide if $v_1$ is a vertex that is as
near as possible to~$v_0$, 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The \definend{\probname{Linear Programming}} 
problem\index{Linear Programming language decision problem@\probname{Linear Programming} language decision problem}
starts with a list of linear inequalities
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n-1}x_{n-1}\leq b_i$ for $a_0,\ldots{}a_{n-1},b_i\in\Q$  
and it looks for a sequence $\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_{n-1}}\in\Q^n$
that is feasible, 
in that substituting the number $s_j$ for the variable $x_i$'s makes each
inequality true.
Give a version that is a
\begin{exparts*}
\item language decision problem,
\item search problem,
\item function problem,
and
\item optimization problem.
(For some parts there is more than one sensible answer.)
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Use the language
  $\set{\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_{n-1}}\in\Q^n\suchthat 
      \text{$a_{i,0}s_0+\cdots+a_{i,n-1}s_{n-1}\leq b_i$ for all $i$}}$.
\item It is a search problem as it is stated.
  We need only find one feasible sequence, if there is one.
\item The function could produce one feasible sequence, if one exists.
\item To make it an optimization function, the natural thing is to 
  optimize a linear function 
  $P(x_0,\ldots{} x_{n-1})=c_0x_0+\cdots+c_{n-1}x_{n-1}$,
  for some $c_0,\ldots{}c_{n-1}\in\Q$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
An \definend{independent set} in a graph is a collection of vertices
such that no two are connected by an edge.
Give a version of the problem of finding an 
independent set\index{Independent Set@\probname{Independent Set} problem} 
that is a
\begin{exparts*}
\item a decision problem,
\item language decision problem,
\item search problem,
\item function problem,
and
\item optimization problem.
\end{exparts*}
(For some parts there is more than one reasonable answer.)
\begin{answer}
A set with one vertex is independent, as is the empty set.
So every graph has a subset of its vertices that is independent.
Because of that, these answers at least state a version for
some number of vertices $k\in\N$.
\begin{exparts}
\item Given a graph, decide if it has an independent set with $k$~vertices. 
\item Use the language
  $\set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat
        \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has $k$ vertices that do not share an edge}}$.
\item Given a graph~$G$, find an independent set with $k$-many vertices.
  (There may be more than one, or none, but we only look for one.)
\item Given a graph, return an independent set of size~$k$
  or the string \str{None}.
\item Given a graph, return an independent set that is maximal, in that
  it is as large as possible for the graph.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Give an example of a problem 
where the decision variant is solvable quickly,
but the search variant is not.
\begin{answer}
Ever list of numbers has a maximum, but finding it appears to mean 
at least looking through the list.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Let
$\lang_{F}=\set{\sequence{n,B}\in\N^2\suchthat
  \text{there is an $m\in\set{1,\ldots\,B}$ that divides $n$}}$
and consider its language decision problem.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that $\sequence{d,B}\in\lang_{F}$ if and only if $B$ is greater
than or equal to the least prime factor of $d$.
\item
Conclude that you can use a solution to the language recognition problem
to solve the search problem of, given a number, returning a
prime factor of that number.
\end{exparts}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show how to use an algorithm that solves the 
\probname{Shortest Path}\index{Shortest Path@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} 
problem
to solve the 
\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path@\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem} 
problem.
How to use it on graphs that are not weighted?
\begin{answer}
Imagine that we have a routine \texttt{shortest\_path} that takes in a 
weighted graph and two vertices, and returns the shortest path between them.
That also solves the decision problem of whether there is any path at all.

If the graph is not weighted, convert it to a weighted graph by 
taking each edge to be of weight~$1$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that with an algorithm that quickly 
solves the 
\probname{Subset Sum}\index{Subset Sum@\probname{Subset Sum} problem} 
problem,
\cref{prob:SubsetSumDecision}, we can quickly solve the associated function
problem of finding the subset.
\begin{answer}
By taking the numbers in the set $S=\set{s_0,\ldots\,s_{n-1}}$ one at a 
time:~$S_0=\set{s_0}$, then $S_1=\set{s_0,s_1}$, \ldots\,,
you can figure out from the decision problem solver
what is the largest number~$s_j$ in the sum.
Subtract that from the goal and then iterate using $S-\set{s_j}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Show how to use an algorithm that solves 
\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path@\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path} problem}
problem to solve the 
\probname{Graph Connectedness}\index{Graph Connectedness@\probname{Graph Connectedness} problem} 
problem, 
which inputs a graph
and decides whether that graph is connected, so that
for any two vertices there is a path between them.
\begin{answer}
Check all pairs of vertices.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}
\end{exercises}






% =======================================
\section{$\P$}\index{P@$\P$|(}
Recall that we usually are not careful to 
distinguish between a language~$\lang$ 
and the problem of deciding which strings are in that language.

%<*def:ComplexityClass>
\begin{definition}\label{def:ComplexityClass}
A
\citetext{\definend{complexity class}}{%
  There are various definitions, which are related but not equivalent.
  Some authors fold in the requirement that that a class be associated
  with some resource specification.
  This has some implications because
  if an author, for instance, requires that each class be problems that
  are somehow solvable by Turing machines then each class is countable.
  Our definition is more general and does not imply that 
  a class is countable.  
}\index{complexity class}\index{class}\index{class!complexity}
is a collection of languages.
\end{definition}
The term `complexity' is there because the collections
are often associated with some resource specification, 
so that a
class consists of the languages that are accepted by a 
mechanical computer whose use of some resource fits the 
specification.\footnote{There are other definitions of complexity class.
  Some authors make it a requirement that in a class the 
  languages can be computed
  under some resource specification.
  This has implications\protect\Dash if all of the members of a class 
  must be computable
  by Turing machines then each class is countable.
  Here, we only say that it is a collection, so 
  our definition is maximally general.}
%</def:ComplexityClass>

\begin{example}
One complexity class is 
\!the collection of languages for which there is an deciding Turing machine
that runs in time $\bigOh(n^{2})$.
That is, 
$\mathcal{C}=\set{\lang_0,\lang_1, \ldots}$, where 
each $\lang_j$ is decided by some machine~$\TM_{i_j}$,
which, when given input~$\sigma$, finishes within $\sizeof{\sigma}^{2}$
steps.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Another is the collection of languages accepted by Turing machines 
that use only in logarithmic space.
So,
when the accepting machine gets input~$\sigma$ 
then it halts after
visiting a number of tape squares 
less than or equal to~$\lg(\sizeof{\sigma})$.  
\end{example}

Some points bear development.
As to the computing machine, 
researchers study not just Turing machines but other types of machines 
as well,  
including nondeterministic Turing machines,
and Turing machines with access to an oracle for random numbers.
The resource specification often 
involve bounds on 
\citetext{the time and space behavior}{%
  We will concentrate our attention resource bounds in the range from
  logarithmic and exponential, because these are the most useful 
  for understanding problems that arise in practice.
  % https://cse.buffalo.edu/~regan/papers/pdf/ALRch27.pdf
  }.
But they could instead be, for instance, 
the complement of $\bigOh(n^2)$, so it isn't always a bound.\footnote{%
    At this writing there are 545 studied classes but the number 
   changes frequently;
   see the Complexity Zoo, 
   \protect\Cref{sec:ComplexityZoo}.\protect\index{Complexity Zoo}}



\subsection{Definition} 
The complexity class that we introduce now  
is the most important one.
It is the collection of language decision problems 
that under Cobham's Thesis we take to be tractable. 

%<*def:P>
\begin{definition}\label{def:P}
A problem is a member of the class 
\definend{$\P$}\index{P@$\P$}\index{polytime}\index{polynomial time}\index{complexity class!polytime}\index{complexity class!P@$\P$} 
if there is an algorithm for it that on a deterministic Turing machine runs in
polynomial time.
\end{definition}
%</def:P>

\begin{example}
One problem that is a member of~$\P$ is that of deciding whether
a given graph is connected.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\mathcal{G}\suchthat \text{for any two vertices $v_0,v_1\in\mathcal{G}$, thre is a path from one to the other}}
\end{equation*}
To verify this, we must produce an algorithm that decides membership
in this language, and that runs in polynomial time.
The natural approach is to do a breadth first search or depth first search
of the graph.
The runtime of both is bounded by 
$\bigOh(\sizeof{\mathcal{N}}^3)$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Another is the problem of deciding whether two natural numbers 
are relatively prime.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sequence{n_0,n_1}\in\N^2\suchthat\text{the greatest common divisor is~$1$}}
\end{equation*}
Again, to verify that this language is a member of~$\P$ we produce 
an algorithm that determines membership, and that runs in polytime.
Euclid's algorithm solve this problem, with runtime 
$\bigOh(\lg(\max(n_0,n_1)))$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Still another problem in~$\P$ is the 
\probname{String Search} problem,\index{String Search problem@\probname{String Search} problem} 
to decide substring-ness.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sequence{\sigma,\tau}\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ is a substring of~$\tau$}}
\end{equation*}
Often $\tau$ is very long and is called the
\definend{haystack}\index{haystack} 
while $\sigma$ is short and is the \definend{needle}.
The algorithm that 
first tests $\sigma$ at the initial character of~$\tau$, 
then at the next character, etc., 
has a runtime of $\bigOh(\sizeof{\sigma}\cdot\sizeof{\tau})$, which 
is~$\bigOh(\max(\sizeof{\sigma},\sizeof{\tau})^2)$.
\end{example}

% Those examples are collections of very different things.
% They have in common that
% the members of $\P$ are problems, essentially languages,
% and for each of which there is a decision algorithm that is sufficiently fast.

\begin{example}
A \definend{circuit}\index{circuit} 
is a directed acyclic graph.
Each 
vertex, called a \definend{gate},\index{gate}\index{circuit!gate} 
is labelled with a two input/one output
Boolean function.
The only exception is that the vertices on the left are the 
\definend{input gates}  
that provide source bits, $b_0,b_1,b_2, b_3\in\B$.
Edges are called \definend{wires}.\index{wire}\index{circuit!wire} 
Below, $\wedge$ is the boolean function `and', $\vee$ is `or',
$\oplus$ is `exclusive or', and $\equiv$ is the negation of
`exclusive or', which returns~\str{1} if and only if the two inputs bits are
the same.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/circuit/circuit005.pdf}
\end{center}
This circuit returns \str{1} if the sum of the input bits is a multiple
of~$3$.
The 
\probname{Circuit Evaluation}\index{Circuit Evaluation problem@\probname{Circuit Evaluation} problem} 
problem inputs a circuit like this one
and computes the output, $f(b_0,b_1,b_2,b_3)$.
This problem is a member of~$\P$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Although polytime is a restriction, nonetheless $\P$ is a very large
collection.
% It contains all problems with a practical solution algorithm. 
More example members:
\begin{parenum} 
% \item determine if a number is prime,
% $\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \text{$\sigma$ represents a prime}}$,
\item matrix multiplication, taken as a language decision problem for
$\set{\sequence{\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_2}\suchthat\text{they represent matrices with $M_0\cdot M_1=M_2$}}$
\item minimal spanning tree,
  $\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},T}\suchthat\text{$T$ is a minimal spanning tree in~$\mathcal{G}$}}$
\item edit distance, the number of
single-character removals, insertions, or substitutions needed to transform
between strings,
  $\set{\sequence{\sigma_0,\sigma_1,n}\suchthat \text{$\sigma_0$ transforms to $\sigma_1$ in at most $n$ edits}}$.
\end{parenum}
\end{example}

% This shows the class~$\P$ as a subset of the collection of all languages.
\begin{fig}[]{This shows all language
decision problems, all $\lang\subseteq\kleenestar{\B}$.
Shaded is~$\P$.
The shading is in layers to depict that 
$\P$ contains problems with a solution algorithm  
that is $\bigOh(1)$,
problems with an algorithm that is $\bigOh(n)$,
ones with a $\bigOh(n^2)$ algorithm, etc.}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp02.pdf}}
\end{fig}

Two final observations.
First, if a problem is solved by an algorithm that is $\bigOh(\lg n)$
then that problem is in~$\P$.
Second, the members of~$P$ are problems, so 
it is wrong to say that an algorithm is in~$\P$.



\subsection{Effect of the model of computation}
A problem is in~$\P$ if it has an algorithm that is polytime.
But algorithms are based on an underlying computing model.
Is membership in~$\P$ dependent on the model that we use?

In particular, our experience with Turing machines
gives the sense that they involve a lot of tape moving.
So we may expect that algorithms directed at Turing machine hardware
are slow.
However,
close analysis with a wide range of alternative computational models
proposed over the years shows that while
Turing machine algorithms 
are often slower than related algorithms for other natural models,
it is only by a factor of between $n^2$ and~$n^4$.\footnote{%
  We take a model to be `natural' if it
  was not invented in order to be a counterexample to this.}
That is, if we have a problem for which there is a
$\bigOh(n)$ algorithm on
another model then we may find that on a Turing machine
model it is $\bigOh(n^3)$, or $\bigOh(n^4)$, or~$\bigOh(n^5)$.
So it is still in~$\P$.

A variation of Church's thesis, the 
\definend{Extended Church's Thesis},\index{Extended Church's Thesis}\index{Church's Thesis!Extended} 
posits that not only are
all reasonable models of mechanical computation of equal power,
but in addition that they are of equivalent speed in that we can simulate any
reasonable model of computation\footnote{%
  One definition of `reasonable' is ``in principle physically realizable''
  \autocite{BernsteinVazirani}.} in polytime on a
probabilistic Turing machine.\footnote{%
  A Turing machine with a random oracle.}
Under the extended thesis, 
a problem that falls in the class~$\P$ using Turing machines
also falls in that class using any other natural models.
(Note, however, that this thesis does not enjoy anything like
the support of the original Church's Thesis.
Also, we know of several problems, 
including the familiar \probname{Prime Factorization} problem,
that under the Quantum Computing\index{Quantum Computing} 
model have algorithms with polytime solutions,
but for which we do not know of any polytime solution in a
non-quantum model.
So the Quantum Computing model
would provide a counterexample to the thesis, if we can produce physical 
devices matching that model.)

\begin{remark}
Breaking news!
Recently engineers at Google claimed to have
achieved 
\definend{Quantum Supremacy},\index{Quantum Supremacy}\index{Quantum computing!quantum supremacy} 
to have solved a problem
using an algorithm running on a physical quantum computer that 
is not known to be solvable on a Turing machine or RAM machine in
\citetext{less than centuries}{%
  See the video from Google at 
  \protect\url{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-ZNEzzDcllU}
  % \href{https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-ZNEzzDcllU}{this YouTube video from Google} 
  and 
  S~Aaronson's Quantum Supremacy FAQ at
  \protect\url{https://www.scottaaronson.com/blog/?p=4317}.
}.

Now, there are reservations.
For one,  
\citetext{the claim is the subject of scholarly controversy}{%
  See the posting from IBM Research at
  \protect\url{https://www.ibm.com/blogs/research/2019/10/on-quantum-supremacy/}
  and
  G~Kalai's Quantum Supremecy Skepticism FAQ at
  \protect\url{https://gilkalai.wordpress.com/2019/11/13/gils-collegial-quantum-supremacy-skepticism-faq/}.
  % \url{https://gilkalai.wordpress.com/2019/09/23/quantum-computers-amazing-progress-google-ibm-and-extraordinary-but-probably-false-supremacy-claims-google/}
}.
For another, on its face, 
this is not general purpose computing;~the problem solved
is exotic.
Whether quantum computers will ever be practical physical devices 
used for everyday problems is not 
at this moment clear, 
although scientists and engineers are making
great progress.
For our purposes we put this aside, but we will
watch events with great interest.
\end{remark}




  

\subsection{Naturalness}
\citetext{We will give the class~$\P$ a lot of attention}{%
  This discussion gained much from the material in 
  \autocite{AllenderLouiRegan}.
  This includes several direct quotations.}
because there are reasons to think that 
it is the collection  
that best captures the notion of problems that have a feasible 
solution.

The first reason echos the prior subsection.
There are many models of computation,
including Turing machines, RAM machines, and Racket programs.
All of them compute the same set of functions as Turing machines, 
by Church's Thesis, but they do so at different speeds.
However, while the speeds differ, all these models
run within polytime of each other.\footnote{%
  All of the non-quantum natural models.} 
That makes $\P$ invariant under the choice of
computing model:~if a problem is in~$\P$ for any model
then it is in~$\P$ for all of these models.
The fact that Turing machines are our standard is in some ways a 
historical accident, but differences between the runtime behavior of
any of these models is lost in the general polynomial sloppiness.

Another reason that $\P$ is a natural class
is that we'd like that if two things, $f$ and~$g$, are easy to 
compute then a simple combination of the two is also easy.
%<*discussion:PClosure>
More precisely, fix total functions $\map{f,g}{\N}{\N}$ and consider
these.
\begin{equation*}
  \prob_f=\set{\strng(\sequence{n,f(n)})\in\kleenestar{\B}^2\suchthat n\in\N}
  \qquad
  \prob_g=\set{\strng(\sequence{n,g(n)})\in\kleenestar{\B}^2\suchthat n\in\N}
\end{equation*}
(Recall that $\strng(\ldots)$ means that we represent 
the numbers as bitstrings).
With that recasting, $\P$ is closed under function addition,
scalar multiplication by an integer,
subtraction, multiplication, and composition.
It is also closed under language concatenation,
and the Kleene star operator.
It is the smallest nontrivial class with these appealing closure properties.
%</discussion:PClosure>

But the main reason that $\P$ is our candidate is Cobham's Thesis, the
contention that the formalization of  
`tractable problem' should be that it has a solution algorithm that runs 
in polynomial time.
We discussed this on page~\pageref{discussion:CobhamsThesis}; 
a person may object
that polytime is too broad a class to capture this idea because 
a problem whose solution algorithm cannot be improved  
below a runtime of~$\bigOh(n^{1\,000\,000})$
is really not feasible or tractable.
% \footnote{%
%   Even if the argument is right that $\P$ is too broad, 
%   it would still be a relevant and useful class because showing that a 
%   problem is not in~$\P$ would show that no
%   algorithm for its solution is practical.
%   % (This is like in the first and second chapter where we assumed considered
%   % Turing machine computations that are unbounded.
%   % Showing that something is not solvable even for an unbounded computation
%   % shows it is not solvable within bounds.)  
%   }
Further, using diagonalization we can produce such problems.
However, the problems produced in that way are artificial, and 
empirical experience over close to a century of computing 
is that problems with solution algorithms of 
very large degree polynomial time complexity
do not seem to arise in practice.
We see plenty of problems with solution algorithms that are $\bigOh(n\lg n)$,
or $\bigOh(n^3)$, and we see plenty of problems that are exponential,
but we just do not see~$\bigOh(n^{1\,000\,000})$.

Moreover,
when a researcher has produced an algorithm for a problem with 
a runtime that is even a moderately large degree polynomial,
what has happened is that, with this foot in the door,
over the next few years the community brings to bear  
an array of mathematical and algorithmic techniques that 
bring the runtime degree down to reasonable size.

Even if the objection to Cobham's Thesis is right and $\P$ is too broad, 
it would nonetheless still be useful because if we could show that a 
problem is not in~$\P$ then we would have shown that it has no
solution algorithm that is practical.\footnote{%
  This argument has lost some of its force in recent years with the
  rise of $\SAT$ solvers.
  These algorithms attack problems believed to not be in~$\P$,
  and can solve instances of the problems of moderately large size,
  using only moderately large computing resources.
  See \protect\cref{extra:Tractability}.
}
(This is like in the first and second chapter where we considered
Turing machine computations that are unbounded.
Showing that something is not solvable even for an unbounded computation
also shows that it is not solvable within bounds.)  

So Cobham's Thesis, to this point, has held up.
Insofar as theory should be a guide for practice, this is a compelling
reason to use~$\P$ as a benchmark for other complexity classes.


\begin{exercises}
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
True or False: if the language is finite then the language decision problem 
is in $\P\mkern-1mu$.
\begin{answer}\verified
True.
Such problems have solution algorithms that are basically a fixed number 
of \codeinline{if ... then ...} branches, so there is
a maximum number of steps that any input can trigger. 
Thus, the solution algorithm is $\bigOh(1)$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your coworker says something mistaken, ``I've got a problem
whose algorithm is in~$\P\mkern-1mu$.''
They are being a little sloppy with terms; how?
\begin{answer}\verified
Algorithms are not in $\P$; rather, problems are in~$\P\mkern-1mu$.
Specifically, a problem is in~$\P$ if from among all
the algorithms that solves it, at least one runs in polynomial time,
is $\bigOh{n^c}$ for some constant~$c$.
So, better is to say something like, ``I've got a problem 
whose solution algorithm takes polytime.''
(It is absolutely right that 
we are not too careful to distinguish between problems, meaning language
decision problems, and languages. 
But problems and algorithms are very different.)
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
What is the difference between  an order of growth and a complexity class?
\begin{answer}\verified
An order of growth is a set of functions.
A complexity class is a set of problems, language decision problems.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Your friend says to you, 
``I think that the \probname{Circuit Evaluation} problem takes exponential
time.
There is a final vertex.
It takes two inputs, which come from two vertices, and each of those
take two inputs, etc., so that a five-deep circuit can have
thirty two vertices.''
Help them see where they are wrong.
\begin{answer}\verified
Your friend is observing that the circuit representation may have length
exponential in the circuit depth, 
since an $r$-deep circuit may have $2^r$~many vertices.
But the definition of polynomial time is that the number of steps taken 
is a polynomial function of the size of the input representation.
If the representation is very large then
that only means that the polynomial receives a large input.
That is, we are not comparing the time taken to the depth of the
circuit, but rather to the size of the representation.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  \url{https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/57518}
\end{credit}
In class, someone says to the professor, 
``Why aren't all languages in~$\P$ according to your definition?
I'll design a Turing machine~$\TM$ so that no matter what the input is, 
it outputs~$1$.
It only needs one step, so it is polytime for sure.''
Perhaps you are 
\citetext{not able rightly to apprehend}{%
  This refers to a time when C~Babbage was looking for money
  to build a mechanical computer and was asked in a parlimentary committee,
   ``Pray, Mr. Babbage, if you put into the machine wrong figures, will the right answers come out?'' and he said,
   ``I am not able rightly to apprehend the kind of confusion of ideas that could provoke such a question.''
  Babbage for the win.} 
the kind of confusion of 
ideas that could provoke such a question, but 
give a calm explanation of how it is mistaken.
\begin{answer}\verified
The student's machine accepts all strings.
The definition requires a machine that only accept strings in the language.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false:~if a language is accepted by a machine then 
its complement is also accepted by a machine.
\begin{answer}\verified
True, although not by the same machine.
Let $\lang$ be computed by~$\TM\!$, so that the machine 
has this input-output behavior.
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{\TM}(\sigma)=\charfcn{\lang}(\sigma)=
  \begin{cases}
    1  &\case{if $\sigma\in\lang$}  \\
    0  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Modify the machine by interchanging the output $0$'s and $1$'s, 
to get a new machine~$\hat{\TM}$ with the complementary behavior. 
\begin{equation*}
  \TMfcn_{\hat{\TM}}(\sigma)=\charfcn{\setcomp{\lang}}(\sigma)=
  \begin{cases}
    0  &\case{if $\sigma\in\lang$}  \\
    1  &\case{otherwise}
  \end{cases}
\end{equation*}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the decision problem for
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat\text{$\sigma=\tau^3$ for some $\tau\in\kleenestar{\B}$}}$
is in~$\P\mkern-1mu$.
\begin{answer}\verified
We must produce an algorithm that runs in polytime.
Given the input~$\sigma$, find its length.
If the length is not divisible by three then return~$0$.
Otherwise, find the substring~$\tau$ that is the first third of~$\sigma$,
and check whether $\tau\concat\tau\concat\tau=\sigma$.
If so, return~$1$, and if not, return~$0$.   
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that the language of palindromes, 
$\set{\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat \sigma=\reversal{\sigma}}$,
is in~$\P\mkern-1mu$.
\begin{answer}\verified
The algorithm is straightforward.
Given~$\sigma$, for $i\in\set{0,1,\ldots{}\sizeof{\sigma}-1}$,
read the characters $\sigma[i]$ and compare them to the corresponding character
read from the back, $\sigma[(\sizeof{\sigma}-1)-i]$.
(You can stop once you get through the halfway point, 
$i=\floor{\sizeof{\sigma}/2}$.)
If each comparison gives equality then return~$1$,
else return~$0$.
This algorithm is polynomial since it is basically a loop. 
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise}
% Prove that this problem is in~$\P$:~the 
% problem of deciding if two natural numbers are relatively prime.
% \begin{answer}\verified
% First, we can take this problem to be the language decision problem for
