The \probname{Double-$\SAT$}\index{Double-SAT problem@\probname{Double-$\SAT$} problem} 
problem inputs a propositional logic statement and
decide whether it has at least two different substitutions of Boolean values
that make it true.
\item
The
\probname{Subset Sum} problem\index{Subset Sum problem@\probname{Subset Sum} problem}
inputs a set of numbers $S\subset\N$ and a target sum~$T\in\N$,
and decides whether least one subset of~$S$ adds to~$T$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item This is the language~$\lang$.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sigma\suchthat
             \text{$\sigma$ represents a Propositional Logic statement
                   where at least two lines of the truth tables
                   end in \true}}
\end{equation*}
These are suitable fill-ins:
\begin{parenum}
\item a Propositional Logic statement,
\item a pointer to two lines of the truth table,
\item for those two truth table lines, the statement returns \true,
\item two truth table lines that cause the expression~$\sigma$ 
  to evaluate to \true,
and
\item pair of truth table lines.
\end{parenum}
\item
Here is the problem cast as the decision problem for a language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{S,T}\suchthat
     \text{a subset of $A\subseteq S$ has $\sum_{a\in A}a=T$}}
\end{equation*}
These are fill-ins:
\begin{parenum}
\item a set and number pair $\sequence{S,T}$,
\item a set of numbers,
\item that is a subset of the given~$S$ and that its elements sum to~$T$, 
\item a subset of~$S$ whose elements sum to~$T$,
and
\item subset of~$S$.
\end{parenum}
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
In the British TV game show 
\citetext{\textit{Countdown}}{%
  See \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countdown_(game_show)}.}
that is 
\citetext{quite popular}{%
  See also \url{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8_Out_of_10_Cats_Does_Countdown}.}, 
players are given six numbers from
$S=\set{1,2,\ldots{} 10, 25, 50, 75, 100}$ (numbers may be repeated), 
along with a target integer~$T\in\closed{100}{999}$.
They must construct an arithmetic expression that evaluates to the target,
using the given numbers at most once.
The expression can involve addition, subtraction, 
multiplication, and division
(the division must have no remainder).
Show that the decision problem for this language 
\begin{equation*}
  \prob[CD]=
  \set{\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_5,T}\in S^6\times I
    \suchthat \text{an allowed combination of the $s_i$ gives $T$}}
\end{equation*}
is in $\NP$.
Use \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.
\begin{answer}\verified
\cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}
requires that we product a deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
It must input pairs of the form $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma=\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_5,T}$.
It must have the property that if
$\sigma\in\prob[CD]$ then there is a~$\omega$ such that~$\TM[V]$  
accepts the input, while if $\sigma\notin\prob[CD]$ then there is no 
such witness~$\omega$.
And it must run in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

The witness~$\omega$ is an arithmetic expression (or, more precisely,
a string representation of an arithmetic expression) that evaluates
to the target~$T$ and that involves a subset of the six 
numbers~$s_0$, \ldots{} $s_5$, each used at most once.
The verifier checks that the expression does indeed evaluate to the target,
and does indeed use each of the six numbers at most once.
Clearly those checks can be done in polytime.

If 
$\sigma\in\prob[CD]$ then by definition there is an
arithmetic expression, and so a witness~$\omega$ exists 
that will cause~$\TM[V]$ to 
accept the input.
If $\sigma\notin\prob[CD]$ then there is no such arithmetic expression,
and therefore no witness~$\omega$ will cause~$\TM[V]$ to accept.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Recall that we recast \probname{Travelling Salesman} optimization problem
as a language decision problem for a family of languages.
Show that each such language 
is in $\NP$ by applying \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv},
sketching a verifier that works with a suitable witness.
\begin{answer}\verified
We recast the \probname{Travelling Salesman} problem from an optimization 
problem by using bounds~$B\in\N$ to get this 
parametrized set of bitstrings.
\begin{equation*}
  \TS_B=\set{G\in\kleenestar{\B}\suchthat
    \text{$G$ is a weighted graph with a circuit of length less than~$B$}}
\end{equation*}

To show that this problem is in~$\NP$,
\cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} requires that we produce a 
deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
It must input pairs of the form $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma$ is a graph,~$\mathcal{G}$ 
(technically, a bitsting representation of a graph).
The verifier must have the property that if
$\sigma\in\TS_B$ then there is a~$\omega$ such that~$\TM[V]$  
accepts the input, while if $\sigma\notin\TS_B$ then there is no 
such~$\omega$.
This verifier must run in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

For a witness we can use a circuit 
$\omega=\sequence{v_{i_0},v_{i_1},\ldots v_{i_k}}$ that
is of total cost less than the bound.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ where 
$\sigma$ is a weighted graph, and checks 
that $\omega$ is indeed a Hamiltonian circuit of the graph, and that it 
costs less than the bound.
A verifier can do those checks in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.
If the graph $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\TS_B$ then there is 
such an~$\omega$, so there is a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
that the verifier can accept.
If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\TS_B$ then there is no such 
$\omega$, and so the verifier will never accept such a pair.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The problem of \probname{Independent Sets} starts with a graph and 
a natural number~$n$ and
decides whether in the graph there are $n$-many independent vertices,
that is, vertices that are not connected.
State it as a language decision problem, and 
use \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} to show that this problem is in~$\NP$.
\begin{answer}\verified
The \probname{Independent Sets} problem 
is the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=
  \set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},n}\suchthat
       \text{the graph~$\mathcal{G}$ has at least $n$ independent vertices}}
\end{equation*}

To show that this language decision problem is in~$\NP$,
\cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} requires that we produce a 
deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
This verifier must input pairs of the form $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma$ is a graph-natural number pair,~$\sequence{\mathcal{G},n}$. 
The verifier must satisfy that if
$\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a~$\omega$ such that~$\TM[V]$  
accepts the input, while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no 
such~$\omega$.
This verifier must run in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

For a witness, take a size-$n$ set of independent vertices from the graph,
$\omega=\sequence{v_0,\ldots{}v_{n-1}}$.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ where 
$\sigma$ is a graph, and checks 
that the vertices are in the graph and that no two of them are connected.
Those checks take time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$
(obviously, this depends on the graph being represented with 
reasonable efficiency, but we always make that assumption).
If the pair $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\lang$ then there is 
such a witness~$\omega$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
that the  verifier can accept.
If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\lang$ then there is no such 
$\omega$, and so there does not exist a pair that the 
verifier will accept.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Use \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} to show that the
\probname{Knapsack} problem\index{Knapsack problem@\probname{Knapsack} problem} 
is in~$\NP$.
\begin{answer}\verified
The \probname{Knapsack} problem 
is the decision problem for this language, $\lang$.
\begin{equation*}
  \set{\sequence{(w_0,v_0),\ldots{} (w_{n-1},v_{n-1}),B,T}\suchthat
       \text{there is $I=\set{i_0,\ldots{} i_k}\subseteq\set{0,\ldots{} n-1}$
             so $\sum_{i\in I}w_i\leq B$ and $\sum_{i\in I}v_i\geq T$}}
\end{equation*}

To show that this language decision problem is in~$\NP$,
\cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} requires that we produce a 
deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
The verifier input pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma=\sequence{(w_0,v_0),\ldots{} (w_{n-1},v_{n-1}),B,T}$. 
The verifier must have the property that if
$\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a~$\omega$ such that~$\TM[V]$  
accepts the input, while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no 
such~$\omega$.
This verifier must also run in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

For a witness, take a set
$\omega=\set{i_0,\ldots{}i_k}\subseteq\set{0,\ldots{}n-1}$.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ and checks 
that the sum of the weights of the indicated pairs, 
$\sum_{i\in \omega}w_i$,  is less than 
or equal to the bound~$B$,
and also that the sum of the values of pairs, $\sum_{i\in \omega}v_i$,  
is greater than 
or equal to the target~$T$.
Those checks clearly take time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.

If $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\lang$ then there is such a
selection of indices, so there is
such a witness~$\omega$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
that the  verifier can accept.
If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\lang$ then there is no such 
$\omega$, and so there does not exist a pair that the 
verifier will accept.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
True or false?
For the
language~$\set{\sequence{a,b,c}\in\N^3\suchthat a+b=c}$,
the problem of deciding membership 
is in $\compclass{NP}$.
\begin{answer}\verified
By \cref{lem:PSubsetNP} it suffices to show that the language 
decision problem is in~$\P$.
But that's clear:~given a triple $\sequence{a,b,c}$, checking whether
the first two sum to the third is clearly a polytime task.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
The \definend{\probname{Longest Path}} problem\index{Longest Path problem@\probname{Longest Path} problem}
is to input a graph and a bound, $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$, 
and determine whether the graph contains a simple path
of length at least~$B\in\N$.
(A path is simple if no two of its vertices are equal).
Show that this is in $\NP$. 
\begin{answer}\verified
We start by expressing this as a language decision problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
             \text{$\mathcal{G}$ has a simple path of length at least $B$}}
\end{equation*}

To show that this problem is in~$\NP$,
\cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv} wants a 
deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
The verifier input pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$. 
The verifier must satisfy that if
$\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a~$\omega$ such that~$\TM[V]$  
accepts the input pair, while if $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there is no 
such~$\omega$.
Also, the verifier must run in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

For a witness, take a path
$\omega=\sequence{v_0,\ldots{}v_{n-1}}$.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ and checks 
that the path is in~$\mathcal{G}$, is simple (that no two vertices are equal), 
and has length at least~$B$.
Those checks clearly can be done in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.

If $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\lang$ then there is such a
path~$\omega$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
that the  verifier accepts.
If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\lang$ then there is no such 
$\omega$, and so there does not exist a pair that the 
verifier accepts.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Recast each as a language decision problem 
and then show it is in~$\NP$.
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \definend{\probname{Linear Divisibilty}} problem\index{Linear Divisibility problem@\probname{Linear Divisibility} problem} 
inputs a pair of 
natural numbers $\sigma=\sequence{a,b}$ and asks
if there is an $x\in\N$ with $ax+1=b$.
\item
Given $n$ points scattered on a line, how far they are from each other
defines a multiset. 
(Recall that a multiset is like a set but element repeats don't collapse.) 
The reverse of this problem, starting with a multiset~$M$ of numbers and 
deciding whether there exist a set of points on a line whose 
pairwise distances defines $M$, is the 
\definend{\probname{Turnpike}} problem.\index{Turnpike problem@\probname{Turnpike} problem}
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item
Here is the recasting, as the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{a,b}\in\N^2\suchthat
             \text{there exists $x\in\N$ with $ax+1=b$}}
\end{equation*}

For
\cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}, we will produce a  
deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$.
It inputs pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma$ is the number pair $\sequence{a,b}$. 
The verifier must satisfy that if
$\sigma\in\lang_0$ then there is a witness~$\omega$ such that~$\TM[V]$  
accepts the input pair, while if $\sigma\notin\lang_0$ then there is no 
such~$\omega$.
The verifier must run in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

For a witness, take a number
$\omega=x$.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ and checks 
that $ax+1=b$.
That can be done in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.

If $\sigma$ is indeed an element of~$\lang_0$ then there is such a
number~$\omega=x$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
that the  verifier can accept.
If $\sigma$ is not an element of~$\lang_0$ then there is no such 
$\omega$, and so there does not exist a pair that the 
verifier accepts.
\item
This is the associated language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{M\suchthat 
              \text{there is a set~$P\subset\Z$ of numbers such that 
                    $M$ is the multiset of pairwise distances}}
\end{equation*}

Consider a deterministic Turing machine verifier, $\TM[V]$,
that inputs pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where 
$\sigma$ is a multiset $M$. 
For a witness, take a set of positions $\omega=P$.
The verifier gets an input pair~$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ and checks 
that the pairwise distances among elements of~$P$ equals~$M$.
That can be done in time polynomial in~$\lh{\sigma}$.

If $\sigma\in\lang_1$ then there is such a
set of positions~$\omega=P$, so there exists a pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ 
that the  verifier accepts.
If $\sigma\notin\lang_1$ then there is no such set
$\omega$, and so there does not exist a pair that the 
verifier accepts.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is the \HP{} in $\NP$?
\begin{answer}\verified
The class~$\NP$ is the set of languages decidable by a nondeterministic
machine in polytime.
Wvery problem decidable by a nondeterministic Turing machine is
decidable by a deterministic Turing machine.
The \HP{} is not decidable by a deterministic Turing machine, 
so it is not in $\NP$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is $\NP$ countable or uncountable?
\begin{answer}\verified
Countable.
A verifier is a deterministic Turing machine,
and there are countably many such macines.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that this problem is in $\NP$.
A company has two delivery trucks.
They work with a weighted graph that called the road map.
(Some vertex is distinguished as the start/finish.) 
Each morning the company gets a set of vertices, $V$.
They must decide if there are two cycles such that every vertex in~$V$
is on at least one of the two cycles, and both cycles have length at
most~$B\in\N$.
\begin{answer}
We use \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}.
This is a suitable language
(where the road map is $\mathcal{G}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$).
\begin{equation*}
  \lang=\set{N\subseteq\mathcal{N}\suchthat
       \text{$N$ is a set of vertices where there are two suitable cycles}}
\end{equation*}

To use \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}, 
we must produce a verifier and suitable witnesses.
The verifier inputs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$, where $\sigma=N$
(technically, $\sigma$ is a string representation of a
set~$N\subseteq\mathcal{N}$).
A witness is the two cycles, $\omega=\sequence{c_0,c_1}$.
The verifier checks that every vertex $v\in V$ is on at least one
of the two cycles, and that each of the cycles is of length at most~$B$.
Clearly that check can be done in time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$.

If $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is a pair of cycles, so there exists a 
suitable witness~$\omega$ such that the verifier accepts the pair
$\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
If $\sigma\notin\lang$ then there are not two such cycles.
So there is no such witness~$\omega$ that checks out, and so the
verifier will not accept for any such~$\omega$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Two graphs $\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1$ are isomorphic if there is a
one-to-one and onto function $\map{f}{\mathcal{N}_0}{\mathcal{N}_1}$ such
that $\set{v,\hat{v}}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_0$ if and only if
$\set{f(v),f(\hat{v})}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_1$.
Consider the problem of computing wheter two graphs are isomorphic.
\begin{exparts}
\item Define the appropriate language.
\item Show that the problem of determining membership in that language
  is a member of the class $\NP$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The natural language is
$\lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1}\suchthat
      \text{they are isomorphic}}$.
\item
We use \cref{lem:VerifierAndGuesserEquiv}, 
so we must produce a verifier and suitable witnesses.
The verifier inputs pairs $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$ where 
$\sigma$ is a pair $\sequence{\mathcal{G}_0,\mathcal{G}_1}$.
The witness is a function, a set of ordered pairs $\sequence{v,f(v)}$.
The verifier checks that the function satisfies the conditions, that it
is one-to-one and onto, and that 
$\set{v,\hat{v}}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_0$ if and only if
$\set{f(v),f(\hat{v})}$ is an edge of~$\mathcal{G}_1$
That check takes time polynomial in~$\sizeof{\sigma}$, 
because it depends only on the size of the two graphs.

If $\sigma\in\lang$ then there is such a function, so
there exists a hint~$\omega$ that will allow the verifier to accept
the pair $\sequence{\sigma,\omega}$.
If the two graphs are not isomorphic then no witness~$\omega$ will 
verify. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:NonDeterAndDeterTMsHaveSameLangs}
The proof of 
\cref{lem:NonDeterAndDeterTMsHaveSameLangs} leaves two things undone.
\begin{exparts}
\item (K\"{o}nig's Lemma) 
  Prove that if a connected tree has infinitely many vertices, but each
  vertex has finite degree, then the tree has an infinite path.
  \hint~fix a vertex~$v_0$ and for each of its neighbors, look at how many
  vertices can be reached without going through~$v_0$.
  One of the neighbors must have infinitely many such vertices;
  call it~$v_1$.
  Iterate.
\item Prove that if a nondeterministic Turing machine recognizes
  a language then there is a deterministic machine that also
  recognizes it. 
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}\verified
\begin{exparts}
\item % (Need for dependent choice is maybe more confusing than helpful.)
As in the hint, fix a vertex,~$v_0$.
Because the graph is connected, every vertex can be reached by a path that
starts with~$v_0$.
For each of its neighbors, $v$, consider the set of vertices~$R_v$
that can be reached by a path from~$v$ that does not go through~$v_0$.
At least one of the neighbors must have infinitely many such vertices,
or else the graph is a union of the finitely many sets~$R_v$ (and $v_0$), 
each of which is finite.
Pick one such neighbor and call it~$v_1$.
By repeating this process we get an infinite path $\sequence{v_0,v_1,\ldots{}}$.
\item
Suppose that a nondeterministic Turing
machine~$\TM$ recognizes a language~$\lang$.
We will describe how a deterministic machine~$\TM[Q]$ can do the same.
The nondeterministic machine~$\TM$ generates a computation tree
and~$\TM[Q]$ can do a breadth-first traversal of that tree.
Fix an input~$\sigma$.
If the tree triggered by~$\sigma$ has an accepting branch then~$\TM[Q]$ will
eventually reach it, and will accept that input.
If the tree has no accepting branch then~$\TM[Q]$ will never find one, 
and so will not accept~$\sigma$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}  \label{exer:DefnYieldsNondeterministicTM}
Following the definition of Turing machine,
on page~\pageref{text:DescriptionOfTMAction},
we gave a formal description of how these machines act.
We did the same for Finite State machines on
page~\pageref{page:DefnYieldsFiniteStateMachine},
and for nondeterministic Finite State machines on 
page~\pageref{page:DefnYieldsNondeterministicFSM}.
Give a formal description of the action of a nondeterministic 
Turing machine.
\begin{answer}
A 
configuration
of a nondeterministic Turing machine~$\TM$
is a set of four-tuples, $\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}$, where
$q\in Q$,
$s\in\Sigma$,
and where $\tau_L$ and~$\tau_R$ are strings of elements 
from the tape alphabet, $\tau_L,\tau_R\in\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
These signify the current state, the character under the read/write head, and
the tape contents to the left and right of the head.
We write $\configuration(t)$ for the machine's configuration after
the $t$-th transition, and say that this is the configuration
at step~$t$.
The 
initial configuration has the form
$\configuration(0)=\set{\sequence{q_0,s,\alpha,\emptystring}}$. 
We say that
$\alpha\concat \sequence{s}$ is the machine's input.

We next describe how the machine~$\TM$ transitions 
from being in configuration
$\configuration(t)$ to being in the next configuration, 
$\configuration(t+1)$.
Start with an empty set and then get $\configuration(t+1)$ by adding 
instructions to it as follows.
 
For each $\sequence{q,s,\tau_L,\tau_R}\in \configuration(t)$, 
compute the set~$\Delta(q,s)$.
That set may be empty, but if not then 
for each instruction $I=q_pT_pT_nq_n$ in that set,
there are three possibilities.
(1)~If $T_n\in\Sigma$ then write that character to the tape, so that
$\sequence{q_n,T_n,\tau_L,\tau_R}\in \configuration(t+1)$.
We say that $I\yields \sequence{q_n,T_n,\tau_L,\tau_R}$.
(2)~If $T_n=\str{L}$ then the machine moves the tape head to the left,
that is, 
$\sequence{q_n,\hat{s},\hat{\tau}_L,\hat{\tau}_R}\in \configuration(t+1)$,
where $\hat{s}$ is the rightmost character of the string~$\tau_L$ (if
$\tau_L=\emptystring$ then $\hat{s}$ is the blank character),
where $\hat{\tau}_L$ is $\tau_L$ with its rightmost character omitted
(if $\tau_L=\emptystring$ then $\hat{\tau}_L=\emptystring$ also),
and where $\hat{\tau}_R$ is the concatenation of $\sequence{s}$ and $\tau_R$.
We say that $I\yields \sequence{q_n,\hat{s},\hat{\tau}_L,\hat{\tau}_R}$.
(3)~If $T_n=\str{R}$ then the machine moves the tape head to the right.
This is like~(2) so we omit the details.

A computation tree branch
is a sequence of configurations
$
  I_0 \yields \configuration(1) \yields 
    \cdots \yields \configuration(h)  
$,
starting with the initial configuration $\configuration(0)$,
and such that there is no instruction~$I$ where $I_k\yields I$.          
If the instruction $I_k=\sequence{\hat{q},\hat{s},\beta,\gamma}$
is such that its state is accepting, $\hat{q}\in A$,
then we say the machine~$\TM$ accepts the input.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that the class of languages~$\NP$ is closed under
\begin{exparts*}
\item union
\item intersection
\item reversal,
\item concatenation,
\item Kleene star.
\end{exparts*}
No one knows whether $\NP$ is closed under complementation.
\end{exercise}
% \begin{answer}
% \begin{exparts}
% \item 
% \end{exparts}
% \end{answer}
\end{exercises}
\index{NP@$\NP$|)}





% ==============================================
\section{Polytime reduction}\index{polytime reduction|(}
% "Reduction decreases the number of problems without increasing the number of
% answers"
When we studied incomputability we found a sense in which we could think
of some problems as harder than others.
Consider, the
$\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$\index{Halts on Three problem@\probname{Halts on Three} problem}  
routine that, given~$x$,
decides whether Turing machine~$\TM$ halts on input~$3$.
We showed that with such a program we could solve the \HP.
We denoted this with
$K\leq_T\computablefunction{halts\_on\_three\_checker}$.
% The intuition is that solving the 
% \probname{Halts On Three}
% problem is at least as hard as solving the \HP.

Formally, we write $B\leq_T A$ when there is a computable function~$f$ such that
$x\in B$ if and only if $f(x)\in A$. 
We
say that~$B$ is Turing-reducible to~$A$,\index{Turing reducible}
because to solve~$B$, it suffices to solve~$A$.

In general, we say that problem~$B$ `reduces to' problem~$A$
if we can answer questions about~$B$ by accessing
information about~$A$.
For example, in Calculus, finding the maximum of a polynomial function
on a closed interval reduces to finding the zeroes of the derivative.
Another example is that, given a list of numbers, finding the median
reduces to the problem of sorting the list.
A reduction is a way to translate problems from one domain to another. 
The intuition is that the capability to do~$A$ 
gives the capability to do~$B$, and so $A$ is harder, or contains
more information, than~$B$.

The reduction $\leq_T$ translates via arbitrary computable functions.
But in this chapter we are focused on solving problems efficiently.
Consequently, the right idea of reduction
is that~$\lang_1\leq\lang_0$ if a method for solving~$\lang_0$ efficiently
gives us a method to solve~$\lang_1$ efficiently, 
that is, to translate by doing at most polynomially-much 
computation.

%<*def:PolyTimeReduction>
\begin{definition}
Let $\lang_0,\lang_1$ be languages, subsets of some $\kleenestar{\Sigma}$.
Then 
\definend{$\lang_1$ is polynomial time reducible to $\lang_0$},\index{polynomial time reducibility}\index{reducibility!polynomial time}\index{reducibility!polytime}
or \definend{Karp reducible}),\index{Karp reducible}\index{reducibility!Karp} 
or \definend{polynomial time mapping reducible},\index{reducibility!polytime mapping}
or
\definend{polynomial time many-one reducible},\index{reducibility!polytime many-one}
written 
\definend{$\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$},
if there is a computable 
\definend{reduction function}\index{reduction function}\index{function!reduction}
or \definend{transformation function}\index{transformation function|see{reduction function}}
$\map{f}{\kleenestar{\B}}{\kleenestar{\B}}$ that runs in polynomial time
and such that
$\sigma\in\lang_1$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$.
\end{definition}
%</def:PolyTimeReduction>

\begin{example}
Recall
the \probname{Shortest Path} problem\index{Shortest Path problem@\probname{Shortest Path} problem} 
that inputs a weighted graph, two vertices, and a bound,
and
decides if there is path between the vertices of length less than the bound.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},v_0,v_1,B}\suchthat
               \text{there is path between the vertices of length less than~$B$}}
\end{equation*}
Recall also 
the \probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}\index{Vertex-to-Vertex Path problem@\probname{Vertex-to-Vetex Path} problem} 
problem that inputs a graph and two vertices, and decides if there is a
path between the two.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{H},w_0,w_1}\suchthat
               \text{there is path between the vertices}}
\end{equation*}
Suppose that we have an algorithm to decide questions of 
membership in~$\lang_0$ that is fast.
Then here is a strategy to leverage that algorithm that will quickly 
decide whether $\sequence{\mathcal{H},w_0,w_1}\in\lang_1$:~make
a weighted graph~$\mathcal{G}$ by starting with~$\mathcal{H}$ 
and giving all its edges weight~$1$.
Then present the input 
$f(\mathcal{H})=\sequence{\mathcal{G},w_0,w_1,\sizeof{\mathcal{H}}}$ 
to the fast algorithm
(where $\sizeof{\mathcal{H}}$ is the number of vertices).
Clearly the step of translating $\mathcal{H}$ into~$\mathcal{G}$ is fast, 
and so the
result is a fast way to decide~$\lang_1$.
We write 
$\text{\probname{Vertex-to-Vertex Path}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Shortest Path}}$.
\end{example}

%<*lem:PolytimeReductionTrans>
\begin{lemma} \label{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}
Polytime reduction is reflexive: $\lang\leq_p\lang$ for all languages.
It is also transitive: $\lang_2\leq_p\lang_1$ and $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$
imply that $\lang_2\leq_p\lang_0$.
% There are pairs of languages that are incomparable, that are
% without a polytime reduction in either direction.
Every nontrivial language is \definend{$P$~hard},\index{P hard@$\P$ hard} 
that is, 
every language $\lang_0$ has the property that if $\lang_1\in\P$
then $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
Under polytime reduction, the class $\P$ is closed downward:~if 
$\prob_0\in\P$ and $\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ then $\prob_1\in\P$. 
So is~$\NP$.
\end{lemma}
%</lem:PolytimeReductionTrans>

%<*pf:PolytimeReductionTrans>
\begin{proof}
% The third sentence holds because there are uncountably many languages
% but only countably many reduction functions, as
% each such function is computed by a Turing machine.
For the third sentence, fix a $\lang_0$ that is nontrivial,
so there is a~$\sigma\in\lang_0$ and a $\tau\notin\lang_0$.
Let $\lang_1$ be an element of~$\P$.
We will specify a reduction function~$f_{\lang_1}$ giving $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
For any $\alpha\in\kleenestar{\B}$,
computing whether $\alpha\in\lang_1$ can be done in polytime.
If it is a member then set $f_{\lang_1}(\alpha)=\sigma$,
and if not then set $f_{\lang_1}(\alpha)=\tau$.

For downward closure of $\P$, suppose that
$\prob_1\leq_p\prob_0$ via the function~$f\!$, 
and also suppose that  
there is a polytime algorithm for determining membership in~$\lang_0$.
Determine membership in $\lang_1$ by:~given
input $\sigma$, find $f(\sigma)$ and apply the $\lang_0$-algorithm
to determine if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$.
Where the $\lang_0$ algorithm runs in time that is $\bigOh(n^i)$,
and where $f$ runs in time that is~$\bigOh(n^j)$,
then determining $\lang_1$ membership in this way 
runs in time that is~$\bigOh(n^{\max(i,j)})$.

The first two sentences, and 
downward closure of $\NP$, are in \cref{exer:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}.
\end{proof}
%</pf:PolytimeReductionTrans>

\begin{fig}[fig:ProblemBeanWithReductions]{The bean encloses all problems.
  % The ones with fast algorithms are at the bottom.
  Problems are shown connected if there is a polynomial time reduction from one 
  to the other (we only show a few problems for graphical clarity).
  Highlighted are connections within the complexity class~$\P$.}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp01.pdf}}
  \hspace{0.6em}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/pnp/pnp09.pdf}}
\end{fig}

\begin{example}  \label{ex:SubsetSumLeqKnapsack}
We will show that $\probname{Subset Sum}\leq_p\probname{Knapsack}$.
% To repeat, the significance is that if we had a polytime algorithm for 
% the \probname{Knapsack} problem then we could 
% use it, along with this reduction, to 
% get a polytime algorithm for the \probname{Subset Sum} problem.
%
Recall that the \probname{Subset Sum} problem 
starts with a multiset $S=\set{s_0,\ldots s_{k-1}}\subset\N$
(a set in which repeated numbers are allowed; basically a list of numbers)
and a target~$T\in\N^+$.
It asks if there is a subset whose elements add to the target.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{S,T}\suchthat
                \text{some subset of $S$ adds to $T$}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Knapsack} problem starts with a multiset
of objects~$K=\set{k_0, \ldots k_{n-1}}$, along with a bound $W\in\N$
and a target $V\in\N$.
There are also two functions, $\set{w,v}{K}{\N+}$, giving 
each~$k_i$ a weight~$w(k_i)$, and a value~$v(k_i)$.
The problem is to decide if there is subset $A\subseteq K$
such that the sum of the element weights is less than or equal to~$W$
while the sum of the element values is greater than or equal to~$V$.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{K,w,v,W,V}\suchthat
                \text{some $A\subseteq K$ has $\sum_{a\in A}w(a)\leq W$
               and $\sum_{a\in A}v(a)\geq V$}}
\end{equation*}
A reduction function~$f$ must input pairs
$\sequence{S,T}$, 
must output $5$-tuples $\sequence{K,w,v,W,V}$,
must run in polytime, 
and must be such that $\sequence{S,T}\in\lang_0$ holds if and
only if $\sequence{K,w,v,W,V}\in\lang_1$ holds.

As an illustration, suppose that we 
want to know if there is a subset of
$S=\set{18, 23, 31, 33, 72,  86, 94}$ that adds to $T=126$,
and we have access to an oracle that can quickly 
solve any \probname{Knapsack} problem.
We could let $K$ equal~$S$, let $w$ and~$v$ be such that
$w(18)=v(18)=18$, $w(23)=v(23)=23$, etc.,
and set the weight and value targets $W$ and $V$ to be~$T=126$. 
% as in the table below.
% \begin{center}
% \begin{tabular}{r|*{7}{c}}
%   \tabulartext{object} &$s_0$ &$s_1$ &$s_2$ &$s_3$ &$s_4$ &$s_5$ &$s_6$  \\
%   \cline{2-8}
%   \rule{0pt}{11pt}
%   \tabulartext{weight} &$18$  &$23$  &$31$  &$33$  &$72$  &$86$ &$94$  \\
%   \tabulartext{value}  &$18$  &$23$  &$31$  &$33$  &$72$  &$86$ &$94$  \\
% \end{tabular}
% \hspace{2em}
% $W=V=126$
% \end{center}

In general, given $\sequence{S,T}$, 
take $f(\sequence{S,T})=\sequence{S,w,v,T,T}$, 
where the functions are given by
$w(s_i)=v(s_i)=s_i$.
Then clearly $\sequence{S,T}\in\lang_0$ if and only if 
$f(\sequence{S,T})\in\lang_1$, and clearly $f$ is polytime.
\end{example}

The prior two examples show one kind of 
natural reduction, when one problem
is a special case of another, or at least closely related.

In addition, the prior example suggests that where the transformation
from one problem set to another is concerned, the details
can hide the ideas.
Often authors will suppress the details and instead 
outline the transformation.
We will do the same here.

\begin{example}
We will sketch an argument that the 
\probname{Graph Colorability}\index{Graph Colorability problem@\probname{Graph Colorability} problem}
problem reduces to the 
\probname{Satisfiability}\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem} 
problem,
that $\probname{Graph Colorability}\leq_p\probname{Satisfiability}$.

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATi>
Recall that a graph is $k$-colorable if we can partition the vertices
into $k$~many classes, called `colors' because that's how they
are pictured, so that there is no edge between two same-colored
vertices.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATi>
\begin{animatedfigure}{A $3$-coloring of \protect\citetext{a graph}{This is the Petersen graph, a rich source of counterexamples for conjectures in Graph Theory}.}\centering 
  \animategraphics[poster=last]{2}{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity}{00}{01}
\end{animatedfigure} 
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATii>
\noindent And, a propositional logic expression
is satisfiable if there is an
assignment of the values \true{} and \false{} to the variables 
that makes the statement
as a whole evaluate to~\true.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATii>

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiii>
We'll go through the $k=3$ construction; other $k$'s work the same way.
Write the set of $3$-colorable graphs as $\lang_1$ and
write the set of propositional logic statements as~$\lang_0$.
To show that $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$, we will produce a 
translation function~$f$.
It inputs a graph~$\mathcal{G}$
and a outputs a propositional logic expression $E=f(\mathcal{G})$,
such that the graph is $3$-colorable 
if and only if the expression is satisfiable.
The function will run in polynomial time.

Let~$\mathcal{G}$ have vertices $\set{v_0,\ldots\, v_{n-1}}$.
The statement will have $3n$-many Boolean variables:
$
  {a_0,\ldots a_{n-1}}
$, and
$  
  {b_0,\ldots b_{n-1}}
$, and
$  
  {c_0,\ldots c_{n-1}}
$.
The idea is that if the $i$-th vertex~$v_i$ gets the first color then 
the associated variable is $a_i=\true$,
while if it gets the second color then $b_i=\true$, and if it gets the
third color then $c_i=\true$.
Thus, for
each vertex~$v_i$, create a clause saying that it gets at least one color.
\begin{equation*}
  (a_i\vee b_i \vee c_i)
\end{equation*}
In addition, for each edge~$\set{v_i,v_j}$,
create three clauses that together ensure that the edge does not connect
two same-color vertices.
\begin{equation*}
  (\neg a_i\vee \neg a_j)
  \qquad
  (\neg b_i\vee \neg b_j)
  \qquad
  (\neg c_i\vee \neg c_j)
\end{equation*}
The desired expression is the conjunction of the clauses.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiii>

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiv>
This illustrates.
\begin{center}
  \vcenteredhbox{\includegraphics{complexity/asy/problems/problems23.pdf}}
  \hspace{1.25em}
  \begin{minipage}{3.25in}\small
    \begin{multline*}
      E=(a_0\vee b_0 \vee c_0)\wedge  
      (a_1\vee b_1 \vee c_1)\wedge  
      (a_2\vee b_2 \vee c_2)\wedge  
      (a_3\vee b_3 \vee c_3)             \\
      \begin{split}
        &\wedge (\neg a_0\vee\neg a_1)
               \wedge (\neg b_0\vee\neg b_1)
               \wedge (\neg c_0\vee\neg c_1)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_0\vee\neg a_3)
               \wedge (\neg b_0\vee\neg b_3)
               \wedge (\neg c_0\vee\neg c_3)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_1\vee\neg a_2)
               \wedge (\neg b_1\vee\neg b_2)
               \wedge (\neg c_1\vee\neg c_2)   \\
             &\wedge (\neg a_1\vee\neg a_3)
               \wedge (\neg b_1\vee\neg b_3)
               \wedge (\neg c_1\vee\neg c_3)  
      \end{split}
    \end{multline*}
  \end{minipage}
\end{center}
The graph has four vertices, so the expression starts with four clauses,
saying that for each vertex~$v_i$ at least one of the associated
variables $a_i$, $b_i$, or~$c_i$ is~$\true$.
The graph has four edges, $v_0v_1$, $v_0v_3$, $v_1v_2$, and
$v_2v_3$.
The expression continues with three clauses for each edge, 
together ensuring that the variables associated with the edge's vertices
do not both have the value~$\true$.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATiv>
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATv>
Thus, $E$ is satisfiable if and only if $\mathcal{G}$ has a $3$-coloring.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATv>
  
%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvi>
Completing the proof means
checking that the translation function, which inputs a bitstring
representation of~$\mathcal{G}$ and outputs a bitstring represention
of~$S$, is polynomial.
That's clear, although the argument is messy so we omit it.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvi>
\end{example}

%<*ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvii>
Echoing what we said above, 
the significance of the reduction is that we now know that
if we could solve the \probname{Satisfiability} problem 
in polynomial time then we could solve the
\probname{Graph Colorability} problem in polynomial time.
%</ex:ColorabilityLeqSATvii>

So in this sense, the \probname{Satisfiability} problem
is at least as hard as \probname{Graph Colorability}.
This section's final example gives a problem that
is at least as hard as \probname{Satisfiability}.

\begin{example}
%<*ex:SATLeqCliquei>
Recall that the \probname{Clique}\index{Clique problem@\probname{Clique} problem} 
problem is the decision problem for the language
$\lang=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat\text{$\mathcal{G}$ has a clique of at least $B$ vertices}}$, 
where a clique is a set of vertices that are all mutually connected.
We will sketch the argument that 
$\text{\probname{Satisfiability}}\leq_p\text{\probname{Clique}}$.\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}

The reduction~$f$ inputs a propositional logic expression~$E$ and outputs
a pair $f(E)=\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$.
It must run in polytime, and must be such that
$E\in\SAT$ if and only if $f(E)\in\lang$.

Consider this expression.
\begin{equation*}
  E=(x_0\vee x_1)\wedge(\neg x_0\vee\neg x_1)\wedge(x_0\vee \neg x_1)
\end{equation*}
It has three clauses, $x_0\vee x_1$, $\neg x_0\vee\neg x_1$, and
$x_0\vee \neg x_1$.
In a clause, an atom is either a variable~$x_i$ or its negation~$\neg x_i$.

For each occurrence of an atom in a clause, put a vertex in $\mathcal{G}$.
The expression~$E$ has three clauses with two atoms each
so the graph shown below has six vertices.
As to the edges in~$\mathcal{G}$, if the associated atoms 
are in different clauses
and are not negations (that is, not $x_i$ and $\neg x_i$)
then connect those vertices.
% \begin{align*}
%   \mathcal{N} &=\set{v_{0,0},v_{0,1},v_{1,0},v_{1,1},v_{2,0},v_{2,1}} \\
%   \mathcal{E} &=\{\,\set{v_{0,0},v_{2,0}},\,
%                   \set{v_{0,0},v_{1,1}},\,
%                   \set{v_{0,0},v_{2,1}},  \\
%               &\qquad \set{v_{0,1},v_{1,0}},\,
%                       \set{v_{0,1},v_{2,0}}, \\
%               &\qquad \set{v_{1,0},v_{2,0}},\,
%                       \set{v_{1,0},v_{2,1}}, \\
%               &\qquad \set{v_{1,1},v_{2,0}}\,\}
                  %   \end{align*}
% This is the graph arising from $E$.
%</ex:SATLeqCliquei>
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity03.pdf}
\end{center}
%<*ex:SATLeqCliqueii>
Observe that 
$S$ is satisfiable if and only if
the graph has a $3$-clique.
Showing that the translation function~$f$ is polytime is routine.
%</ex:SATLeqCliqueii>
\end{example}

Those examples give some sense of why 
the \probname{Satisfiability} problem
can be convenient, a benchmark problem for reducibility.
Often it is natural to describe the conditions in a problem
with logical statements.
In the next section we will give a theorem saying that 
\probname{Satisfiability}
is at least as hard as every problem in $\NP$.

We close with a comment on $\leq_p$.
The definition of $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_2$ is that 
$\sigma\in\lang_1$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_0$
for some polytime computable~$f$.
So~$f$ takes the input~$\sigma$ and does a computation, and at the 
end asks the $\lang_0$~oracle the single question of the membership
of $f(\sigma)$.    
Other reductions are possible, for instance one that can consult
$\lang_0$ any finite number of times, called 
\definend{Cook reducibility}.\index{Cook reducibility}\index{reducibility!Cook}

% .......................................
\begin{exercises}
\begin{exercise}
Your friend is confused.
``\Cref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}
says that every language in $\P$ is $\leq_p$ to every other language.
But there are uncountably many languages and only countably many
$f$'s because they each come from some Turing machine.
So I'm not seeing how there are enough reduction functions for a given 
language to be related to all others.'' 
Straighten them out.
\hint~the definition of reduction function is asymmetric.
Such a function must do the right thing for every input, 
but it need not be onto and so it may leave elements of the codomain
untouched, that is, free to vary.
\begin{answer}
Recall the proof that where $\lang_0$ is a nontrivial language, 
every $\lang_1\in\P$ has $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
Because $\lang_0$ is nontrivial, 
there is a~$\sigma\in\lang_0$ and a $\tau\notin\lang_0$.
For $\lang_1\in\P$,
specify a reduction function~$f_{\lang_1}$ as follows.
Given $\alpha\in\kleenestar{\B}$,
compute whether $\alpha\in\lang_1$.
If so then set $f_{\lang_1}(\alpha)=\sigma$.
If not then set $f_{\lang_1}(\alpha)=\tau$.

Now the hint comes into play.
This same reduction function works for any language $\lang$
where $\sigma\in\lang$ and $\tau\notin\lang$.
So this single reduction function works for uncountably many $\lang$'s.

That is, for a $\lang_0$, there are only countably many $\lang_1$ 
below it, that is, such that $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
However, for $\lang_1$ there may be uncountably many $\lang_0$ such that
$\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$,
because of the asymmetry in the definition of reduction function.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}


\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that if $\lang_0\notin\P$ and $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$
  then $\lang_1\notin\P$ also.
What about $\NP$? 
\begin{answer}
The same is true about $\NP$.
Both are immediate from \cref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Prove that $\lang\leq_p\setcomp{\lang}$
if and only if $\setcomp{\lang}\leq_p\lang$.
\begin{answer}
Assume $\lang\leq_p\setcomp{\lang}$.
Then there is a polytime~$f$ so that 
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\setcomp{\lang}$.
That's equivalent to 
$\sigma\notin\lang$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\notin\setcomp{\lang}$,
which holds when
$\sigma\in\setcomp{\lang}$ if and only if~$f(\sigma)\in\lang$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
\Cref{ex:SubsetSumLeqKnapsack} includes as 
illustration a \probname{Subset Sum} problem,
where $S=\set{18, 23, 31, 33, 72,  86, 94}$ 
and $T=126$.
Solve it.
\begin{answer}
There is indeed a subset that adds to~$T$,
namely $A=\set{23, 31, 72}$.
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} % 
Suppose that the language $A$ is polynomial time reducible to the language~$B$,
$A\leq_p B$. 
Which of these are true?
\begin{exparts}
\item A tractable way to decide $A$ can be used to tractably decide~$B$.
\item If $A$ is tractably decidable then~$B$ is tractably decidable also.
\item If $A$ is not tractably decidable then $B$ is not tractably decidable too.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item This is wrong.
For instance, if $A=\emptyset$ then $\emptyset\leq_p B$ for any language~$B$
but while there is a decider for $\emptyset$ that runs in time~$\bigOh(1)$, 
there may be no polynomial time decider for~$B$
(for instance, if $B=K$ then there is no decider at all).
The correct statement is the opposite:~a 
polytime decider for~$B$ can be used to decide 
the set~$A$ in polytime.
\item This is also wrong. 
As in the prior item, if $A$ is the empty set, which is decidable, 
and $B=K$, then $A\leq_pB$ but $B$ is not decidable in polytime.
The reason that $A\leq_pB$ is that $A$ can be decided in polytime
without any reference to~$B$ at all; in fact, deciding $A$ is $\bigOh(1)$.
Instead, the other way around is right:~if 
$B$ is polytime decidable then $A$ is polytime decidable also.
\item This is true.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Fix an alphabet~$\Sigma$.
The \definend{\probname{Substring} problem}\index{Substring problem@\probname{Substring} problem} 
inputs two strings and decides if the second is a substring of the first.
The \definend{\probname{Cyclic Shift} problem}\index{Cyclic Shift problem@\probname{Cyclic Shift} problem}
inputs two strings and decides if the second is a cyclic shift of the first.
(Where $\alpha=a_0a_1\ldots{}a_{n-1}$ and
$\beta=b_0b_1\ldots{}b_{n-1}$ are length~$n$ strings,
$\beta$ is a cyclic shift of $\alpha$ if there is an 
index $k\in\closed{0}{n-1}$ such that $a_i=b_{(k+i)\bmod n}$ for 
all~$i$.)
\begin{exparts}
\item Name three cyclic shifts of $\alpha=\str{0110010}$.
\item Decide whether $\beta=\str{101001101}$ is a cyclic shift of 
  $\alpha=\str{001101101}$.
\item State the \probname{Substring} problem as a language decision problem.
\item Also state the \probname{Cyclic Shift} problem as a
  language decision problem. 
\item Show that $\probname{Cyclic Shift}\leq_p\probname{Substring}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Three are: $\beta_2=\str{1001001}$, $\beta_1=\str{1100100}$,
  and $\beta_0=\str{0110010}$.
\item It is a cyclic shift, starting from index $k=6$.
\item It is the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\sigma,\tau}\in\Sigma^2\suchthat
                        \text{$\tau$ is a substring of $\sigma$}}
\end{equation*}
\item It is the decision problem for this.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\alpha,\beta}\in\Sigma^2\suchthat
               \text{$\beta$ is a cyclic shift of $\alpha$}}
\end{equation*}
\item The key is the observation that for same length strings, 
  $\beta$ is a cyclic shift of $\alpha$ if and only if 
  $\beta$ is a substring of $\alpha\concat\alpha$.
  So the reduction function~$f$ inputs $\sequence{\alpha,\beta}$
  and if the two are the same length then 
  it outputs $\sequence{\alpha\alpha,\beta}$ (else it outputs
  some pair sure to fail, such as $\sequence{\emptystring,\str{0}}$).
  Clearly~$f$ is computable in polytime,
  and $\sequence{\alpha,\beta}\in\lang_1$ if and only if 
  $f(\sequence{\alpha,\beta})\in\lang_0$.
  Therefore $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:IndSetLeqVertexCover}\label{exer:DefVertexCover}
The \probname{Independent Set} problem\index{Independent Set problem@\probname{Independent Set} problem} 
inputs a graph and a bound, and
decides if there is a set of vertices, of size at least equal to the bound,
that are not connected by any edge.
The \probname{Vertex Cover} problem\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
inputs a graph and a bound and decides if there is a vertex set, 
of size less than or equal to the bound, 
such that every edge contains at least one vertex in the set.
\begin{exparts}
\item State each as a language decision problem.
\item
Consider this graph.
Find a vertex cover with four elements.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity22.pdf}
\end{center}
\item In that graph
find an independent set with six elements.
\item Show that in a graph, $S$ is an independent set if and only if
  $\mathcal{N}-S$ is a vertex cover, where $\mathcal{N}$ is the
  set of vertices.
\item Conclude that $\probname{Vertex Cover}\leq_p\probname{Independent Set}$.
\item Also conclude that 
  $\probname{Independent Set}\leq_p \probname{Vertex Cover}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \probname{Independent Set} problem is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G,B}}\suchthat
               \text{there is $I\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{G})$ with 
                    $\sizeof{I}\leq B$
                     such that every edge contains a vertex from~$I$}}
\end{equation*}
\probname{Vertex Cover} is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{H},D}\suchthat
               \text{there is $C\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{H})$ 
                     with $\sizeof{C}\leq D$
                     such that every edge contains a vertex from~$C$}}
\end{equation*}
\item A vertex cover with $k=4$ elements is $S=\set{q_2,q_5,q_8,q_9}$.
\item An independent set with $\hat{k}=6$ elements is 
  $\hat{S}=\set{q_0,q_1,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7}$.
\item For any edge, if it has at least one endpoint in
$S$ then it has at most one endpoint in the complement $\hat{S}=\mathcal{N}-S$.
Conversely, If an edge has at most one endpoint in~$\hat{S}$ 
then it has at least one endpoint in the complement~$S=\mathcal{N}-\hat{S}$. 
\item We will do $\probname{Independent Set}\leq_p\probname{Vertex Cover}$; the 
other reduction is similar.
The definition requires that we produce a polytime computable
function~$f$.
Given an instance of \probname{Independent Set} 
$\sigma=\sequence{\mathcal{G},\hat{k}}$,
then we convert that to an instance of  \probname{Vertex Cover}
$f(\sigma)=\sequence{\mathcal{G},\lh{\mathcal{G}}-\hat{k}}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
Show that $\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}\leq_p\probname{Travelling Salesman}$.\index{Hamiltonian Circuit problem@\probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem}\index{Travelling Salesman problem@\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem}
\begin{exparts}
\item State each as a language decision problem.
\item Produce the reduction function.  
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
The \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} problem is the decision problem for this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\mathcal{H}\suchthat
                \text{the graph contains a circuit that includes each vertex exactly once}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Travelling Salesman} problem is this decision problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
          \text{some circuit of $\mathcal{G}$ that includes each vertex has total cost less than or equal to $B$}}
\end{equation*}
\item
The reduction function 
inputs an instance of \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit}, a graph
$\mathcal{H}=\sequence{\mathcal{N},\mathcal{E}}$
whose edges are unweighted.
It returns
the instance of \probname{Travelling Salesman} that uses the vertex set
$\mathcal{N}$ as cities and that takes the distances between the cities
to be: $d(v_i,v_j)=1$ if $v_iv_j$ is an edge of $\mathcal{G}$ and
$d(v_i,v_j)=2$ if $v_iv_j\notin\mathcal{E}$,
and such that the bound~$B$ is the number of vertices~$|\mathcal{N}|$.

The bound means that there will be a \probname{Travelling Salesman} solution
if and only if there is a \probname{Hamiltonian Circuit} solution, namely
the \probname{Travelling Salesman} solution uses the edges of 
the Hamiltonian circuit.

Clearly $\mathcal{H}\in\lang_0$ if and only if
$f(\mathcal{H})\in\lang_1$.
What remains is to show that $f$ runs in polytime. 
The number of
edges is less than twice the number of vertices, so that polytime in the
input graph size is the same as polytime in the number of vertices.
To output the \probname{Travelling Salesman} instance, the algorithm examines
all the pairs of vertices, which is a nested loop and so takes time that
is quadratic in the number of vertices.
So $f$ runs in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The \probname{Vertex Cover} problem\index{Vertex Cover problem@\probname{Vertex Cover} problem}
inputs a graph and a bound and decides if there is a vertex set, 
of size less than or equal to the bound, 
such that every edge contains at least one vertex in the set.
The \probname{Set Cover} problem\index{Set Cover problem@\probname{Set Cover} problem} 
inputs a set~$S$, a collection of subsets $S_0\subseteq S$, \ldots{}  
$S_{n}\subseteq S$, and a bound, and
decides if there is a subcollection of the $S_j$, with a number of sets at most
equal to the bound,
whose union is~$S$.
\begin{exparts}
\item State each as a language decision problem.
\item Find a vertex cover for this graph.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity27.pdf}
\end{center}
\item Make a set~$S$ consisting of all of that graph's edges, 
  and for each~$v$ make a subset $S_v$ of the edges incident on
  that vertex.
  Find a set cover.
\item Show that $\probname{Vertex Cover}\leq_p\probname{Set Cover}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item 
\probname{Vertex Cover} is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
               \text{there is $C\subseteq\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{G})$ 
                     with $\sizeof{C}\leq B$
                     such that every edge contains a vertex from~$C$}}
\end{equation*}
\probname{Set Cover} is the decision problem for 
this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{S,S_0,\ldots{}S_{k-1},D}\suchthat
               \text{each $S_j$ is a subset of~$S$, and $k\leq D$, and 
                     $S_0\cup\cdots{} \cup S_{k-1}=S$}}
\end{equation*}
\item One vertex cover is $C=\set{q_0,q_3,q_4,q_6,q_7,q_9}$.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity28.pdf}
\end{center}
\item The set of edges is $S=\set{a,b,\ldots{} n}$.
The subsets are these.
\begin{align*}
  &S_0=\set{a,b},\;
  S_1=\set{a,c},\;
  S_2=\set{b,d,e},\;
  S_3=\set{c,d,f,g},\;
  S_4=\set{e,h,i},       
  \\  
  &\qquad
  S_5=\set{g,h,j,k},\;
  S_6=\set{f,j,l},\;
  S_7=\set{i,k,m},\;
  S_8=\set{l,m,n},\;
  S_9=\set{n}
\end{align*}
The union of the subsets associated with vertices in the
vertex cover
\begin{equation*}
  S_{q_0}\cup S_{q_3}\cup S_{q_4}\cup S_{q_6}\cup S_{q_7}\cup S_{q_9}
  =\set{a,b}\cup\set{c,d,f,g}\cup\set{e,h,i}\cup\set{f,j,l}
     \cup\set{i,k,m}\cup\set{n}
\end{equation*}
equals~$S$.
\item We must produce a reduction function,~$f$.
It inputs an instance $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}$ of the
\probname{Vertex Cover} problem.
The computation is:~construct the set~$S$ of all edges, and 
for each vertex~$v$, construct $S_v\subseteq S$ consisting of the
edges incident on~$v$.
Then the output of the function is an instance of the \probname{Set Cover}
problem, 
$f(\sequence{\mathcal{G},B})=\sequence{S,S_{v_0},\ldots{} B}$.
Clearly we can compute this function in polytime, and clearly
also $\sequence{\mathcal{G},B}\in\lang_0$ if and only if
$\sequence{S,S_{v_0},\ldots{} B}\in\lang_1$. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise} \label{exer:DefMaxFlow}
In this network, each edge is labeled
with a capacity.
(Imagine railroad lines going from $q_0$ to~$q_6$.)
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity25.pdf}
\end{center}
The 
\definend{\probname{Max-Flow}} problem\index{Max-Flow problem@\probname{Max-Flow} problem} 
is to find the maximium amount that can flow from left to right.
That is, we will find a \definend{flow}~$F_{q_i,q_j}$\index{flow} 
for each edge, 
subject to the constraints that the flow through an edge must not exceed
its capacity and that the flow into a vertex must equal the flow out
(except for the source~$q_0$ and the sink~$q_6$).
The problem is to find the edge flows so that the source and sink see maximal
total flow. 
The \definend{\probname{Linear Programming} optimization} 
problem\index{Linear Programming optimization problem@\probname{Linear Programming} optimization problem}
starts with a list of linear equalities and inequalities, such as 
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n-1}x_{n-1}\leq b_i$ for $a_0,\ldots{}a_{n-1},b_i\in\Q$, 
and it looks for a sequence $\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_{n-1}}\in\Q^n$
that satisfies all of the constraints,
and such that a linear expression
$c_0x_0+\cdots+c_{n-1}x_{n-1}$ is maximal.
\begin{exparts}
\item Express each as a language decision problem, 
  remembering the technique of converting optimization problems using
  bounds.
\item By eye, find the maximum flow for the above network.
\item For each edge $v_iv_j$, define a variable $x_{i,j}$.
Describe the constraints on that variable imposed by the edge's capacity.
Also describe the constraints on the set of variables imposed 
by the limitation that for many vertices the flow in must equal
the flow out.
Finally, use the variables to give an expression to optimize 
in order to get maximum flow.  
\item Show that 
  $\probname{Max-Flow}\leq_p\probname{Linear Programming}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item \probname{Max-Flow} has this language.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},B}\suchthat
               \text{the flow~$F$ meets the constraints of $\mathcal{G}$ and the flow into the sink is at least~$B$}}
\end{equation*}
This is the language for the \probname{Linear Programming} optimization problem.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{\vec{x},C,Z,B}\suchthat
                \text{the point~$\vec{x}$ meets the constraints~$C$ and
                     makes~$Z(\vec{x})\geq B$}}
\end{equation*}
\item There are algorithms to solve these problems, but for small ones we
can do it by eye.
The maximum flow is~$5$, with each edge's numbers
given in parentheses.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity26.pdf}  
\end{center}
\item The variables are $x_{0,1}, \ldots{}x_{5,6}$.
These are the capacity constraints.
\begin{align*}
  &0\leq x_{0,1}\leq 3,\quad 
  0\leq x_{0,2}\leq 2,\quad 
  0\leq x_{0,3}\leq 1,\quad
  0\leq x_{1,3}\leq 2,\quad
  0\leq x_{1,4}\leq 2,\quad
  \\  &\qquad  
  0\leq x_{2,3}\leq 2,\quad
  0\leq x_{2,5}\leq 1,\quad  
  0\leq x_{3,4}\leq 4,\quad
  0\leq x_{3,5}\leq 2,\quad
  0\leq x_{3,6}\leq 2,\quad
  \\  &\qquad  
  0\leq x_{4,6}\leq 1,\quad\text{and}\quad
  0\leq x_{5,6}\leq 2
\end{align*}
These are the flow constraints.
\begin{align*}
  &x_{0,1}=x_{1,3}+x_{1,4},\quad
  x_{0,2}=x_{2,3}+x_{2,5},\quad
  x_{0,3}+x_{1,3}+x_{2,3}=x_{3,4}+x_{3,5}+x_{3,6},
  \\  &\qquad  
  x_{1,4}+x_{3,4}=x_{4,6},\quad\text{and}\quad
  x_{2,5}+x_{3,5}=x_{5,6}
\end{align*}
We want to maximize $Z=x_{3,6}+x_{4,6}+x_{5,6}$.
Observe that all of these equations and inequalities are
linear.
\item We must construct a reduction fuction~$f$.
The input is a triple $\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},B}$.
Use that information to make variables, construct capacity constraints and
flow constraints~$C$, and produce an optimization expression~$Z$.
In particular, the flow~$F$ (shown in parentheses in the network above) 
gives an associated point~$\vec{x}$ 
in the \probname{Linear Programming} instance.
Clearly that can be done in polytime.
Clearly also $\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},B}\in\lang_0$
if and only if $\sequence{\vec{x},C,Z,B}\in\lang_1$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
The 
\definend{\probname{Max-Flow} problem}\index{Max-Flow problem@\probname{Max-Flow} problem} 
inputs a directed graph where each edge is labelled with a capacity,
and the task is to find a 
the maximium amount that can flow from the source node to the sink node
(for more, see \cref{exer:DefMaxFlow}).
The \citetext{\definend{\probname{Drummer}} problem}{%
  This is often called the \probname{Marriage} problem,
  where the men name suitable women, but perhaps it is time for
  a new name.}\index{Drummer problem@\probname{Drummer} problem}\index{Marriage problem@\probname{Marriage} problem|see{\probname{Drummer} problem}}
starts with two same-sized sets, the rock bands,~$B$, and 
potential drummers,~$D$.
Each band $b\in B$ has a set $S_b\subseteq D$ of drummers
that they would agree to take on. 
The goal is to make the most number of matches.
\begin{exparts}
\item Consider four bands $B=\set{b_0,b_1,b_2,b_3}$ and
  drummers $D=\set{d_0,d_1,d_2,d_3}$.
  Band $b_0$ likes drummers $d_0$ and~$d_2$.
  Band $b_1$ likes only drummer~$d_1$, and 
  $b_2$ also likes only~$d_1$.
  Band~$b_3$ like the sound of both $d_2$ and~$d_3$.
  What is the largest number of matches?
\item Express each as a language decision problem.
\item Draw a graph with the bands on the left and the drummers on the right.
  Make an arrow from a band to a drummer if there is a connection.
  Now add a source and a sink node to make a flow diagram.
\item Show that 
  $\probname{Drummer}\leq_p\probname{Max-Flow}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The maximum number of matches is three, as in 
  $(b_0,d_0)$, $(b_1,d_1)$, and $(b_3,d_2)$.
\item The \probname{Max-Flow} problem has this language, where $W$ is a bound.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_0=\set{\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},W}\suchthat
               \text{the flow~$F$ meets the constraints of $\mathcal{G}$ and the flow into the sink is at least~$W$}}
\end{equation*}
The \probname{Drummer} problem has this, where $X$ is a bound.
\begin{equation*}
  \lang_1=\set{\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X}\suchthat
            \text{each $d_{i_j}\in B_j$ and $m\geq X$}}
\end{equation*}
\item Here is a network diagram (note the weights of~$1$ on each edge).
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity29.pdf}
\end{center}
\item A reduction function~$f$
is given a sequence 
$\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X}$.
It constructs a network that, from left to right, has a source node,
a bank of band nodes, a bank of drummer nodes, and a sink node.
Every edge is directed, and every edge has weight~$1$.
The pairs $(b,d)$ in the input give a flow~$F$.
So the output is the triple
$f(\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X})=\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},X}$.
Clearly we can compute this~$f$ in polytime.
And also clearly, 
$\sequence{B_0,\ldots{}B_k,(b_0,d_{i_0}),\ldots{}(b_m,d_{i_m}),X}\in\lang_1$ if and only if $\sequence{F,\mathcal{G},X}\in\lang_0$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise} \label{exer:Def3SAT}
In a propositional logic expression, a single variable is an \definend{atom},
and an atom or its negation is a \definend{literal}.
We shall say that a \definend{clause} is a disjunction of literals, 
so that $P_0\vee\neg P_1\vee\neg P_2$ is a $3$-literal clause.
Note that a clause evaluates to~\true{} if and only if at least
one of the literals evaluates to~\true.
A propositional logic expression is in \definend{Conjunctve Normal Form}
if consists of a conjunction of clauses.
The \definend{\probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem}
is to decide the satisfiability of propositional logic expression
where every clause consists of three literals (involving different variables).
One such expression is 
$(P_0\vee\neg P_1\vee\neg P_2)\wedge(P_1\vee P_2\vee\neg P_3)$.
The \probname{Independent Set} problem\index{Independent Set problem@\probname{Independent Set} problem} 
inputs a graph and a bound, and
decides if there is a set of vertices, of size at least equal to the bound,
that are not connected by any edge.
\begin{exparts}
\item In this graph
find an independent set.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity30.pdf}
\end{center}
\item State \probname{Independent Set} as a language decision problem.
\item Decide if $E=(P_0\vee\neg P_1\vee\neg P_2)\wedge(P_1\vee P_2\vee\neg P_3)$
  is satisfiable.
\item State \probname{$3$-Satisfiability} as a language decision problem.
\item With the expression~$E$, 
  make a triangle for each of the two clauses, where the vertices of the
  first are $v_0$, $\overline{v_1}$, and~$\overline{v_2}$, 
  while the vertices of the second are $w_1$, $w_2$, and $\overline{w_3}$.
  In addition to the edges forming the triangles, 
  also put one connecting $\overline{v_1}$ with~$w_1$, 
  and one connecting $\overline{v_2}$ with~$w_2$.
  % Argue that the expression is satisfiable if and only if the graph
  % has an independent set.
\item Sketch an argument that
  $\probname{$3$-Satisfiability}\leq_p\probname{Independent Set}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item The natural independent set is this.
\begin{center}
  \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity31.pdf}
\end{center}

\end{exparts}
\end{answer}

\end{exercise}

% 3-SAT < Integer LP
\recommended
\begin{exercise}
\begin{credit}
  SE user user326210, \url{https://math.stackexchange.com/a/2564255}
\end{credit}
The \definend{\probname{$3$-Satisfiability} problem}
is to decide the satisfiability of propositional logic expression
where every clause consists of three literals
(see \cref{exer:Def3SAT} for more).
The \definend{\probname{Linear Programming} language decision} 
problem\index{Linear Programming language decision problem@\probname{Linear Programming} language decision problem}
starts with a list of linear equalities and inequalities, such as 
$a_{i,0}x_0+\cdots+a_{i,n-1}x_{n-1}\leq b_i$ for $a_0,\ldots{}a_{n-1},b_i\in\Q$, 
and it looks for a sequence $\sequence{s_0,\ldots{} s_{n-1}}\in\Q^n$
that that is feasible, that satisfies all of the constraints.
The \definend{\probname{Integer Linear Programming} problem}\index{Integer Linear Programming problem@\probname{Integer Linear Programming} problem}
adds the requirement that all of the numbers be integers.
\begin{exparts}
\item Consider the propositional logic clause $P_0\vee \neg P_1\vee\neg P_2$.
Create variables $z_0$, $z_1$, and~$z_2$ and list linear constraints such
that each must be either~$0$ or~$1$.
Also give a linear inequality that holds if and only
if the clause is true.
\item
Show that $\probname{$3$-Satisfiability}\leq_p\probname{Integer Linear Programming}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
These constraints
\begin{equation*}
  0\leq z_0\leq 1, \quad
  0\leq z_1\leq 1, \quad
  0\leq z_2\leq 1
\end{equation*}
ensure, in an Integer Programming context, that each variable is 
either~$0$ or~$1$.  
As to the expression, use $z_0+(1-z_1)+(1-z_2)>0$.
\item
We must produce a reduction function~$f$.
It is given a propositional logic expression.
For each variable $P_i$ in that expression, create a variable $z_i$
for the Integer Linear Programming problem.
To force each~$z_i$ to be either $0$ or~$1$, include the constraint
$0\leq z_i\leq 1$.
For every clause in the propositional logic expression, add a 
constraint to the Integer Linear Programming instance as in the prior item.
\begin{equation*}
  P_i\vee \neg P_j\vee\neg P_k
  \qquad\text{is associated with}\qquad
  z_i+(1-z_j)+(1-z_k)>0
\end{equation*}
Clearly this function can be done in polytime.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}
We can do reductions between problems of types other than language decision problems.
Here are two optimization problems.
The \definend{\probname{Assignment} problem}\index{Assignment problem@\probname{Assignment} problem} inputs two same-sized sets, of workers 
$W=\set{w_0,\ldots{}w_{n-1}}$ and tasks
$T=\set{t_0,\ldots{} t_{n-1}}$. 
For each worker-task pair there is a cost $C(w_i,t_j)$.
The goal is to assign each of the tasks, one per worker,
at minimal total cost.
The \probname{Travelling Salesman} problem,\index{Travelling Salesman problem@\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem}
of course, inputs a graph whose edge weights give a cost for traversing that
edge, and asks for circuit of minimal total cost.
\begin{exparts}
\item By eye, solve this \probname{Assignment} problem instance.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{r}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{Cost $C(t_i,w_j)$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_0$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_1$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_2$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_3$}}  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \tabulartext{$t_0$} &$13$ &$4$  &$7$  &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_1$} &$1$  &$11$ &$5$  &$4$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_2$} &$6$  &$7$  &$2$  &$8$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_3$} &$1$  &$3$  &$5$  &$9$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\item  Consider this bipartite graph.
  \begin{center}
    \includegraphics{complexity/asy/complexity/complexity23.pdf}
  \end{center}
  Each $t_i$ is shown connected to each $w_j$.
  As edge weights, add the costs from the table.
  In addition, connect each pair of $w$'s with an edge of weight~$0$,
  and similarly connect each pair of $t$'s.
  Restate the \probname{Assignment} problem as that of finding a circuit
  in this graph. 
  Use this to show that 
  $\probname{Assignment}\leq_p\probname{Travelling Salesman}$.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item A little time playing with the table numbers
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{r}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$C(t_i,w_j)$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_0$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_1$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_2$}}
    &\multicolumn{1}{r}{\tabulartext{$w_3$}}  \\
  \cline{2-5}
  \tabulartext{$t_0$} &$13$ &$4$  &$7$  &$6$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_1$} &$1$  &$11$ &$5$  &$4$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_2$} &$6$  &$7$  &$2$  &$8$  \\
  \tabulartext{$t_3$} &$1$  &$3$  &$5$  &$9$  \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
shows that the optimum solution is to pair
$t_0,w_1$, along with $t_1,w_3$, and $t_2,w_2$, and finally $t_3,w_0$.
The total cost is~$11$.
\item Because all pairs of $w$'s are connected by edges of cost zero, 
as are all pairs of $t$'s, the cost of the circuit is just the cost of
passing between the top and bottom.
That happens twice in each direction, and making that cost be less than 
or equal to the bound is the \probname{Assignment} problem.

To show that $\probname{Assignment}\leq_p\probname{Travelling Salesman}$,
we construct a reduction function~$f$.
It inputs a triple $\sequence{W,T,C}$ of workers, tasks, and the costs.
It construcs the bipartite graph with a bank of $w$'s on one side, and
a bank of $t$'s on the other.
Every pair of $w$'s is connected by an edge of cost zero, as is
every pair of $t$'s.
Between each $t_i$ and~$w_j$ is an edge of cost $C(t_i,w_j)$.
The output of the reduction function is 
$f(\sequence{W,T,C})=\mathcal{G}$.
This is computable in polytime, and 
also an optimum solution for the \probname{Assignment} problem instance
corresponds to an optimum solution to the 
\probname{Travelling Salesman} problem instance. 
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\recommended
\begin{exercise}\label{exer:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed}
\Cref{lem:BasicFactsAboutPolytimeRed} leaves a couple of points undone.
\begin{exparts}
\item
Show that $\leq_p$ is reflexive and transitive.
\item
It says that nontrivial languages are $\P$~hard.
What about trivial ones?
Which languages reduce to the empty set?
To $\kleenestar{\B}$?
\item Show that $\NP$ is downward closed, that  
if $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_0$ and $\lang_0\in\NP$ then 
$\lang_1\in\NP$ also.
\end{exparts}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item
We must show that $\lang\leq_p\lang$ for any 
$\lang\in\powerset(\kleenestar(\Sigma))$.
Take the reduction function to be the identity, $f(\sigma)=\sigma$.
Clearly it can be computed in polytime, and just as clearly
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang$.

For transitivity, let $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$ via the function~$f$,
and let $\lang_1\leq_p\lang_2$ via the function~$g$,
which are polytime computable.
Then for all $\sigma\in\kleenestar{\B}$ we have 
$\sigma\in\lang_0$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$,
and $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$ if and only if $\composed{g}{f}(\sigma)\in\lang_2$.
Because $f,g$ are polytime, $\composed{g}{f}$ is polytime also.
\item
Suppose that $\lang\leq_p\emptyset$.
Then there is a function~$f$ such that
$\sigma\in\lang$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\emptyset$.
Since $f(\sigma)$ is never an element of the empty set, 
$\sigma$ is never an element of~$\lang$.
That is, $\lang$ is empty.

The argument that $\lang\leq_p\kleenestar{\B}$ implies that
$\lang=\kleenestar{\B}$ is just as easy.
\item 
  Assume that $\lang_0\leq_p\lang_1$, so that there is a polytime function 
  $\map{f}{\kleenestar{\B}}{\kleenestar{\B}}$ such that 
  $\sigma\in\lang_0$ if and only if $f(\sigma)\in\lang_1$.
  We will use this reduction to show that $\lang_1\in\NP$ implies that
  $\lang_0\in\NP$.

  If $\lang_1\in\NP$ then there is a nondeterministic Turing machine~$\TM_1$
  that decides~$\lang_1$.
  For the nondeterministic machine~$\TM_0$ that decided~$\lang_0$, 
  start with an input candidate string~$\tau$.
  Compute~$f(\tau)$.
  Run $\TM_1$ on input~$f(\tau)$. 
  If it accepts then $\TM_0$ accepts, and if it rejects then $\TM_0$ rejects.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
Is there a connection between subset and polytime reducibility?
Find languages $\lang_0,\lang_1\in\powerset(\kleenestar{\B})$ for each:
\begin{exparts*}
\item $\lang_0\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$,
\item $\lang_0\not\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$,
\item $\lang_0\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\not\leq_p \lang_1$,
\item $\lang_0\not\subset \lang_1$ and $\lang_0\not\leq_p \lang_1$.
\end{exparts*}
\begin{answer}
\begin{exparts}
\item Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of strings whose length is a
multiple of four, and $\lang_1$ to be the languge of strings whose length
is even.
Clearly both languages are in~$\P$, so $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$.
\item 
Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of even-length strings, 
and $\lang_1$ to be the languge of odd-length strings.
Clearly both languages are in~$\P$, so $\lang_0\leq_p \lang_1$.
\item Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of even-length strings and
$\lang_1=\kleenestar{\B}$.
Because some strings are elements of it and some are not, the set~$\lang_0$
does not reduce to~$\kleenestar{\B}$.
\item Take $\lang_0$ to be the language of even-length
strings and take $\lang_1=\emptyset$.
Because some strings are elements of it and some are not, the set~$\lang_0$
does not reduce to~$\kleenestar{\B}$.
\end{exparts}
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

\begin{exercise}
% The symbol for polynomial time reducibility is a less-than sign,
% which suggests a relationship between the two problems.
When  $\prob_i\leq_p\prob_j$, does that mean
that the best algorithm to decide $\prob_i$ takes time that is  
less than or equal to the amount
taken by the best algorithm for $\prob_j$?
Fix a language decision problem~$\prob_0$ 
whose fastest algorithm is $\bigOh(n^3)$,
an~$\prob_1$ whose best algorithm is $\bigOh(n^2)$,
a~$\prob_2$ whose best is $\bigOh(2^n)$,
and a~$\prob_3$ whose best is $\bigOh(\lg n)$.
In the array entry $i,j$ below, put `N' if $\prob_i\leq_p\prob_j$ is not
possible.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{r|*{4}{c}}
  \multicolumn{1}{r}{\ }
             &$\prob_0$  &$\prob_1$  &$\prob_2$  &$\prob_3$  \\
    \cline{2-5}
  $\prob_0$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(0,3)} \\  
  $\prob_1$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(1,3)} \\  
  $\prob_2$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(2,3)} \\  
  $\prob_3$  &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,0)}  &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,1)}
                &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,2)}   &\fillinblankLabelled{(3,3)} \\  
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{answer}
It is not possible for $\prob_0\leq_p\prob_2$ or for
$\prob_1\leq_p\prob_2$
\end{answer}
\end{exercise}

% \begin{exercise} % https://cs.stackexchange.com/q/92028
% If a problem~$\lang$ is in $\NP$ and $\lang\leq_p\hat{\lang}$
% then what can we say about the problem~$\hat{\lang}$?
% \begin{answer}
% Nothing.
% For instance, it could be that $\lang$ is in~$\P$ and so is $\hat{\lang}$.
% It could also be that $\hat{\lang}$ is in some higher-up class.
% There is not enough information to conclude anything. 
% \end{answer}
% \end{exercise}

\end{exercises}
\index{problem reduction|)}




% ====================================================
\section{$\compclass{NP}$ completeness}\index{NP complete@$\NP$ complete|(}
\margingraphic{0.17\textwidth}{complexity/pix/cook.jpg}{\label{fig:StephenCook}\href{https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Cook}{Stephen Cook b~1939}}\index{Cook, S!picture} % http://web.cs.toronto.edu/Assets/Computer+Science+Digital+Assets/cooknsercmed.jpg http://web.cs.toronto.edu/news/current/herzbergcook.htm
Because $\P\subseteq\NP$, the class $\NP$ contains lots of easy problems,
ones with a fast algorithm.
For instance, one member of $\NP$ is
the problem of determining whether a number is odd.
Nonetheless, the interest in the class is that it also contains lots of
problems that seem to be hard.
Can we prove that these problems are indeed hard?

In 1971 this question was raised by S~Cook.
He noted that with polynomial time reducibility we have a way to make 
precise that
an efficient solution to one problem results in an efficient
solution for the other.
And, he
showed that among the problems in $\NP$,
there are ones that are maximally hard.\footnote{%
  This was also shown by L~Levin, but he was behind the Iron Curtain so
  knowledge of his work did not have a chance to spread to 
  the rest of the world for some time.}

%<*th:CookLevin>
\begin{theorem}[Cook-Levin theorem]\index{Cook-Levin theorem}\index{Satisfiability@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}
The \probname{Satisfiability} problem\index{Satisfiability problem@\probname{Satisfiability} problem}
has the property any problem in $\NP$ reduces to it,
$\prob\leq_p\SAT$ for any $\prob\in\NP$.
\end{theorem}
%</th:CookLevin>

%<*pf:CookLevini>
First, $\SAT\in\NP$ because
a nondeterministic machine can guess which line of the truth
table to verify.
Said another way:~given a Boolean formula,
use as a witness~$\omega$ a sequence giving an assignment of truth values
that satisfies the formula.
%</pf:CookLevini>
